# Audit — 2026-09-12

Raised by a 44-finder sweep over the iOS app, the backend and the website. The
run died partway on an account session limit, so **30 finders returned and 14
did not**: `backend/notify.py`, `backend/valuation.py`, `backend/prompts.py`,
the observability and cache-resilience passes, and **every website finder**.
Those surfaces have no coverage in this document and were audited separately.

The run's own summary reported "0 confirmed, 193 refuted". That is an artifact,
not a result: the verification stage was killed by the same limit and a failed
verifier counts as a refusal. **Nothing here was refuted.** Every item marked
fixed below was verified by hand against the code first.

| | count |
|---|---|
| critical | 5 |
| high | 50 |
| medium | 85 |
| low | 53 |
| **total** | **193** |

No two findings shared a file and a title closely enough to merge, so this is
the raw set. Several *are* the same defect seen from different angles — two
finders reported the `isPro` dead end independently, in different words, which
is the strongest signal in the set — and those are left as they came in rather
than merged, because the second description is usually worth reading.

## Status

**94 of 193 entries are marked `[FIXED]`.** Every one was verified by
hand against the code before being changed, and every fix was mutation-tested:
the change reverted, the suite confirmed red, the change restored.

What that leaves is `medium` and `low`. **Every `high` and every `critical` entry is fixed.**

The last seven highs closed were four contrast or VoiceOver defects and three arithmetic ones, which is the pattern across this whole audit: the palette, the announcements and the rounding are where this app's defects cluster, because none of the three is visible to a compiler or to a passing glance.

### The backend sweep

Every unfixed `backend/*.py` entry — eighteen of them — was put through a
verifier and, on a STILL_LIVE verdict, two adversarial refuters. **Seven were
already fixed and simply unmarked**: the chain-purpose OIDs, the
`notification_type` log bound (both entries for it), the `/auth/entitlement`
rate limit, the public `/openapi.json`, the sorted-set member collision, and
the 429 `Retry-After`. Six of the eleven live ones are now
fixed — the spoofable `device_id`, the `summary` envelope, the 403 the client
throws away, the zero cap that broke every purchase, the eviction log that
reported the cap where the count belonged, and the refund that crossed
midnight onto the wrong day. Five are left:

| file | what is left |
|---|---|
| `main.py` | no request-level body cap before the multipart is spooled |
| `main.py` | `POST /apple/notifications` has no rate limiter |
| `main.py` | `no_price` hardcodes `operation="scan"`, so a bot test photo files as a user failure |
| `main.py` | an abandoned scan is billed but counted in no column |
| `quota.py` | DeviceCheck bit0 is never cleared, so "until the next reset" never comes |

That 7-of-18 ratio is the third independent measurement of the same thing: an
unmarked entry here is a claim to check, not a defect to accept.

Two things worth recording about the process, because they changed how the rest
of the list should be read:

**A finding's reasoning can be wrong while its conclusion is right.** The
`"order"` clamp entry named the wrong mechanism — it claimed `inverted` was
computed after the swap, and it is not; the branch works when called directly
and has a passing test. The conclusion held for a different reason:
`reconcile_prices` sorts the four points before the clamp ever sees them, so
the evidence is destroyed upstream and no real call site can reach the branch.
Fixing what the finding described would have changed nothing.

**And a finding's proposed fix can be worse than the defect.** The disabled
`PrimaryButton` entry proposed dimming the whole button. Measured, that gives
2.16:1 in light mode — still a failure — and drags dark mode from 10.50:1 down
to 3.03:1, because it dims a cream label toward a dark ground. The shipped fix
switches the ink instead: 8.07:1 light, 10.50:1 dark. There is now a test that
measures the proposal and asserts it fails, so it cannot be "fixed" that way
later.

The same held on a separate 19-finding verification pass over the companion
audit: **14 were already fixed** and 5 were live. So an entry here that is not
marked `[FIXED]` is a claim to check, not a defect to accept.

## Critical

### **[FIXED]** `settings.flashMode = .auto` set without checking `supportedFlashModes` — NSInvalidArgumentException abort on flashless devices

`ios/SnapWorth/Camera/CameraManager.swift:127`

`capturePhoto()` builds `AVCapturePhotoSettings()` and sets `flashMode = .auto` unconditionally (line 127) before calling `photoOutput.capturePhoto(with:delegate:)` (line 128). AVFoundation requires the mode to be present in `photoOutput.supportedFlashModes`; `capturePhoto(with:delegate:)` raises `NSInvalidArgumentException` — an uncatchable ObjC exception, i.e. process abort — otherwise. `supportedFlashModes` is `[.off]` whenever the active device has no flash. The app target is `TARGETED_DEVICE_FAMILY = 1` (project.pbxproj:814, :846) and `UIRequiredDeviceCapabilities` lists only `arm64` (Info.plist), so it installs on every iPad in iPhone compatibility mode, and no iPad has ever had a rear camera flash. This is the identical mechanism and the identical device population as finding I-8 in docs/AUDIT-2026-09-07.md:108-109 (hard-coded `maxPhotoDimensions`) — that fix landed 60 lines above in this same function (lines 68-85, with a comment explaining the exception) and stopped at the dimensions, leaving the flash line.

**What happens.** On any iPad running the app in compatibility mode, every shutter tap aborts the process: ScanView.swift:177 fires `Haptics.capture()`, `capturePhoto` reaches line 128 with `flashMode == .auto` while `supportedFlashModes == [.off]`, and the app is killed. 100% of captures on that hardware, and the same abort on iPhone whenever the system has disabled the flash (overheated device, very low battery), where `.auto` drops out of `supportedFlashModes`.

**Fix.** Read the supported set on the session queue the capture already runs on, and pick from it:
```swift
let settings = AVCapturePhotoSettings()
let modes = self.photoOutput.supportedFlashModes
settings.flashMode = modes.contains(.auto) ? .auto : (modes.first ?? .off)
```
Same shape as `preferredPhotoDimensions` — never hand AVFoundation a value the active configuration does not list.

### **[FIXED]** Free-trial copy is shown to users who are not eligible for the intro offer — "Start Free Trial" charges $39.99 immediately

`ios/SnapWorth/Services/StoreKitPurchaseService.swift:188`

`introOffer(for:)` reads `product.subscription?.introductoryOffer`, which is the offer *configured on the product* in App Store Connect. It is not a statement about the current customer. StoreKit exposes eligibility separately as `product.subscription?.isEligibleForIntroOffer` (per subscription *group*), and `isEligibleForIntroOffer` appears nowhere in the repository — I grepped every .swift file, including tests.

Everything downstream treats a non-nil offer as "this user gets it": PaywallView.swift:24 lifts the yearly offer, PaywallCopy.headline (PaywallView.swift:306-307) renders "Try SnapWorth\nfree for 3 days", PaywallCopy.offerPhrase (PaywallView.swift:344) puts "3-day free trial" on the yearly card, PaywallCopy.subheadline (PaywallView.swift:320-321) renders "Then $39.99/year", and PaywallCopy.ctaTitle (PaywallView.swift:354) renders the button as "Start Free Trial". The class doc-comment block at PaywallView.swift:290-303 states the rule as "the word 'free' requires `IntroOffer.isFree`" — the payment *mode* is checked rigorously, and eligibility is not checked at all. This is a different axis of the same bug the prior audits fixed, and none of the four audit docs mentions eligibility.

The offer is live: ios/SnapWorth/SnapWorth.storekit configures `com.snapworth.yearly` with `paymentMode: "free"`, `subscriptionPeriod: "P3D"`, `numberOfPeriods: 1`, in subscription group 20099901 alongside the monthly product. So this is on screen today, not hypothetically after a Connect change.

**What happens.** A user subscribes yearly, takes the 3-day free trial, then cancels (or lets the subscription lapse later). Weeks later they reopen the app, hit a Pro gate, and the paywall appears with yearly selected by default. Apple considers them ineligible — one introductory offer per subscription group — but the app still renders the headline "Try SnapWorth free for 3 days", the card detail "$0.77 per week · 3-day free trial", and the CTA "Start Free Trial". They tap it, and `product.purchase()` (StoreKitPurchaseService.swift:62) puts up Apple's sheet charging $39.99 today with no trial. The app promised free and Apple billed immediately. Same outcome for anyone who already consumed an intro offer anywhere in group 20099901.

**Fix.** Gate the offer on eligibility. `isEligibleForIntroOffer` is async, so thread it through the load rather than the sync builder: in `loadProducts()` (line 132-146), after a successful fetch, build `let eligible = await product.subscription?.isEligibleForIntroOffer ?? false` for each product and pass it into `buildPricing`/`introOffer`, returning `nil` from `introOffer` when `!eligible`. Then every copy path already degrades correctly — headline falls back to "Unlock\nSnapWorth Pro", the CTA to "Subscribe Yearly", the card detail to "$0.77 per week". Add a test that an ineligible product yields `introductoryOffer == nil`, mirroring the existing PaywallCopyTests.

### **[FIXED]** A refunded or revoked subscription keeps Pro for the rest of its paid term — the stored proof re-grants it forever and nothing can take it away

`backend/entitlements.py:671`

`_rederive` (lines 660-693) rebuilds Pro from the JWS held at `entproof:{subject}`. That JWS is a snapshot: it carries the revocation state it was signed with, and Apple never rewrites it. `_store_proof` (line 512) gives it a TTL of `expires_at + EXPIRY_GRACE_SECONDS - now` — the full remaining term, up to ~365 days for a yearly — and `_rederive` re-caches the derived Pro entry (line 686) on every miss without ever refreshing or invalidating the proof. So the loop is self-sustaining: the 24h `ent:` entry lapses, `current()` falls through, the pre-refund JWS re-verifies, `is_active` is True (expiresDate is untouched by a refund), Pro is re-cached for another 24h. Forever, with zero client involvement.

The three things that were supposed to prevent this all miss:
1. The comment at lines 176-178 claims "the access path turns a revoked transaction into FREE before it gets this far." That only holds if the client re-presents the revoked JWS. The shipped client never does: `StoreKitPurchaseService.swift:240` sets `activeJWS` only when `transaction.revocationDate == nil`, and `Transaction.currentEntitlements` omits revoked transactions entirely. So `record()` is never called with a revoked transaction, and the proof-deleting `else` branch at lines 477-483 — whose comment says exactly "a refunded subscription would be resurrected by `_rederive`" — never executes for a refund.
2. `clear()` (lines 695-699) does delete both keys, but it has no production caller: `grep -rn '\.clear(' backend/*.py` finds only metrics, comps, observability and notify. Only `tests/test_entitlements.py` calls it.
3. Apple does tell the server. `/apple/notifications` (main.py:1136) verifies a REFUND/REVOKE notification, `Notification.is_loss` is computed (appstorenotify.py:111), and the result goes to `notify.subscription_event` → a Telegram message and an index row. Nothing touches `ent:` or `entproof:`. appstorenotify.py's docstring makes this explicit ("This module never grants access") — but with the proof store in place, not revoking *is* granting.

The test that appears to cover this, `test_revocation_is_not_resurrected_by_the_proof` (tests/test_entitlements.py:312), passes a revoked JWS into `record()` by hand — a call the real client cannot make — so the suite is green over a path that does not exist in production.

Note the data model blocks the obvious fix: a notification has no subject, and `notify._index_subscription` (notify.py:2185) stores only `auditlog.pseudonymise(subject)[:6]`, which is not reversible. There is no originalTransactionId → subject mapping anywhere (`txn:{otid}` holds device ids, not subjects), so the notification handler cannot find the `entproof:` key to delete.

**What happens.** A user buys the $39.99 yearly on 1 Sep and requests a refund on 3 Sep; Apple grants it and POSTs a REFUND notification. The server verifies it, Telegrams "↩️ Refund", and stops. `entproof:{subject}` still holds the 1 Sep JWS with `expiresDate` = 1 Sep 2027 and no `revocationDate`, TTL ~365 days. The user deletes the app (or simply never reopens it), so nothing re-POSTs. Every `require_auth` → `current()` after the 24h Pro entry lapses hits `_rederive`, which re-verifies the stale JWS, sees `is_active` True, and re-caches Pro for another 24h. The refunded user keeps unlimited scans until 1 Sep 2027. The operator, having received the refund alert, has no reason to think otherwise, and the only remedy is deleting the Redis key by hand — which requires knowing the App Attest subject, which the refund notification does not carry.

**Fix.** Put the revocation on the access path, keyed by something a notification actually has. On REFUND/REVOKE (and EXPIRED with a revocation), write a tombstone `entrevoked:{originalTransactionId}` → revocationDate with TTL ≥ ENTITLEMENT_PROOF_TTL, and have `_rederive` (and `record`) consult it after `verify_signed_transaction` returns: if a tombstone exists for `ent.original_transaction_id`, return FREE and delete `_proof_key(subject)`. The check must live here rather than in the notification handler, because the JWS can never carry a revocation that post-dates it. Add the reverse index while you are there — `txn:{otid}` already exists at line 441; storing the subject set alongside the device bindings would let the handler call `clear()` directly and kill the entry immediately instead of at the next miss. Then replace `test_revocation_is_not_resurrected_by_the_proof` with one that drives the revocation through `parse_notification`, not through a `record()` call the client cannot make.

### **[FIXED]** `isPro` in the widget blob can never become true — no caller of `writeHaul` ever passes it

`ios/SnapWorth/Services/WidgetDataStore.swift:286`

`writeHaul(results:isPro:)` defaults `isPro` to nil and resolves it as `let pro = isPro ?? previous.isPro` (line 286). All three production call sites omit the argument: `ScanRepository.swift:50` (`writeHaul(results: [])`), `ScanRepository.swift:122` (the debounced `scheduleWidgetSync`, which is also what `refreshWidget()` funnels into), and `SnapWorthApp.swift:132` (`seedWidgetData`). So `pro` always resolves to `previous.isPro`, which starts at `WidgetHaulData.empty.isPro == false` (WidgetDataStore.swift:68) and has no path to true — `isPro` is a v2 key, so a 1.3.x blob cannot seed it either. Two of the six widgets are then permanently wrong: line 330-331 gates `monthProfit` on `pro && !monthProfits.isEmpty` and `monthFlips` on `pro`, so both are always nil/0 and `MonthProfitView` (MoreWidgets.swift:262, 272-277) renders the free-tier upsell branch; and line 326 writes `pro ? nil : FreeScanCounter.remaining`, so a subscriber gets a free-scan count written into their blob.

**What happens.** A Pro subscriber adds the new "Profit this month" widget: it reads "Pro" / "Track profit with Pro" forever, no matter how many flips they sell. The same subscriber's "Scans left" widget takes the non-Pro branch (MoreWidgets.swift:164) and shows "1" / "free scan left today" — and because `ScanViewModel.swift:90-95` only increments the counter and writes `serverRemaining` for non-subscribers, `FreeScanCounter.remaining` stays at `Config.freeScansAllowed` (1) indefinitely, so it never even counts down. A paying user is told they have one free scan left, and their profit widget is dead on arrival, with no recovery path short of an app update.

**Fix.** Thread entitlement through every writer: `WidgetDataStore.writeHaul(results: all, isPro: purchaseService.isSubscribed)` at ScanRepository.swift:50/122 and SnapWorthApp.swift:132 (inject the purchase service into `ScanRepository`, or read the cached entitlement the paywall already persists). Also re-write the blob on entitlement change — `StoreKitPurchaseService.refreshSubscriptionStatus` and the `Transaction.updates` handler — so a purchase or a lapse reaches the Home Screen without waiting for the next scan. Add a data-layer test asserting `readHaul().isPro == true` after a Pro write, and that `monthProfit` is non-nil for a Pro library with a sold flip this month.

### **[FIXED]** isPro is never written as true — no caller ever passes it, so every Pro widget is permanently locked

`ios/SnapWorth/Services/WidgetDataStore.swift:286`

`writeHaul(results:isPro:)` declares `isPro: Bool? = nil` (line 267) and resolves it as `let pro = isPro ?? previous.isPro` (line 286), where `previous` is the blob it just read back (line 285). That is a fixpoint: the value can only ever come from the previous copy of itself. The seed is `WidgetHaulData.empty`, which has `isPro: false` (SnapWorthWidgets.swift:66).

There are exactly three production call sites and not one of them passes the argument: `ScanRepository.swift:50` (`writeHaul(results: [])`), `ScanRepository.swift:122` (`writeHaul(results: all)`), `SnapWorthApp.swift:132` (`writeHaul(results: results)`). `WidgetDataStore` is also the only writer of the app-group suite in the whole app target (`grep UserDefaults(suiteName:` finds hits only in this file, lines 205/213/335/346), so no other code path backfills the flag. `isPro` in the stored blob is therefore `false` for every user, forever.

Downstream, the widget target branches on that field in three places and all three take the free-tier branch for a paying subscriber:
- `WidgetDataStore.swift:330` writes `monthProfit: pro && !monthProfits.isEmpty ? monthProfit : nil` → always nil, so `MonthProfitView.value` (MoreWidgets.swift:262) returns `"Pro"` and `caption` (MoreWidgets.swift:274) returns `"Track profit with Pro"`. The Profit-this-month widget upsells Pro to someone who already bought it, and the month's profit is never shown to anyone.
- `WidgetDataStore.swift:331` writes `monthFlips: pro ? monthProfits.count : 0` → always 0.
- `WidgetDataStore.swift:326` writes `freeScansRemaining: pro ? nil : FreeScanCounter.remaining` → always the free counter, so `ScansLeftView.headline` (MoreWidgets.swift:164) shows a subscriber the free-scan number instead of their streak.

**What happens.** A user subscribes to Pro, sells six items this month for $214 realized profit, and adds the "Profit this month" widget. It renders "Pro" / "Track profit with Pro" — the paywall pitch for a feature they are paying for — and never shows the $214. Their "Scans left" widget compounds it: for a subscriber `FreeScanCounter.used` never increments and `serverRemaining` is never written (both are gated on `!purchaseService.isSubscribed`, ScanViewModel.swift:92-96), so `FreeScanCounter.remaining` evaluates to `nil ?? max(0, Config.freeScansAllowed - 0)` = 1 (Config.swift:91) on every single write. The widget reads "1" / "free scan left today" permanently, no matter how many scans they run.

**Fix.** Get entitlement into the write. Either (a) add `isPro:` to all three call sites — `ScanRepository` has no `PurchaseService`, so persist the flag: have `StoreKitPurchaseService` write its entitlement to `UserDefaults.standard` on every `updateSubscriptionStatus`, and change line 286 to read that persisted flag as the default rather than `previous.isPro`; and (b) call `writeHaul(results:isPro:)` from the entitlement-refresh path so the widgets flip on purchase and on lapse instead of waiting for the next scan. Keep the `?? previous` fallback only for the case where the flag has genuinely never been established.

## High

### **[FIXED]** Setup-failure path returns without `commitConfiguration()`, leaving the session permanently mid-configuration and unrecoverable

`ios/SnapWorth/Camera/CameraManager.swift:61`

`setupSession()` opens a configuration block with `session.beginConfiguration()` at line 51 and commits it at line 91, but the input guard's `else` branch (lines 59-62) sets `error = .setupFailed` and returns at line 61 with the block still open. `beginConfiguration`/`commitConfiguration` must be balanced. Two compounding consequences: (1) line 93 is never reached, so `isConfigured` stays false and every subsequent `onAppear` → `requestPermissionAndSetup()` → `setupSessionIfNeeded()` (line 45) calls `setupSession()` again, opening another unbalanced configuration block; (2) the only recovery offered is the alert copy itself, "Camera setup failed. Please restart the app." (line 189) — which is literally the only way out. The `try?` at line 57 also swallows the `AVCaptureDeviceInput` error, so all three distinct failures collapse into one opaque message.

**What happens.** Any state where the guard at lines 55-58 fails: `AVCaptureDevice.default(.builtInWideAngleCamera, for: .video, position: .back)` returns nil (simulator, or a device whose back wide camera is unavailable), `AVCaptureDeviceInput(device:)` throws, or — the reachable production path — `canAddInput(input)` returns false because an earlier configuration pass already added a video input (see the `isConfigured` race below). The user gets a modal telling them to restart the app, and if they instead switch tabs and come back, each retry nests another open configuration block on a session that can no longer be reconfigured.

**Fix.** Make the block symmetric and commit on every exit: replace line 51 with
```swift
self.session.beginConfiguration()
defer { self.session.commitConfiguration() }
```
and drop the explicit commit at line 91. Separately, replace `try? AVCaptureDeviceInput(device:)` with a `do/catch` so the surfaced `CameraError` can distinguish "no camera" from "camera unavailable".

### **[FIXED]** ListingAPIClient actor reads a main-context SwiftData model off the main thread

`ios/SnapWorth/Services/ListingService.swift:157`

`ListingAPIClient` is declared `actor` (ListingService.swift:140) and `generate(for result: ScanResult, condition:, marketplace:)` takes a `ScanResult` — a `@Model final class` (ScanResult.swift:4) with no `Sendable` conformance anywhere in the repo (grep for Sendable in ios/SnapWorth/Models returns nothing). The only caller is `@MainActor ResultViewModel.generateListing` (ResultViewModel.swift:86), so the object crosses from the MainActor onto the actor's own serial executor, which is a cooperative-pool thread, never the main thread. The actor then reads the model's SwiftData-backed stored properties there: line 160 `result.priceRange(for: condition)` (reads `valueLow`, `valueHigh` and `conditionRaw` via `baselineCondition`), and lines 174-176 `result.itemName`, `result.brand`, `result.category` inside `liveGenerate`; `mockListing` (line 210) does the same. That object belongs to `sharedModelContainer.mainContext` (SnapWorthApp.swift:144, `.modelContainer` at :97). `ModelContext` and its managed objects are not thread-safe — every property access goes through the context's snapshot machinery — so reading them from the actor while the main thread is free to mutate the same object is an unsynchronised read/write on the same context. Swift 5 language mode (SWIFT_VERSION = 5.0 in project.pbxproj) downgrades the Sendable violation to a warning, which is why this compiled and shipped. This is the only place in the target where a `@Model` crosses an isolation boundary — `ThriftRunController.update(results:)` is `@MainActor`, and `WidgetDataStore.writeHaul(results:)` / `NotificationManager.digest(for:)` are nonisolated *synchronous* functions only ever called from the MainActor, so they run there.

**What happens.** Open a result sheet for a saved find and tap "Generate eBay listing" (ResultView.swift:1086). While the request is in flight, tap a different condition chip — `conditionChip`'s action writes `result.condition` and calls `result.refreshPortfolioValue()` on the MainActor (ResultView.swift:282-286) — or type in the "Paid" field, which writes `result.paidPrice` (ResultView.swift:216-217). The actor is concurrently reading `valueLow`/`valueHigh`/`conditionRaw` on a pool thread. Result is a Core Data multithreading violation: garbage or torn values in the generated listing's price range, and with `-com.apple.CoreData.ConcurrencyDebug 1` an immediate `__Multithreading_Violation_AllThatIsLeftToUsIsHonor__` trap; in the field it surfaces as intermittent crashes or a listing priced off a half-written value.

**Fix.** Never hand a `PersistentModel` to a non-MainActor actor. Snapshot on the caller's side: change the signature to take Sendable values — e.g. `generate(itemName: String, brand: String, category: String, condition: Condition, range: (low: Decimal, likely: Decimal, high: Decimal), marketplace: Marketplace)` — and build them in `ResultViewModel.generateListing` (ResultViewModel.swift:86-88) while still on the MainActor. Alternatively build the whole `ListingAPIRequest` struct there and pass that in. Apply the same change to `liveGenerate` (:169) and `mockListing` (:210) so no `ScanResult` remains in the actor's API.

### **[FIXED]** A cap-blocked reschedule leaves the old same-ID request pending, so the stale notification fires anyway

`ios/SnapWorth/Services/NotificationManager.swift:459`

`add()` only achieves idempotence through `center.add(request)` at line 473 ("same identifier replaces any pending"). But line 459 returns early when `resolveDailyCap` says another category owns the target day, and unlike the past-date guard at 454-457 it does NOT call `removePendingNotificationRequests(withIdentifiers: [id])`. The previously-scheduled request under the same fixed identifier survives and fires on its old date with its old body. This directly contradicts the contract `syncFreeScanReminder` documents for itself at lines 288-290: "Idempotent via the fixed identifier, so calling it after every scan and every foreground is the intended use: the previous request is simply replaced by the next correct one." It also breaks the one-per-day cap the function exists to enforce, because the leaked request still occupies its old day.

**What happens.** Free user has opted into the daily free-scan reminder at 18:00. Last night `freeScan.daily` was scheduled for today (Sep 12) 18:00 — nothing else occupied Sep 12, so the cap allowed it. An item listed on Aug 30 has `ledger.day.20260913` pending for Sep 13 10:00. At 10:00 today the user scans; `ScanViewModel.noteScanForStreakAndReminder` (ios/SnapWorth/ViewModels/ScanViewModel.swift:264) calls `syncFreeScanReminder(scannedToday: true)`, so `nextFreeScanDate` returns Sep 13 18:00. `resolveDailyCap(.freeScan, Sep 13)` finds the ledger request (priority 4 > freeScan 2) and returns false at line 499; `add()` returns at 459. The Sep 12 18:00 request is untouched and fires tonight: "Your free scan is back. What did you find today?" — the allowance was spent at 10:00 this morning. Tapping it deep-links straight to the camera (line 542) and the scan 402s into the paywall. The same path leaves a stale `portfolio.weekly` body pending, i.e. a notification stating a portfolio total that is no longer correct.

**Fix.** Clear the identifier before evaluating the cap, or on the blocked path: change line 459 to `guard await resolveDailyCap(...) else { center.removePendingNotificationRequests(withIdentifiers: [id]); return }`. Every fixed-ID category (recap, portfolio, trial, freeScan) relies on replacement rather than explicit cancellation, so the blocked path must cancel.

### **[FIXED]** The opt-in "daily" free-scan reminder is a one-shot trigger that only re-arms when the app is opened, so it fires exactly once for a lapsed user

`ios/SnapWorth/Services/NotificationManager.swift:469`

Line 469 builds `UNCalendarNotificationTrigger(dateMatching: comps, repeats: false)` with comps containing `.year/.month/.day`, so every request is a single delivery on one specific date. For recap/ledger/trial/portfolio that is correct (each is re-derived from state). For `.freeScan` it is not: the feature is a recurring daily nudge, and the only things that ever re-arm it are `syncFreeScanReminder` call sites that all require the app to be in the foreground — ios/SnapWorth/ViewModels/ScanViewModel.swift:264 (after a scan), ios/SnapWorth/Views/MainTabView.swift:95 (`scenePhase == .active`), ios/SnapWorth/Views/NotificationSettingsView.swift:120 (toggle/time change). `grep -rn "BGTaskScheduler|BGAppRefresh|UIBackgroundModes"` over ios/ returns nothing, so there is no background path either. The reminder whose entire purpose is to bring back a user who has stopped opening the app dies after the first delivery that user ignores.

**What happens.** User opts in on Monday in Settings → Notifications (`resyncFreeScan` schedules Monday 18:00, one-shot). Monday 18:00 it fires. The user does not open the app. Tuesday: no pending request exists and nothing re-arms it. Wednesday, Thursday, and every day after: silence, indefinitely, while the Settings screen still shows "Daily free scan" ON with a time picker and the footer promises "one reminder at the time you pick — only on days you haven't scanned yet".

**Fix.** For `.freeScan`, schedule a repeating trigger: `UNCalendarNotificationTrigger(dateMatching: DateComponents(hour: h, minute: m), repeats: true)`, and keep the foreground path to re-point/suppress it on a day already scanned. If a per-day body (the streak copy) must be preserved, enqueue a short ladder of dated one-shots (`freeScan.daily.<yyyymmdd>` for the next N days) refreshed on each foreground, and teach `cancel(.freeScan)`/`category(fromID:)` about the ladder.

### **[FIXED]** After the priming alert is declined, nothing in the app can ever request notification permission again, while Settings shows every category switched ON

`ios/SnapWorth/Services/NotificationManager.swift:126`

`center.requestAuthorization` appears at exactly one place in the whole project — line 126 in `enableFromPriming`, reachable only through `shouldPrimeAfterScan()` (lines 117-120), which is gated on `!primingShown`. `declinePriming()` (line 131) sets `primingShown = true` permanently. From then on the authorization status stays `.notDetermined`; `isAuthorized()` (lines 108-113) maps `.notDetermined` to false, so `add()` returns silently at line 458 for every category, forever. There is no `requestAuthorizationIfNeeded`-style entry point for the Settings screen, and `setEnabled` (line 91) only writes UserDefaults. Compounding it, ios/SnapWorth/Views/NotificationSettingsView.swift:88 raises the "Notifications are off — turn them on in iOS Settings" banner only when the status `== .denied`, which is never this user's status.

**What happens.** New user scans once, gets the priming alert, taps "Not now". Later they go Settings → Notifications: Weekly portfolio, Monthly recap, Ledger reminders and Trial reminders all render ON (`defaultEnabled` is true for all four, line 68), and they switch on "Daily free scan" and pick 18:00. No notification is ever delivered — not the trial heads-up before their first charge, not a single ledger follow-up — with no prompt, no banner, and four toggles asserting the opposite.

**Fix.** Add an authorization request that Settings can call: in `setEnabled(_:_:)` at line 91, when `on == true` and `authorizationStatus() == .notDetermined`, call `requestAuthorization` (then `syncEligible`); and treat `.notDetermined` like `.denied` in the NotificationSettingsView banner condition so a user who declined priming sees why the toggles do nothing.

### **[FIXED]** "Scans left" widget shows yesterday's spent count, so a free user is told to go Pro on a day their allowance has already reset

`ios/SnapWorth/Services/WidgetDataStore.swift:236`

`WidgetHaulData.scansLeft` (WidgetDataStore.swift:236-240, byte-identical copy at SnapWorthWidgets.swift:234-239) reads `freeScansRemaining` with no reference to when it was written. The count is stamped into the blob only by `writeHaul` (line 436, `freeScansRemaining: pro ? nil : FreeScanCounter.remaining`) alongside `updatedAt` (line 435) — and `updatedAt` is decoded (line 132) but never read by any widget or by `scansLeft`. The allowance itself resets on the *UTC* day (`FreeScanCounter.isServerToday`, ScanViewModel.swift:291-295; `remaining`, :346-348), but the extension cannot recompute it: the counter keys live in `UserDefaults.standard` (ScanViewModel.swift:299,307,325), not in the App Group, so the widget has only the blob. Every writer of that blob runs in the foreground app — `SnapWorthApp.swift:77` (launch `.task`), `:93` (entitlement change), `ScanRepository.swift:50,122` — and there is no BGTaskScheduler or background mode anywhere in ios/. `HaulOnlyProvider.getTimeline` (MoreWidgets.swift:26-30) therefore re-renders the same stale number hourly, forever.

**What happens.** `Config.freeScansAllowed = 1`. A free user scans once at 18:00 on Monday; the debounced write in `ScanRepository.scheduleWidgetSync` stores `freeScansRemaining: 0`. They do not reopen the app. At 00:00 UTC the allowance resets and `FreeScanCounter.remaining` is 1 again, but nothing rewrites the blob. All Tuesday the widget whose stated purpose is "How many free scans you have today" (MoreWidgets.swift:183) renders a large terracotta "0" (`isSpent`, MoreWidgets.swift:167-171) captioned "Back tomorrow, or go Pro" and speaks "No free scans left today" — for a user whose whole daily allowance is untouched. Because the widget is read *before* the app is opened, which is its only purpose, this is the state nearly every free widget user sees every morning: a false out-of-quota claim plus a Pro upsell for something they already have.

**Fix.** Make the count day-scoped in the shared model instead of trusting it indefinitely: give `scansLeft` an as-of date (`func scansLeft(asOf: Date) -> ScansLeft`, passed `entry.date` from the providers) and return `.unknown` — which already renders "—" / "Open SnapWorth" — when `updatedAt` is not the same UTC day as that date, using the same UTC calendar rule as `FreeScanCounter.isServerToday`. Add a second timeline entry at the next UTC midnight in `HaulOnlyProvider.getTimeline` so the flip happens at the reset rather than up to an hour late. Complementary (and the only way to show a real number without launching the app): move the `FreeScanCounter` keys into the `group.eu.snapworth.app` suite so the extension can compute `remaining` itself.

### **[FIXED]** "Restore purchase" on the paywall is silent when there is nothing to restore

`ios/SnapWorth/ViewModels/PaywallViewModel.swift:86`

`restore(service:)` sets `isRestoring`, calls `service.restorePurchases()`, and then does exactly one thing with the result: `if service.isSubscribed { isPurchaseComplete = true }`. There is no `else`. `AppStore.sync()` succeeding with no entitlement to restore is a *success* — nothing is thrown (StoreKitPurchaseService.swift:96-104 only throws on a real sync error) — so the catch block at line 87 never runs, `errorMessage` stays nil, `isPurchaseComplete` stays false, and the sheet does not dismiss.

The correct pattern already exists 60 lines away in the same codebase: SettingsViewModel.swift:15-17 sets `restoreMessage = service.isSubscribed ? "Your subscription has been restored." : "No active subscription found."` and raises an alert. The paywall's copy of the same flow dropped the negative branch. None of the four prior audit docs covers this (they cover the `.pending` dismissal bug and the pricing-failure state, both fixed).

**What happens.** An App Review tester — or any user who taps Restore speculatively — opens the paywall on a sandbox Apple ID with no prior purchase and taps "Restore purchase". `AppStore.sync()` completes, `refreshSubscriptionStatus()` finds no entitlement, `isSubscribed` stays false. The GhostButton's spinner runs for a second, stops, and absolutely nothing else changes: no message, no alert, no dismissal, no state change anywhere on screen. It is indistinguishable from a dead button, which is the standard Guideline 2.1 "feature does not function" rejection — and reviewers tap Restore on essentially every subscription app.

**Fix.** Give the negative branch a voice: `if service.isSubscribed { isPurchaseComplete = true } else { pendingMessage = "No active subscription found on this Apple ID." }` — `pendingMessage` renders in neutral grey at PaywallView.swift:105-112, which is the right tone since nothing failed (the red `errorMessage` at :145 would overstate it). Also clear `pendingMessage` alongside `errorMessage` at the top of `restore()` (line 82) so a stale Ask-to-Buy notice does not sit under the new one.

### **[FIXED]** Tag re-read updates the estimate but leaves vm.shareCard showing the old number

`ios/SnapWorth/Views/ResultView.swift:838`

vm.shareCard is rendered once in .task(id: result.id) (line 209) and re-rendered only on paidPriceText change (line 218) and on a condition-chip tap (line 293). rescan(withTag:) calls result.applySharpened(response) at line 838, which replaces itemName, brand, valueLow, valueHigh, confidence and valuationDetailData — i.e. exactly the fields ShareCardView renders — and nothing re-renders the card. The toolbar's "Result card" button hands vm.shareCard straight to ActivityShareSheet (lines 170-175). Worse, the sibling path renders live: renderGuessCards(result:photo:) (line 177) builds from the current result, so the two share options on the same dialog emit two different dollar figures for the same item.

**What happens.** Pro user scans a jacket, gets $40-$75, taps "Add the tag", the re-read identifies it as a Gore-Tex shell and the card now reads $120-$190 on screen. They tap Share > "Result card" and post a 1080x1920 image to Instagram Stories that says "$40-$75" and carries the old item name. Choosing "Guess-the-price story" instead posts $120-$190 for the same tap.

**Fix.** In the success branch of rescan(withTag:), after line 838 add vm.prepareShareCard(result: result, photo: photo) (or vm.scheduleShareCardUpdate(result: result, photo: photo), matching the condition chip at line 293).

### **[FIXED]** vm.generatedListing is not invalidated when the condition changes or the tag re-read lands

`ios/SnapWorth/Views/ResultView.swift:286`

ResultViewModel.selectMarketplace clears generatedListing on a marketplace switch with the explicit reason "so the user never sees eBay copy under a Vinted tab" (ResultViewModel.swift:70-75). The other two inputs to the same request get no such treatment. generateListing passes condition: result.condition and the range from result.priceRange(for:) (ResultViewModel.swift:86-88, ListingService.swift:157-171), yet conditionChip (ResultView.swift:280-293) mutates result.condition, and rescan replaces valueLow/valueHigh at line 838 — neither clears vm.generatedListing, so generatedListingView keeps rendering the previous listing's title, body copy and Ask/Floor (lines 1094-1108).

**What happens.** Pro user generates an eBay listing while the chip reads "Good": Ask $72, Floor $48, body "...in good used condition". They then tap "Used" — the hero range drops 22% to $35-$58 — but the Snap to Sell card still shows Ask $72 / Floor $48 and the "good used condition" wording, and "Copy" puts that price on the clipboard (ResultViewModel.swift:101-103). Same after a tag re-read: the listing still names the pre-re-read item at the pre-re-read price.

**Fix.** Set vm.generatedListing = nil (and vm.listingError = nil) in the conditionChip action alongside the refreshPortfolioValue call at line 286, and in the rescan success branch after line 838 — the same invalidation selectMarketplace already performs.

### **[FIXED]** The per-IP limiter on the three unauthenticated /auth routes is keyed on a client-chosen value

`backend/auth.py:257`

`_limit_unauthenticated` (auth.py:245-257) passes `request.client.host` to `deps.ip_limiter` instead of going through `main._client_ip`. `main._client_ip` (main.py:513-535) exists specifically because `request.client.host` is not trustworthy here: the container runs `uvicorn ... --proxy-headers --forwarded-allow-ips='*'` (backend/Dockerfile:57), and in the installed uvicorn 0.52.4 `_TrustedHosts.get_trusted_client_address` returns `x_forwarded_for_hosts[0]` — the LEFTMOST, entirely client-supplied hop — when `always_trust` is set. So `scope['client']` is whatever the caller put first in `X-Forwarded-For`. `/scan`, `/trends` and `/listing` all call `_client_ip(request)` (main.py:1330, 1917, 1959) and are keyed on the rightmost hop; the three routes added by the fix for AUDIT-2026-09-07 #150 are not. docs/RUNBOOK.md:537-542 states this exact fact ("uvicorn runs with `--forwarded-allow-ips='*'`, which makes `request.client.host` the client-supplied hop") as the reason `_client_ip` was rewritten and `TRUSTED_PROXY` deleted — the new helper in auth.py reintroduces the value that change removed. This is not the already-fixed AUDIT-v2 SEC-M4 / AUDIT-2026-09-07 B-14: `_client_ip` itself is still correct, it is simply not used here.

**What happens.** POST /auth/challenge 120 times with a rotating leftmost hop and a constant trusted proxy hop — `X-Forwarded-For: 10.0.0.<i>, 172.16.0.9` — and all 120 return 200 with zero 429s. The identical loop with a fixed `X-Forwarded-For: 10.0.0.77, 172.16.0.9` returns 61x 429 after the 60/h IP_RATE_MAX_REQUESTS bucket fills. The rightmost hop, which is what `_client_ip` would key on, is identical in both runs. Practical effect: /auth/challenge writes an unbounded number of 300s-TTL Redis keys per source, and /auth/attest runs an unthrottled x.509 chain walk plus nonce/counter verification per call — the two costs the limiter was added to bound.

**Fix.** Key the limiter on the same value the request path uses. `_client_ip` lives in `main` and `auth` cannot import it (circular), so inject the resolved IP rather than the helper: in `main._lifespan` (main.py:201) set `auth.deps.ip_limiter = lambda request: _enforce_ip_limit(_client_ip(request))`, change `AuthDeps.ip_limiter` (auth.py:119) to `Callable[[Request], Awaitable[None]] | None`, and make auth.py:254-257 `await deps.ip_limiter(request)`. Alternatively move `_client_ip` into `ratelimit.py`, which both modules already import.

### **[FIXED]** Unbounded x5c chain turns the unauthenticated /apple/notifications POST into a ~33x CPU amplifier

`backend/entitlements.py:224`

`_decode_x5c_chain` enforces a lower bound on the certificate chain (`if len(chain) < 2`, line 224) but no upper bound, and `_verify_chain` (lines 232-271) walks the *whole* caller-supplied list: it checks validity on every cert (237-239), runs `_require_ca` on every cert from index 1 up (252-253), and does one ECDSA verification per adjacent pair (255-268). The only thing that aborts early is the root pin at 243-245 — so an attacker who puts the *real* Apple Root CA G3 DER as the last element gets the full walk executed on ~80 certificates of their choosing. Note `appattest._verify_chain` (backend/appattest.py:91,99-102) only ever touches `certs[0]` and `certs[1]`, so the prior audit's judgement that "verification is milliseconds" (docs/AUDIT-2026-09-07.md:150) does not hold for this function. The reachable path is `main.py:1170 -> appstorenotify.parse_notification -> entitlements.verify_apple_jws` (entitlements.py:319-320), which is the first thing the handler does: no auth, and — unlike /auth/challenge, /auth/attest and /auth/assert, which were given `auth._limit_unauthenticated` (backend/auth.py:245-257, 263, 281) — no rate limiter of any kind, no `Depends`, and no global limiting middleware (main.py:417-480). The container runs `uvicorn --workers 1` (backend/Dockerfile:57), so this CPU comes out of the single event loop that also serves /scan.

**What happens.** Measured against the shipped code: a JWS whose `alg` header says ES256 and whose `x5c` is [80 attacker-generated SECP521R1 CA certs, chained to each other] + [the real Apple Root CA G3 DER] is 65,072 bytes — under the 65,536 `max_length` on `signedPayload` (main.py:1124-1125) and under `MAX_SIGNED_PAYLOAD`. `verify_apple_jws` spends 49.4 ms per call on it before raising "Signed transaction chain is not signed by Apple", versus 1.5 ms for an ordinary 4-cert rejection. One unauthenticated source sending ~20 req/s of these saturates the single worker's CPU and stalls every in-flight /scan; nothing returns 429 and nothing is metered except an unbounded stream of `rejected App Store notification` WARNING lines.

**Fix.** Bound the chain in `_decode_x5c_chain`: Apple sends exactly three certificates, so reject `len(chain) > 3` (or a small constant) alongside the existing `< 2` check, before any base64/DER parsing. Additionally reorder `_verify_chain` so the root pin at 243-245 runs before the per-cert validity loop at 237-239, and give the route the same per-IP limiter the other three unauthenticated routes already use (`auth._limit_unauthenticated` / `main._ip_limiter`) so an unsigned-body flood is throttled at all.

### **[FIXED]** `_verify_chain` never checks what the leaf certificate is authorised to do, so any Apple-issued EC leaf can sign transactions

`backend/entitlements.py:252`

`_require_ca` is applied to `certs[1:]` (lines 252-253) — every certificate except the leaf. The leaf's only proven properties are (a) it chains to the pinned Apple Root CA G3 through CA-flagged intermediates, and (b) `public_key()` is an `EllipticCurvePublicKey` (line 326). Nothing checks that it is a *transaction-signing* leaf. Apple's own verification (the `ChainVerifier` in app-store-server-library) requires the leaf to carry extension OID `1.2.840.113635.100.6.11.1` and the intermediate to carry `1.2.840.113635.100.6.2.1`; neither is checked here.

Apple Root CA G3 is not a StoreKit-only root. It anchors several CA branches that issue ECC P-256 leaves to ordinary enrolled developers from a CSR — the developer generates and keeps the private key. An Apple Pay payment-processing certificate is the clearest example: leaf ← Apple Application Integration CA ← Apple Root CA G3, P-256, key held by the requester.

The comment at lines 248-251 reasons that "Root pinning above bounds the damage" from a permissive chain walk. It bounds it to "anything Apple's CA hierarchy ever signed as an EC leaf," which for a purpose-agnostic root is not a bound. The same gap applies to the unauthenticated `/apple/notifications` endpoint, which shares `verify_apple_jws` (line 293) — main.py:1136 accepts any body that passes it, so a forged notification can also write rows into the operator's revenue index.

The asymmetry with the sibling module is telling: `appattest.py` pins its leaf by extension OID (`_NONCE_OID`, appattest.py:56, read at :180). This one does not. AUDIT-v2's SEC-M2 closed the *intermediate* basicConstraints/keyUsage gap (that is `_require_ca`); the leaf-purpose check is a different check and appears in none of the four prior audits.

**What happens.** An attacker enrols as an Apple developer, requests an Apple Pay payment-processing certificate (ECC P-256, chaining leaf → Apple Application Integration CA → Apple Root CA G3) and keeps the private key. They ES256-sign `{"bundleId":"eu.snapworth.app","productId":"com.snapworth.yearly","originalTransactionId":"1","environment":"Production","expiresDate":<now+10y>}` with `x5c` = [their leaf, the AAI CA, Apple Root CA G3]. Every check passes: validity dates (line 238), root pinning (line 243), `_require_ca` on the two non-leaf certs, the pairwise signature hops (line 267) — all genuine Apple signatures — and `jwt.decode` against their own leaf key. `/auth/entitlement` returns `tier: "pro"`, and `_store_proof` writes a 400-day proof that `_rederive` renews indefinitely. Free Pro, for anyone with $99 and an hour, with no jailbreak and nothing to share.

**Fix.** Pin the leaf's purpose. After `_verify_chain` returns, require the leaf to carry `x509.ObjectIdentifier("1.2.840.113635.100.6.11.1")` and the intermediate `certs[1]` to carry `x509.ObjectIdentifier("1.2.840.113635.100.6.2.1")`, via `cert.extensions.get_extension_for_oid(...)` with `ExtensionNotFound` → `EntitlementError`, exactly as `appattest.py:180` does for its nonce OID. These are the two OIDs Apple's own library checks. `tests/test_entitlements.py:48` (`_make_cert`) will need to add both extensions to the synthetic chain, plus a new negative test that a chain whose leaf lacks the OID is rejected — that test is the whole value of the change.

### **[FIXED]** Arming FREE_SCANS_FIRST_DAY hands the welcome allowance to the entire existing free user base, not just new users

`backend/quota.py:349`

Two lines combine. (1) `quota.py:302` writes `_seen_key` with `_COUNTER_TTL` (30h) using `cache.add`, and `add` never refreshes an existing key — `InMemoryCache.add` returns False without touching the expiry (cache.py:85-88) and `RedisCache.add` is `SET ... NX` (cache.py:124). So the seen marker expires 30 hours after the subject was FIRST minted, not 30 hours after last use: every subject, however active, falls back into the `first_time` branch of `starting_balance` roughly every 30 hours, forever. The `_welcome` docstring (quota.py:344-347) knows this recurs and claims the welcome marker bounds it to once per subject. (2) That claim fails because `_welcome` at lines 348-350 returns early when `first_day` is 0 and writes NO marker at all. A subject first seen while the welcome was off therefore carries no welcome key. The moment the lever is armed, the next recurrence of `first_time` reaches line 352, `add(welcome_key, _utc_day())` succeeds for an *existing* subject, and `_limit_for` (line 187) returns `first_day` for the rest of that UTC day. This is not specific to the ops-bot lever; raising `FREE_SCANS_FIRST_DAY` from 0 to 3 as a Railway variable does exactly the same thing, so it already happened on 2026-09-07 per docs/AUDIT-2026-09-07.md:4. AUDIT-2026-09-07 B-7 was the mirror-image case (a DeviceCheck-recognised reinstall claiming the welcome) and its fix wrote `_DENIED` only on the refusal path at quota.py:333-337, not on the welcome-is-off path.

**What happens.** Executed against the real modules: subject `existing` is first seen with the lever at 0 (`starting_balance` returns 1, welcome key is None) and spends its single daily scan. The operator then arms the lever to 3 and the 30h seen-key window lapses. On the same UTC day, `starting_balance("existing", None)` returns 3, the welcome key is written with today's date, `status()` reports limit=3 / remaining=2, and two further `reserve()` calls succeed. An existing user who had already exhausted the day's allowance is handed 2 extra paid Gemini scans. Repeats once for every subject in the install base within ~30h of arming. It also contaminates the `free_scan_limit_hit -> paywall_viewed -> purchase_started` funnel the lever exists to measure (quota.py:50-54), because the "first-day cohort" silently contains every pre-existing user.

**Fix.** In `_welcome`, write the marker on the off-path too, so "seen before the welcome existed" is recorded durably: replace lines 348-350 with a branch that does `await self._cache.add(self._welcome_key(subject), _DENIED, _WELCOME_TTL)` (swallowing CacheUnavailable, as the reinstall path at 333-337 does) before `return self._limit`. Genuinely new subjects arriving after the lever is armed still reach line 352 with no marker and are welcomed correctly. Additionally give `_seen_key` `_WELCOME_TTL` rather than `_COUNTER_TTL` at line 302 — "have I ever seen this subject" is not a 30-hour question, and the current TTL also re-runs the DeviceCheck query for every active user every 30 hours.

### **[FIXED]** PrimaryButton fills with the foreground terracotta and labels it cream — 3.18:1 light, 2.49:1 dark, 4.37:1 even under Increased Contrast

`ios/SnapWorth/DesignSystem/DesignSystem.swift:332`

Line 332 fills the capsule with `Color.snapTerracotta` and line 327 draws the label in `Color.snapOnAccent` (fixed cream #FBF7F2) at `.snapButton` = dmSans 17pt semibold. Measured: cream on light terracotta #D96C47 = 3.18:1; cream on dark terracotta #E8845F = 2.49:1; cream on the Increased-Contrast light value #BE5433 = 4.37:1. All three are under the 4.5:1 AA minimum for 17pt (≈12.75 WCAG pt, not large text), and the dark-mode value is under even the 3:1 large-text floor. The codebase already knows this is wrong: `SnapDarkHex.terracottaFill` = #A8482C exists precisely for this case (`ios/SnapWorth/Services/WidgetDataStore.swift:313`, cream = 5.43:1), and `SnapWorthTests.swift:1707` asserts `contrast(cream, terracotta) < 3.0` under the name `test_theForegroundTerracottaWouldFailAsAFill`. The widget extension and the website (`--terra-text`) both got the split token; the app's own primary button never did. The comment at lines 84-87 asserts the opposite — "the accent is dark enough in both themes" — and is numerically false in both.

**What happens.** Any primary CTA, in either theme, with or without Increased Contrast: tap the paywall's "Start free trial" (PaywallView.swift:156), "Reveal the estimate" (ResultView.swift:691), "Unlock why this price" (ResultView.swift:909), "Scan item" (ThriftFlipView.swift:97). In dark mode the cream label on #E8845F measures 2.49:1 — worse than the 3.0:1 the unit test already treats as disqualifying for this exact colour pairing.

**Fix.** Add a fill half to the pair and use it as the button background: `static let snapTerracottaFill = snapAdaptive(light: Color(hex: SnapDarkHex.terracottaFill), dark: Color(hex: SnapDarkHex.terracottaFill), lightHighContrast: Color(hex: SnapDarkHex.terracottaFillDeep), darkHighContrast: Color(hex: SnapDarkHex.terracottaFillDeep))` and change line 332 to `.background(Color.snapTerracottaFill.opacity(...))`. Keep `snapTerracotta` for foregrounds only. Then extend `test_creamClearsAAOnEveryFilledAccent` to cover the app's button fill so the two cannot drift again.

### **[FIXED]** PlanCard's savings badge is light-on-light in dark mode: snapEspresso on snapAmber = 1.46:1

`ios/SnapWorth/DesignSystem/DesignSystem.swift:684`

Line 681 sets the badge text to `Color.snapEspresso` and line 684 sets its background to `Color.snapAmber`. Both tokens are adaptive and both invert to a *light* value in dark mode: snapEspresso dark = #F0E9E2 (DesignSystem.swift:62 → SnapDarkHex.espresso) and snapAmber dark = #E5BE7C (line 56). Cream ink on light amber measures 1.46:1 at dmSans 10pt semibold (line 680). Light mode is fine (#2B211C on #EBB868 = 8.67:1), so this only appears when the device is in dark mode. This is the same class of bug the widget commit `6a21b87` fixed ("the palette drew light-mode accents on a dark tile") — here it is a dark-mode *ink* drawn on a token that stayed light. snapAmber is also the only accent with no `lightHighContrast`/`darkHighContrast` pair at all (lines 55-57), so Increased Contrast cannot rescue it either.

**What happens.** Open the paywall with the device in dark mode (PaywallView.swift:55-59, badge = "SAVE 40%" from `yearly.savingsPercent`, falling back to "BEST VALUE"). The badge renders as a bare amber capsule — the savings percentage, the reason the yearly plan is pre-selected, is unreadable at 1.46:1.

**Fix.** The badge is a filled accent, so its ink must not follow the theme: change line 681 to a fixed dark ink, e.g. `.foregroundStyle(Color(hex: "2B211C"))`, or introduce `Color.snapOnAmber` alongside the existing `snapOnAccent`/`snapOnCharcoal` fixed-ink tokens (lines 82-87). Also give snapAmber real high-contrast pairs while you are in the token block.

### **[FIXED]** A runtime fee-table override silently erases Poshmark's low-price flat fee, flipping a skip verdict into a buy

`ios/SnapWorth/Models/MarketplaceFees.swift:178`

`overrideTable` reconstructs each entry with only `MarketplaceFee(sellingFeePercent:fixedFee:)` (line 178), so `lowPriceFlatFee` falls back to its `nil` default. `fee(for:)` prefers the override over the shipped default (line 161), so an override entry for `.poshmark` deletes the `below: 15, fee: 2.95` rule declared at line 136 — and the override JSON shape decoded at line 170 (`[String: [String: Double]]`, keys `pct`/`fixed`) has no way to express the rule at all, unlike the website's mirror of the same table, which does carry `flatBelow`/`flatFee` (website/index.html:2483-2485). Same line, second defect: `Decimal(pct)` / `Decimal(fixed)` build money from `Double`, exactly what the table's own comment at lines 125-126 forbids ("Built from string literals, not Double... Decimal(0.1325) would capture the Double's rounding error").

**What happens.** The remote push this override exists for (header comment, lines 116-119) writes `{"poshmark":{"pct":0.20,"fixed":0}}` — a no-op refresh of the percentage. `fee(for: .poshmark).fees(on: 12)` then returns 12 × 0.20 = $2.40 instead of $2.95. On the repo's own pinned case (SnapWorthTests.swift:816-829 — $12 sale, paid $9, $0.10 shipping) net profit goes from −$0.05 to +$0.50, turning a skip into a buy. That is verbatim the wrong-verdict failure `test_verdictFlipsWithTheRightFee` and `test_poshmark_flatFeeUnderFifteen` (line 787) were written to prevent, and no test covers the override path.

**Fix.** Extend the override JSON with optional `flatBelow`/`flatFee` (matching the website table) and pass them into the constructed `MarketplaceFee`; when an override omits them, inherit `defaults[marketplace]?.lowPriceFlatFee` rather than dropping it. Decode the numbers as strings and build with `Decimal(string:)` so an override is as exact as the shipped defaults.

### **[FIXED]** `Condition.inferred` matches defect terms as bare substrings, so "stainless", "flawless", "unworn", "undamaged", "heavyweight" all grade `.used`

`ios/SnapWorth/Models/ScanResult.swift:422`

`asserts` finds the needle with `clause.range(of:)` — a plain substring search with no word boundary (line 389) — and the `.used` vocabulary at lines 422-423 contains `stain`, `flaw`, `worn`, `damage`, `heavy`, `fair`. Each one is a prefix or infix of a common, positive secondhand-listing word. The negation machinery cannot help: "un-" is not in `negators` (line 374) and the trap words are not negated at all. I reimplemented the exact algorithm (clauseBreaks → clauses → asserts → inferred) and ran it: "Stainless steel body, no dents or scratches" → .used; "Flawless — no visible wear anywhere" → .used (note `—` is not a clause break); "Unworn, tags still attached" → .used; "Undamaged; clean throughout" → .used; "Heavyweight cotton hoodie, no stains" → .used; "Fairly clean, minor lint" → .used. This path is live, not legacy-only: `baselineCondition` (line 175) falls through to `inferred` whenever `condition_grade` is absent, and backend/valuation.py:231 drops the grade via `_enum` any time the model answers outside {new, likeNew, good, used} — "like new" with a space, "excellent", "very good" all become null, which is why the repo's own fixture at ScanAPIClient.swift:327 carries `conditionGrade: "Very good"`. The code comment at lines 417-421 names this precise trap class ("rip" matches "striped", "wear" matches "menswear") and then ships six more instances of it.

**What happens.** A KitchenAid/Le Creuset scan whose notes read "Stainless steel body, no dents or scratches" and carries no usable `condition_grade` gets `baselineCondition == .used` (multiplier 0.78). The Condition card preselects "Used" on a clean item, and because `priceRange` divides by that baseline, the user correcting the chip to "Good" multiplies the estimate by 1/0.78 (+28%) and to "New" by 1.35/0.78 (+73%) — a correction that should not have moved the number at all, since the AI's 40–90 was already priced for a clean item. The inflated range then flows into the share card, the widget/portfolio total (`refreshPortfolioValue`, line 247) and the `/listing` request price (ListingService.swift:160).

**Fix.** Match on word boundaries instead of substrings. In `asserts`, replace `clause.range(of: needle)` with a bounded match — a regex of the form `\b(?:stains?|flaws?|damaged?|worn|torn|tears?|heavy|fair|poor)\b` over the clause, or require that the character before and after the hit is not a letter. Then add the negative cases to `ProductionHardeningTests` beside the existing ones (lines 2288-2319): stainless, flawless, unworn, undamaged, heavyweight, fairly must all be `.good`.

### **[FIXED]** The analytics opt-out never reaches the TelemetryDeck SDK — identifier-bearing session signals keep leaving the device after the user turns it off

`ios/SnapWorth/Services/Analytics.swift:288`

`backend?.setEnabled(newValue)` (Analytics.swift:288) is the only code path that mutates `config.analyticsDisabled` (TelemetryDeckAnalytics.swift:33-35). It runs only from the `Analytics.isEnabled` *setter*, and nothing in the shipping app ever assigns to `Analytics.shared.isEnabled` — grep across `ios/` finds exactly one reference, a read at TelemetryDeckAnalytics.swift:45. The only place the preference is flipped is `SettingsView.swift:13` — `@AppStorage(Analytics.enabledKey) private var analyticsEnabled = true`, bound to the "Share anonymous analytics" Toggle at SettingsView.swift:141 — and `@AppStorage` writes `UserDefaults.standard` directly. There is no `.onChange(of: analyticsEnabled)` anywhere in that file (verified through SettingsView.swift:153-200). So `TelemetryDeckAnalytics.setEnabled` is dead code and `config.analyticsDisabled` stays frozen at whatever it was when `AnalyticsBootstrap.start()` ran. `Analytics.track`'s guard (Analytics.swift:300) still works because it re-reads UserDefaults — so *our* events do stop, which is what makes this invisible. The SDK's own signals do not. This is the second half of AUDIT-2026-09-07 I-19, whose fix was specified as "build the config explicitly with analyticsDisabled = !Analytics.shared.isEnabled **and update it live on toggle**". The config half landed; the live-update half was plumbed but is never invoked, and no test covers it (`setEnabled` appears in SnapWorthTests only for `Haptics`).

**What happens.** User opens Settings and turns "Share anonymous analytics" off, then backgrounds the app for more than 5 minutes and reopens it. `TelemetryClient.swift:495-500` (willEnterForeground, 5*60 check) calls `TelemetryDeck.generateNewSession()`, which rotates `sessionID` (TelemetryClient.swift:74) and its `didSet` fires `TelemetryDeck.internalSignal("TelemetryDeck.Session.started")` at TelemetryClient.swift:81. `internalSignal` checks `configuration.analyticsDisabled` (TelemetryDeck.swift:176) — still `false` — so the signal is sent. It carries `clientUser: CryptoHashing.sha256(identifierForVendor.uuidString, salt: "")` (SignalManager.swift:178, 398-402) plus the whole default payload: `TelemetryDeck.Retention.totalSessionsCount`, `distinctDaysUsed`, `distinctDaysUsedLastMonth`, `averageSessionSeconds`, `Acquisition.firstSessionDate` (Signal.swift:116-130), device model, timezone, screen resolution and the accessibility flags. Same for `Acquisition.newInstallDetected` on a fresh install. The SDK is only actually silenced on the next cold launch, when `AnalyticsBootstrap.start()` re-reads the preference. Opting back in has the mirror bug: our events resume via the `track` guard while `analyticsDisabled` stays `true`, so the SDK's session/retention signals stay off until relaunch and the funnel's session counts under-report for that install.

**Fix.** Route the toggle through the facade instead of writing UserDefaults behind its back. Minimal change in SettingsView.swift, on the Toggle at :141: `.onChange(of: analyticsEnabled) { _, newValue in Analytics.shared.isEnabled = newValue }`. Cleaner: drop the `@AppStorage` at :13 and give the Toggle a computed `Binding(get: { Analytics.shared.isEnabled }, set: { Analytics.shared.isEnabled = $0 })` so the setter is the only writer of `snapworth_analytics_enabled`. Then add a regression test: configure `Analytics.shared` with a spy `AnalyticsService`, flip the preference the way the UI does, and assert the spy received `setEnabled(false)` — that assertion is what was missing when I-19 was signed off.

### **[FIXED]** /trends cache is not keyed on entitlement, so a Pro purchase is invisible for 30 minutes

`ios/SnapWorth/Services/ListingService.swift:349`

TrendsAPIClient holds one Trends value in a process-lifetime actor property (`private var cached: (value: Trends, at: Date)?`, line 349), serves it for 30 minutes (`ttl` line 347, hit at lines 354-356, store at line 368), and exposes no invalidation. But /trends returns a *different payload per tier*: `backend/main.py:1918` calls `notify.trends(is_pro=principal.is_pro)`, and the docstring at main.py:1911-1916 states free gets counts and direction only while Pro also gets `average_estimate` per category and `notable_finds`. The client renders the Pro extras purely off whatever is in that cached payload: `HistoryView.swift:569-570` gates on `isPro` and then on `!trends.notableFinds.isEmpty`, and `HistoryView.swift:626` / `:650` need `row.averageEstimate` to be non-nil. `fetch()` (line 351) is the only reader in the app, `HistoryView.swift:239-241` is the only call site and has no `.refreshable`, and nothing clears the cache — `StoreKitPurchaseService.setSubscribed` (lines 323-329) is the single entitlement transition point and does not touch it.

**What happens.** A free user opens My Finds. /trends returns the free shape (`averageEstimate == nil` on every row, `notableFinds == []`) and it is cached. They tap that same card's CTA — "See average prices and the week's best finds" (`HistoryView.swift:593`) — which fires `paywallViewed(trigger: .trends)` and opens the paywall, and they subscribe. `isPro` flips to true, so `HistoryView.swift:569` now takes the `if isPro` branch, which renders nothing because `trends.notableFinds` is empty in the cached free payload, and no row shows "avg $…" because `averageEstimate` is nil. For up to 30 minutes after paying, both features they were just sold render as blank space, with no user-reachable way to refetch — leaving the tab and returning re-runs `.task` but hits the cache, and only force-quitting the app clears it.

**Fix.** Key the cache on the tier it was fetched under and treat a mismatch as a miss: change line 349 to `private var cached: (value: Trends, at: Date, isPro: Bool)?`, give `fetch(isPro:)` the flag from `purchaseService.isSubscribed` at `HistoryView.swift:240`, and require `cached.isPro == isPro` in the hit check at line 354. Additionally add `func invalidate() { cached = nil }` and call it from `StoreKitPurchaseService.setSubscribed` (line 328) whenever the value changes, so a server-side entitlement change (restore, expiry, family sharing) also drops the stale payload.

### **[FIXED]** Dot-as-thousands token is never normalized — price comes back 1000x too small

`ios/SnapWorth/Services/PriceTagOCR.swift:126`

`normalizedDecimal` has a branch for both-separators (line 118) and for comma-only (line 126), but no branch for dot-only. A dot-only token therefore falls straight through to `Decimal(string: t)` at line 135, which treats the dot as a decimal point. That directly contradicts the regex at line 94, whose first alternative `\d{1,3}(?:[.,]\d{3})+` was written to match `.` as a *grouping* separator: the matcher says "thousands", the normalizer says "decimal". Verified against the regex: "€1.299" produces exactly one match with token "1.299". The mis-parsed value 1.299 is non-integral, so line 102 also flags it `strong`, guaranteeing it wins `best(of:)` over every honest candidate on the tag. Note `Decimal(string:)` parses a leading prefix rather than failing, so even "€1.500.000" survives as 1.5 instead of being rejected.

**What happens.** Photograph a EU-format tag reading "€1.299" (1299 euros). `detectPrice` returns Decimal 1.299. `ThriftFlipViewModel.readPriceTag` (ThriftFlipViewModel.swift:115-117) writes shelfPriceText = "1.30" and shows "Read $1.30 — tap to correct if it's off", so the flip verdict is computed against a $1.30 cost basis on a $1299 item and reports a massive profit / "buy". "€12.500" → 12.5. The same fires in a US store whenever Vision misreads the comma in "$1,299" as a period — a routine OCR confusion on a low-contrast tag — silently turning a $1,299 shelf price into $1.30.

**Fix.** Add the dot-only branch, mirroring the comma branch symmetrically: for a token with a single separator, exactly three trailing digits is grouping and one-or-two is a decimal; with multiple separators of the same character, all are grouping. Equivalently, key the normalization off which regex alternative matched (add captures for the grouping runs and the optional `[.,]\d{1,2}` tail) and strip every separator except that tail. This is the same rule `MoneyInput` already documents for commas (MarketplaceFees.swift:24-31) — three decimal places is not a retail price. Add regression cases: normalizedDecimal("1.299") == 1299, ("12.500") == 12500, ("12.99") == 12.99.

### **[FIXED]** detectPrice ranks lines by height only, so a large non-price number is returned as the shelf price

`ios/SnapWorth/Services/PriceTagOCR.swift:35`

`firstPrice` at line 74 returns a bare `Decimal?`, discarding the `strong` flag computed at line 102. So the cross-line ranking at line 35 (`priced.max(by: { $0.height < $1.height })`) is by glyph height alone, with no notion of whether the winning line contained anything price-like. This breaks the invariant the file states at lines 67-69 — "bare integers are often SKUs, sizes or quantities, so strong candidates are always preferred over them" — which holds *within* a line and is silently abandoned *across* lines, exactly where the tallest-text heuristic is weakest. The regex also has no exclusion for a `%` suffix or a `/unit` suffix, so percentages are first-class price candidates.

**What happens.** A clearance tag whose largest printed text is the discount: Vision returns observations ["70% OFF", "$12.99"] with the "70% OFF" line taller. Line 35 picks it, `firstPrice("70% OFF")` == 70 (confirmed against the regex: one match, token "70"), and detectPrice returns 70 while a strong, symbol-bearing $12.99 candidate sits in the same result set. Same failure for a Goodwill-style tag that prints the size largest ("SIZE 14" → 14) or a garment label ("100% COTTON" → 100). The user sees "Read $70.00" and the profit verdict is computed off it.

**Fix.** Carry the strength through: have the per-line helper return the `PriceMatch` (or a `(Decimal, Bool)`) instead of a `Decimal`, then rank lexicographically — strong before weak, tallest within each group — so a weak bare integer can only win when no strong candidate exists anywhere in the image. Additionally reject a numeric token immediately followed by `%` (and by `/` + a unit) in `matches`, since neither is ever a shelf price.

### **[FIXED]** A failed context.save() is never rolled back, so one bad row poisons every later write in the session

`ios/SnapWorth/Services/ScanRepository.swift:21`

All three mutators mutate the shared main context and then call `try context.save()`, and on failure they throw `AppError.persistence` while leaving the pending change in the context: `save` at lines 20-25 (`context.insert(result)` then `try context.save()`), `delete` at lines 31-36 (`context.delete(result)` then save), `deleteAll` at lines 43-48. There is no `context.rollback()` on any of those paths. The context here is `@Environment(\.modelContext)` — i.e. `sharedModelContainer.mainContext`, injected by `.modelContainer(sharedModelContainer)` in SnapWorthApp.swift:97 — which has `autosaveEnabled == true` by default (nothing in the app sets it; `grep autosaveEnabled` over ios/ returns nothing). Two things follow. (1) SwiftData saves the whole context, so an unsavable pending change makes every subsequent `context.save()` in the session fail too, from any call site. (2) Autosave keeps retrying the change the user was told had failed. Note the existing tests cannot see this: `ScanPersistenceFailureTests` and `MonthCountTests` (ios/SnapWorthTests/ProductionHardeningTests.swift:812, 876) build their repositories on `ModelContext(container)`, a secondary context whose autosave defaults to false.

**What happens.** Disk is full (or the `@Attribute(.externalStorage)` JPEG write fails) while saving a scan. `save` throws, ScanViewModel sets `saveFailed = true` and the sheet footer says the find was not added to My Finds — correct so far. The ScanResult stays registered as a pending insert. Now the user long-presses an old find and confirms Delete: `context.delete` succeeds locally, `context.save()` re-attempts the stuck insert and throws, so HistoryViewModel.delete (HistoryViewModel.swift:186) shows "Delete Failed" — but the row is already gone from the context, so `@Query` drops it from the grid immediately and it reappears on the next launch. Same for marking an item sold, and for Settings → Clear history. Conversely, once space frees up, autosave silently commits both the "failed" insert and the "failed" deletion.

**Fix.** Discard the failed change before throwing: add `context.rollback()` at the top of each of the three catch blocks (ScanRepository.swift:22, 34, 45) so the shared context is clean and the error the caller reports is the truth. Because rollback un-registers a never-persisted insert, give the result sheet a value copy to display rather than the managed object (ScanViewModel already holds `scanResult`; ResultView should not depend on the object still being in the context), otherwise the "a valid AI result must survive a persistence failure" guarantee in ProductionHardeningTests.swift:801 regresses. Alternatively do the insert+save in a child context so a failure cannot reach `mainContext` at all.

### **[FIXED]** `current`/`isRunning` count an ended-but-still-visible Activity as a live run, so no new run can start

`ios/SnapWorth/Services/ThriftRunController.swift:25`

`current` is `Activity<ThriftRunAttributes>.activities.first` with no `activityState` filter. `Activity.activities` contains activities that have ended but are still on screen, not only running ones — an activity ended with the default dismissal policy (which is what iOS uses when it ends an activity itself at its own maximum lifetime) stays in the collection until the system removes it, up to four hours later. The same applies to an activity the user swipes off the Lock Screen: it goes to `.ended`/`.dismissed` but is still enumerated. `isRunning` (line 28) is therefore true for a dead run, and both the `!isRunning` guard in `start()` (line 41) and `guard let activity = current` in `update()` (line 71) act on it.

**What happens.** User starts a run at 9:00am. iOS ends the Activity at 5:00pm at its 8-hour lifetime limit but leaves it on the Lock Screen until roughly 9:00pm. At 6:00pm the user walks into another shop and taps "Start a run": line 41 sees `isRunning == true` and returns false, so no new Activity is requested and the button does nothing — the Lock Screen keeps showing this morning's dead total. Every scan in the new shop then goes through `update()`, which fetches that same ended Activity at line 71 and calls `activity.update(...)` on it at line 93, which is a no-op, so the running total silently never moves. Identical outcome if the user swipes the Activity away from the Lock Screen mid-run — the exact case the class comment on lines 8-12 says it was designed to handle.

**Fix.** Filter on state: `Activity<ThriftRunAttributes>.activities.first { $0.activityState == .active || $0.activityState == .stale }`. `.stale` must be included — with `staleAfter` at 90 minutes (line 22) an ordinary run with no scan for an hour and a half is in `.stale` and is still live and updatable, so filtering to `.active` alone would report a real run as finished.

### **[FIXED]** DeviceIdentity mirrors the device id into UserDefaults forever, so a restored backup gives N phones one identity — defeating the subscription device cap

`ios/SnapWorth/Services/TokenStore.swift:138`

`DeviceIdentity.id` writes the id to `UserDefaults` at line 138 and never removes it, even when the Keychain write on line 139 succeeds (`_ = store.write(value)` discards the result, and nothing ever calls `defaults.removeObject`). After the first launch the id lives in BOTH stores permanently.

The two stores have opposite migration semantics, and the whole design rests on the Keychain's:
  - The Keychain item is `kSecAttrAccessibleAfterFirstUnlockThisDeviceOnly` (KeychainDeviceIdentityStore.write, line 165) — excluded from encrypted backups and from Quick Start / iCloud device-to-device migration.
  - `UserDefaults` is `<container>/Library/Preferences/eu.snapworth.app.plist` — included in both. This repo already establishes that mechanism: docs/AUDIT-2026-09-07.md:52 notes the attest key id in UserDefaults is "restored by iCloud/Quick Start" while the ThisDeviceOnly Keychain token is not.

So the doc comment at TokenStore.swift:101-103 — "`ThisDeviceOnly` means the value is never carried to another device by a backup, so two phones restored from one backup are correctly two devices" — is false as written. The UserDefaults copy on line 138 is exactly the carrier it claims does not exist, and the legacy-adoption branch on line 134 is what re-adopts it on the new device.

The id is the key the server's anti-sharing control uses: AttestationService.swift:73 sends it as `device_id`, and backend/entitlements.py:575 (`identity = device_id or subject`) / :589 bounds `MAX_DEVICES_PER_SUBSCRIPTION` (6) on distinct ids, with `notify.subscription_over_cap` (:595) as the sharing alert. entitlements.py:556-558 states the binding's correctness premise outright: "a Keychain-backed value that outlives app deletion".

**What happens.** Take one encrypted iTunes/Finder backup of a phone with a Pro subscription and restore it onto any number of other iPhones (or hand the same backup around). On each restored phone the Keychain read at line 129 returns nil (ThisDeviceOnly did not migrate), line 134 finds the migrated UserDefaults value, and every phone sends the same `device_id`. All of them collapse onto ONE binding slot in entitlements.py:589, so the 6-device cap is bypassed without bound and `subscription_over_cap` never fires — the operator sees zero churn on a subscription being shared across arbitrarily many phones. Same outcome via Quick Start when the source phone keeps the app installed: two phones, one device identity.

**Fix.** Only keep the UserDefaults copy while it is the sole copy. In `DeviceIdentity.id`, write to defaults first (so a failed Keychain write still has the documented fallback), then drop it once the durable write succeeds:

```swift
let value = defaults.string(forKey: Self.legacyDefaultsKey) ?? UUID().uuidString
defaults.set(value, forKey: Self.legacyDefaultsKey)
if store.write(value) {
    // The Keychain now holds it, and it is ThisDeviceOnly. Leaving a copy in
    // UserDefaults would migrate the identity to a restored device, which is
    // precisely what this store exists to prevent.
    defaults.removeObject(forKey: Self.legacyDefaultsKey)
}
cached = value
```
