The marketplace picker is never disabled during generation — only the Generate button is (ResultView.swift:1088), while marketplacePicker at ResultView.swift:998 stays live — so selectMarketplace can run mid-flight, set selectedMarketplace = .vinted and clear generatedListing, only for the eBay response to land and reinstate itself. The backend tailors both voice and price per marketplace (backend/main.py:710-736, 763), so this is not just wrong copy.

**What happens.** User taps "Generate eBay listing", waits ~3s, taps the Vinted chip. The eBay response arrives and is installed under the selected Vinted chip: eBay-voiced description and eBay-derived Ask/Floor, with the Open button reading "Open eBay". The analytics event also mis-attributes the generation to vinted because line 94 re-reads selectedMarketplace after the await.

**Fix.** Capture the request identity before the await (`let requested = selectedMarketplace`), and after it `guard requested == selectedMarketplace else { return }` before assigning generatedListing; track .listingGenerated with `requested`, not the live property. Apply the same guard to result.condition, which can also change mid-flight via the condition chip.

### A redacted (unloaded) plan card is still tappable, stranding the user on a plan with no price and a disabled CTA

`ios/SnapWorth/Views/PaywallView.swift:82`

Lines 70 and 82 apply `.redacted(reason: .placeholder)` to a `PlanCard` whose product StoreKit did not return. `.redacted` only changes rendering — it does not disable hit-testing, and `PlanCard` is a `Button` with `.buttonStyle(.plain)` (DesignSystem.swift:649, :700) whose action assigns `vm.selectedProductID` unconditionally (PaywallView.swift:80). Neither card gets `.disabled(...)`.

Selecting an unloaded product drives three derived values wrong at once: `isPurchasable` (line 266-268) returns false so the CTA is disabled, `pricing(_:)` (line 270-272) falls back to `PlanPricing.loading` whose `displayPrice` is "—", and `PaywallCopy.subheadline` (line 316) therefore returns "Loading plans…" when nothing is loading. `reconcileSelection` only runs from `.task` (line 235) and after a manual retry (line 134), so it cannot undo a tap.

**What happens.** Partial product fetch — the monthly product is missing (Missing Metadata in App Store Connect, or a truncated StoreKit response). `loadProducts` keeps what arrived and sets `pricingFailed = true` (StoreKitPurchaseService.swift:145); `reconcileSelection` leaves yearly selected; the monthly card renders as grey placeholder bars. The user taps the grey monthly card to see what it costs. Selection moves to `com.snapworth.monthly`: the header now reads "Loading plans…" forever, the CTA reads "Subscribe Monthly" and is inert, and the only way out is guessing that tapping the yearly card back restores a working screen. On this path the paywall cannot be bought from at all.

**Fix.** Add `.disabled(!isLoaded(Config.monthlyProductID))` to the monthly card (line 82) and `.disabled(!isLoaded(Config.yearlyProductID))` to the yearly card (line 70), next to the existing `.redacted` modifiers — a placeholder should not be selectable. `isLoaded` is already defined at line 276.

### Widget/Live Activity resync is wired only to the condition chip; every other mutation in this sheet skips it

`ios/SnapWorth/Views/ResultView.swift:292`

ScanRepository.refreshWidget() has exactly one caller in the whole app (line 292). Its docstring claims a condition change is "the only way a value moves without a row being added or removed" (ScanRepository.swift:112-117), but ResultView is also the app's only ledger editor and its only re-pricing path, and neither resyncs: the onChange handlers at lines 216, 222 and 226 write paidPrice / soldPrice / feesEstimate, setStatus at lines 493-516 writes status and soldDate, and line 838 replaces valueLow/valueHigh and itemName. WidgetDataStore.writeHaul derives totalLow/totalHigh from priceRange(for:), lastItemName/recentFinds from itemName, and monthProfit/monthFlips from status + soldDate + realizedProfit (= soldPrice - paidPrice - fees) — WidgetDataStore.swift:615-651. scheduleWidgetSync also drives ThriftRunController, so the thrift-run Live Activity lags identically (ScanRepository.swift:122-133).

**What happens.** User opens a find from My Flips, taps "Sold", types 80 in "Sold for" and 12 in "What did you pay?". The Home Screen Pro widget still reads "This month: $0 from 0 flips" — and after a tag re-read the haul total and the last-item range still show the pre-re-read numbers — until the user happens to scan or delete something, which is the next thing that calls scheduleWidgetSync.

**Fix.** Call ScanRepository(context: modelContext).refreshWidget() from the three money onChange handlers (lines 219, 223, 227), at the end of setStatus (line 515), and in the rescan success branch after line 838. It is already debounced 600ms, so keystroke-frequency calls coalesce.

### One paywall sheet hardcodes trigger: .snapSell for three different entry points

`ios/SnapWorth/Views/ResultView.swift:247`

ResultView has a single `.sheet(isPresented: $showPaywall)` that always constructs PaywallView with trigger: .snapSell (line 247), but three distinct buttons set showPaywall = true: "Add the tag" (line 808, pre-tracked as .addTag at line 806), "Unlock why this price" (line 911, pre-tracked as .valuationDetail at line 910) and "Unlock marketplace listings" (line 1189, pre-tracked as .snapSell). PaywallView tracks .paywallViewed(trigger:) itself in .onAppear (PaywallView.swift:239), so every open from this sheet emits a second event labelled snap_sell. Every other presenter in the app passes a trigger that matches its call site (ScanView.swift:301 uses vm.paywallTrigger, FlipsView.swift:43 uses paywallTrigger); ResultView is the only one where the two disagree.

**What happens.** A free user taps "Unlock why this price". TelemetryDeck receives paywall_viewed{trigger: valuation_detail} followed by paywall_viewed{trigger: snap_sell} for the same impression, and any purchase that follows is attributed to snap_sell. The valuation-detail and add-tag paywall conversion rates read as ~0 while snap_sell absorbs all three surfaces' traffic — which is exactly the number docs/AUDIT-2026-09-07.md recommendation 6 asks to measure before/after.

**Fix.** Add `@State private var paywallTrigger: PaywallTrigger = .snapSell`, set it at each of the three call sites before showPaywall = true, and pass it at line 247: `PaywallView(purchaseService: purchaseService, trigger: paywallTrigger)`. Then drop the manual Analytics.shared.track(.paywallViewed(...)) at lines 806, 910 and 1188, since PaywallView.swift:239 already emits it.

### No capture-in-flight guard: a double shutter tap wipes the freeze-frame mid-analysis, and can spend a second free scan

`ios/SnapWorth/Views/ScanView.swift:189`

The shutter is disabled only on `vm.isAnalyzing || cameraManager.authStatus != .authorized` (line 189). `isAnalyzing` does not become true until the photo has been delivered and round-tripped through the delegate → `cameraManager.capturedImage` → `onChange` (line 241) → `triggerScan` → `startScan`, i.e. 300-500ms after the tap, so a second tap inside that window issues a second `capturePhoto` (CameraManager.swift:123-130). Both delegate callbacks assign `capturedImage`, and `onChange` fires for each because `UIImage` equality is NSObject identity — two distinct decoded images are never equal. So `triggerScan` runs twice. The second `startScan` is correctly rejected by `guard !isAnalyzing` (ScanViewModel.swift:47) and returns immediately — but `triggerScan` then carries on past the await and executes lines 394-395 unconditionally, clearing `vm.capturedImage` while scan #1 is still in flight.

**What happens.** Double-tap the shutter (the natural reaction when the first tap appears to do nothing — see the silent-drop finding): roughly 150ms after the analysing overlay appears, the frozen capture behind it (lines 229-235) is wiped and the overlay spends the remaining 1-4s of the scan sitting on top of the live camera feed — exactly what the comment at lines 226-227 says must not happen, so the user keeps holding the phone up. Tail case: if the second photo lands after scan #1 has completed (slow capture in low light, fast scan), `startScan` is not blocked — a second scan runs, decrementing `freeScansUsed` and spending a second server quota unit for one double-tap (ScanViewModel.swift:91-96), and `vm.scanResult` is swapped under the already-presented result sheet, which re-reads it at line 345.

**Fix.** Track the in-flight capture in `CameraManager`: `@Published private(set) var isCapturing = false`, set true in `capturePhoto()` before `photoOutput.capturePhoto`, cleared on both delegate paths (CameraManager.swift:145, :148), and add `|| cameraManager.isCapturing` to the shutter's `.disabled`. Then make `triggerScan`'s cleanup conditional on having actually run a scan — e.g. have `startScan` return `Bool` and skip lines 394-398 when it returned false.

### **[FIXED]** V2 notifications carrying `summary` instead of `data` are 400'd before their type is read, so Apple retries them for ~3 days

`backend/appstorenotify.py:162`

`parse_notification` requires `payload["data"]` to be a dict (lines 160-162) before anything else is inspected. In Apple's `responseBodyV2DecodedPayload` the `data`, `summary` and `externalPurchaseToken` fields are mutually exclusive: `RENEWAL_EXTENSION` with subtype `SUMMARY` (the response to a mass renewal-date extension) carries `summary`, and `EXTERNAL_PURCHASE_TOKEN` carries `externalPurchaseToken`. Both are genuine, correctly-signed, for-our-bundle notifications with no `data` member, so line 162 raises and main.py:1172-1176 converts it to HTTP 400. This contradicts the module's own stated contract at lines 45-47 ("Anything absent here is verified, acknowledged and ignored") and the behaviour the runbook promises at docs/RUNBOOK.md:685 — the guarantee only actually holds for unrecognised types that happen to carry `data`, which is the only shape backend/tests/test_appstorenotify.py:302-309 exercises (`CONSUMPTION_REQUEST`). A secondary effect: because `notificationType` and `notificationUUID` are not read until lines 185-191, the WARNING at main.py:1175 prints only "Notification carries no data" with no type and no UUID, so the operator cannot tell which notification is stuck.

**What happens.** When the operator calls Apple's `PUT /inApps/v1/subscriptions/extend/mass/{productId}`, Apple posts a `RENEWAL_EXTENSION`/`SUMMARY` notification. The endpoint answers 400, Apple records `UNSUCCESSFUL_HTTP_RESPONSE_CODE` and redelivers the same notification on its retry schedule for roughly three days, each attempt emitting a `rejected App Store notification` WARNING — the exact signal docs/RUNBOOK.md:687-689 tells the operator to read as "the wrong bundle is configured in App Store Connect or someone is probing the endpoint".

**Fix.** Read `notificationType`, `notificationUUID`, `subtype` and `signedDate` first (move the current lines 185-197 above the data block). Then: if the type is `TEST` or in `INDEXED_TYPES`, require `data` and proceed exactly as now; otherwise return a `Notification` with `entitlement=None` so `is_indexed` is False and main.py:1197-1198 answers 200 "ignored". Take `bundleId`/`environment` from whichever of `data`/`summary` is present so the bundle and environment gates still run. Also hoist the `if not otid or not note.is_indexed: return` guard in notify.subscription_event (backend/notify.py:893) above the `ent.original_transaction_id` dereference at line 892, which would otherwise raise AttributeError on an entitlement-less notification.

### **[FIXED]** The unauthenticated-route IP limiter keys on the client-supplied hop, so /auth/challenge, /auth/attest and /auth/refresh are limited by a header the caller picks

`backend/auth.py:257`

Reported because it is the bypass of the `ratelimit.py` IP backstop I was asked to assess, even though the defective line is in auth.py. `_limit_unauthenticated` passes `request.client.host` into `deps.ip_limiter` (which is `main._enforce_ip_limit`, wired at main.py:201), and that value is used verbatim as the limiter key (main.py:564, `f"ip:{ip}"`). Dockerfile:57 runs uvicorn with `--proxy-headers --forwarded-allow-ips='*'`, which makes `request.client.host` the *leftmost* X-Forwarded-For entry — entirely caller-supplied. `_client_ip`'s own docstring (main.py:519-527) spells this out and RUNBOOK.md:542 repeats it, which is exactly why the authenticated request path was moved to the rightmost hop for AUDIT-2026-09-07 B-14. The fix for B-5 ("unauthenticated auth routes have no rate limit") then used the unsafe source, so those three routes still have no effective limit — a fresh bucket per request rather than a shared one. These are the routes in front of the quota: `/challenge` writes a Redis key per call and `/attest` runs a full x.509 chain walk.

**What happens.** Send `POST /auth/challenge` repeatedly with a different `X-Forwarded-For: 1.2.3.<n>` on each request. Uvicorn rewrites `request.client.host` to that value, `_enforce_ip_limit` keys on `ip:1.2.3.<n>`, every bucket is empty, and `IP_RATE_MAX_REQUESTS` (60/h) is never reached — unbounded challenge minting and unbounded attestation chain walks from one source. The same header defeats the limiter on `/auth/attest` and `/auth/refresh`.

**Fix.** Use the same accessor the request path uses: replace lines 256-257 with `await deps.ip_limiter(_client_ip_value)` where the value comes from the rightmost X-Forwarded-For hop. Cleanest is to move `main._client_ip` into a shared module (or pass it in alongside `deps.ip_limiter`) and have `_limit_unauthenticated` call `await deps.ip_limiter(deps.client_ip(request))`, so there is exactly one definition of "the source IP" in the codebase and no route can pick the spoofable one.

### **[FIXED]** Chain walk accepts any leaf certified under Apple Root CA G3 — the App Store signing purpose OID is never checked

`backend/entitlements.py:271`

`_verify_chain` returns `certs[0]` (line 271) after proving only three things: every cert is in its validity window, `certs[-1]` is byte-identical to the pinned Apple Root CA G3, and each adjacent pair is signed by the next. It never checks what the leaf was *issued for*. Apple's own verification procedure — and their `app-store-server-library` `ChainVerifier` — additionally requires the marker extension OID `1.2.840.113635.100.6.11.1` on the leaf and `1.2.840.113635.100.6.2.1` on the intermediate, because a single root certifies many unrelated Apple certificate families and the OID is the only thing that says "this key is allowed to sign StoreKit/App Store Server JWS". The comment at lines 247-251 identifies exactly this class of risk for the *intermediate* slot and closes it with `_require_ca`, but leaves the leaf slot open. This is not confined to the reporting path: `verify_apple_jws` is also the gate for `verify_signed_transaction` (line 362), which `EntitlementService.record` and `auth.record_entitlement` (backend/auth.py:437) use to grant Pro.

**What happens.** An adversary in possession of any ECDSA private key whose certificate was issued by any CA chaining to Apple Root CA G3 — not Apple's App Store JWS signing key — can sign a payload, attach that leaf plus its genuine issuing chain plus the real root as `x5c`, and `verify_apple_jws` returns the payload as authentic. With `bundleId` set to ours that yields (a) forged App Store Server Notifications writing the operator's revenue index and Telegram alerts via main.py:1200, and (b) a forged `signedTransaction` with a future `expiresDate` POSTed to /auth/entitlement, which upgrades the caller to Pro. The pinned root is doing all the work here, and it is a much weaker statement than "Apple signed this transaction".

**Fix.** After `leaf = _verify_chain(certs)` in `verify_apple_jws` (line 320), require `leaf.extensions.get_extension_for_oid(x509.ObjectIdentifier("1.2.840.113635.100.6.11.1"))` to be present and raise `EntitlementError` when it is not; require the same for `1.2.840.113635.100.6.2.1` on `certs[1]`. Combine with the fixed chain length of 3 from the bound above. The test fixtures in backend/tests/test_entitlements.py `_make_cert`/`build_chain` will need to add those extensions to the synthetic leaf and intermediate.

### **[FIXED]** The sharing alert — the only remaining control on a shared JWS — is silenced by a client-chosen device_id

`backend/entitlements.py:606`

The device cap deliberately does not refuse anyone (lines 548, 573: "The cap is anti-abuse, not an authorisation boundary"), so the `notify.subscription_over_cap` alert at lines 607-609 *is* the control: "What a genuinely shared subscription gets instead is a visible signal: steady churn, reported."

That alert is gated on `not _is_legacy_subject(evicted)` (line 606), and `_is_legacy_subject` (line 139) infers the identity's *kind* from the shape of the string: `len(identity) == 64 and all(c in "0123456789abcdef")`. But `identity` is `device_id or subject` (line 575), and `device_id` comes straight off the wire — auth.py:170 validates it only as `^[A-Za-z0-9._-]+$`, max 64 chars. A 64-character lowercase hex string satisfies both, so a client can declare itself a pre-1.3.4 reinstall ghost.

The docstring at lines 599-605 explains the intent correctly (don't report migration churn as sharing) but the implementation trusts the attacker to describe itself. The honest clients cannot trip this by accident — `TokenStore.swift:134` generates `UUID().uuidString`, 36 chars with dashes — which means a 64-hex identity is *only* ever an attacker or a legacy ghost, and the code reads it as the harmless one.

**What happens.** Twenty people share one $39.99 yearly JWS in a Discord, each running a build that sends `device_id` as a random 64-character lowercase hex string. Every `/auth/entitlement` POST past the sixth evicts the least-recently-seen binding with `idle_seconds` near zero — textbook sharing churn — and every one of those evictions is classified as a reinstall ghost, so `notify.subscription_over_cap` is never called. The operator's Telegram stays silent while one subscription serves twenty users, and because the cap does not refuse, nothing else notices either.

**Fix.** Stop inferring the identity's kind from its shape; record it when it is written. Store bindings as `{identity: {"seen": ts, "kind": "device"}}` (tolerating the two older shapes in `_decode_bindings`, which already handles the bare-list form), or prefix stable ids at line 575 — `identity = f"dev:{device_id}" if device_id else subject` — and treat only unprefixed 64-hex entries as legacy. Either way a client-supplied value can no longer masquerade as a ghost. `_is_legacy_subject`'s docstring claim that "the two never collide" is true for the honest client and false for the one this alert exists to catch.

### **[FIXED]** Unbounded attacker-controlled `notification_type` is logged at ERROR from the unauthenticated endpoint

`backend/main.py:1127`

`AppleNotification.signedPayload` is explicitly length-capped on the line immediately above (`max_length=appstorenotify.MAX_SIGNED_PAYLOAD`, lines 1124-1125), but `notification_type: str | None = None` at line 1127 has no `max_length` and no pattern. Its only use is to be interpolated verbatim into the ERROR log at line 1157-1163, which is reached with no signature check, no authentication and no rate limiter — it is the first branch of the handler, taken whenever `signedPayload` is absent. FastAPI reads the whole JSON body before validation, so the field size is bounded by nothing in this codebase. `configure_logging` defaults `json_output=False` (backend/observability.py:74), i.e. the plain `%(message)s` formatter at 80-83; only `LOG_FORMAT=json` selects the JSON formatter that would escape newlines, and that is set in .env.example:40 rather than in code or the Dockerfile.

**What happens.** `curl -X POST https://api.snapworth.eu/apple/notifications -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{"notification_type":"<1 MB of text>"}'` emits a 1 MB ERROR record per request, repeatable at whatever rate the attacker chooses, inflating log storage and drowning the `Version 1` diagnostic this branch exists to surface. With `LOG_FORMAT` unset — the code default — a value containing `\n2026-09-12T00:00:00 ERROR snapworth ...` forges additional well-formed lines in the plain-text log, so anything reading those logs line-wise (alerting, grep-based triage in the runbook) can be fed fabricated records by an unauthenticated caller.

**Fix.** Cap the field: `notification_type: str | None = Field(default=None, max_length=64)` at line 1127, matching the treatment `signedPayload` already gets, and log it defensively as `%r` on a slice (`body.notification_type[:64]!r`) at line 1163 so control characters cannot reach a line-oriented formatter. The same per-IP limiter recommended for the chain-bound finding also caps how often this branch can be driven.

### No request-level body cap: the whole multipart file part is received and spooled to disk before any /scan code, auth, or rate limit runs

`backend/main.py:1294`

`_read_capped` (main.py:1248, called at 1294) cannot bound what the caller makes the process hold, because it never sees the network. FastAPI resolves `file: UploadFile = File(...)` by calling `await request.form()` inside the route handler *before* `solve_dependencies`, so the entire multipart body is already parsed when `scan()`'s first line executes -- before `require_auth` (1285), before `_enforce_limits` (1328), before `_refuse_if_paused` (1332). Starlette 1.6.0 applies `max_part_size` only on the non-file branch of `MultiPartParser.on_part_data` (formparsers.py:181-188); a *file* part is appended to `_file_parts_to_write` with no size check and written to `SpooledTemporaryFile(max_size=1MB)` (formparsers.py:230), which rolls over to a real temp file on the container filesystem. Nothing anywhere in backend/ reads the request-level `Content-Length` (grep: the only two hits are the comments at main.py:1253 and 1292). This is the un-implemented half of AUDIT-2026-09-07 B-1: the fix shipped the chunked read (which does stop the 2GB-in-a-`bytes`-object OOM), but B-1's other recommendation -- "check the request-level Content-Length header before parsing" -- was never added, and the docstring at 1249-1259 now asserts the whole problem is solved.

**What happens.** `POST /scan` with `Content-Type: multipart/form-data`, no Authorization header, and a `file` part of 4 GB: uvicorn + Starlette receive and write all 4 GB to the container's filesystem, then `require_auth` 401s (or `_read_capped` returns 400). The Dockerfile runs `uvicorn --workers 1`, so one such request fills the replica's ephemeral disk and starves every in-flight scan; it needs no credential and no quota.

**Fix.** Add an `@app.middleware("http")` ahead of the route that rejects `POST /scan` (and `/listing`) when the request-level `content-length` header exceeds `MAX_UPLOAD_BYTES` plus a small multipart overhead, returning 413 before `request.form()` is ever reached; for chunked bodies with no `content-length`, wrap `receive` to tally bytes and abort past the same cap. Keep `_read_capped` as the second line of defence.

### **[FIXED]** /scan's 403 safety-pause copy is discarded by the client -- a 24h lockout renders as "Something went wrong. Please try again."

`backend/main.py:1334`

`_refuse_if_paused` raises 403 with carefully written user-facing copy ("Scanning from this device is paused for 24 hours after repeated photos that could not be analysed.", main.py:333-336). `APIErrorDetail.parse` (ScanAPIClient.swift:566-581) extracts that string correctly and `ScanAPIError.serverError` carries it, but `AppError.from` has no case for 403 -- it falls to `default: return .unknown(detail)` (AppError.swift:135), and `.unknown`'s `errorDescription` (AppError.swift:92-93) ignores its associated value and returns the fixed string "Something went wrong. Please try again." `ScanViewModel.swift:131` calls `AppError.from(error)` and that is what the alert shows. The same defect class was explicitly hunted down and fixed for 401 (AppError.swift:60) and 422 (AppError.swift:119); 400 and 403 -- the two statuses the /scan validation and policy paths actually use (main.py:1270, 1307, 1334) -- were missed. The 400 messages ("Image exceeds 10 MB limit.", "That image is too small to identify.", "Unsupported file type ...") are unreachable from the shipping client, which downscales to 1568px at q0.8 (ScanAPIClient.swift:428-443), but the 403 is entirely server-initiated and the client has no pre-check for it.

**What happens.** A device whose fifth photo in a rolling 24h trips Gemini's safety filter crosses `SAFETY_BLOCKS_BEFORE_PAUSE=5` (main.py:300). Every subsequent /scan for the life of the `safety:blocks:` key (24h, main.py:301) returns 403. The user sees "Something went wrong. Please try again." -- literally an instruction to retry -- and retries indefinitely for a day, never learning the device is paused or that a different photo is the remedy. Each retry also burns a rate-limit slot.

**Fix.** Add explicit cases in `AppError.from` (AppError.swift, beside the 422 case at :122) for the statuses whose `detail` is backend-authored user-safe copy: `case 400, 403: return .unusablePhoto(detail)` (or a new case that surfaces `detail` verbatim, as `.aiFailed`/`.unusablePhoto` already do). If the mapping cannot be changed, have main.py:1334 return a status the client already surfaces rather than inventing one it drops.

### POST /apple/notifications has no rate limiter at all

`backend/main.py:1136`

`apple_notifications` (main.py:1136-1137) takes no `Request`, never calls `_enforce_ip_limit`/`_enforce_limits`, and is not covered by `auth._limit_unauthenticated`. It is the only unauthenticated write endpoint in the app with no throttle: `/auth/challenge`, `/auth/attest` and `/auth/refresh` all call `_limit_unauthenticated` (auth.py:263, 281, 331) after AUDIT-2026-09-07 #150 made exactly this argument for exactly this reason. Every request runs `appstorenotify.parse_notification` → `entitlements.verify_apple_jws`, which base64-decodes the JWS, parses every certificate in the attacker-supplied `x5c` header (`_decode_x5c_chain`, entitlements.py:222-229 — bounded only by `MAX_SIGNED_PAYLOAD` = 65_536, so ~40 DER parses per request), then walks validity dates and the pinned-root comparison. It also emits a `log.warning` with the rejection reason per call (main.py:1195). The JSON body itself is unbounded — `signedPayload`'s `max_length` is a pydantic constraint applied only after Starlette has buffered the entire body — and the Dockerfile runs a single uvicorn worker (`--workers 1`), so this is the same shape as the `_read_capped` problem on /scan but with no cap and no limiter.

**What happens.** 80 consecutive `POST /apple/notifications {"signedPayload":"garbage"}` from one source all return 400 with zero 429s, each having run a full JWS parse attempt; the same 80 calls to /auth/challenge produce 20x 429. A 2 MB JSON body is accepted and buffered. Anyone who knows the URL (it is in docs/RUNBOOK.md:594 and in the public /openapi.json — see the separate finding) can hold the single worker's event loop and flood the log with no credential of any kind.

**Fix.** Give the handler the request and throttle it like the other unauthenticated routes: `async def apple_notifications(body: AppleNotification, request: Request)` and `await _enforce_ip_limit(_client_ip(request))` as the first statement. Apple's own delivery volume is a few notifications per day, so a bucket far below `IP_RATE_MAX_REQUESTS` is safe; because Apple retries any non-2xx for ~3 days, prefer a limit high enough that a genuine retry burst never trips it.

### **[FIXED]** Unauthenticated log injection and unbounded log write through AppleNotification.notification_type

`backend/main.py:1127`

`notification_type: str | None = None` (main.py:1127) carries no `max_length` and no charset constraint, unlike `signedPayload` on the line above it. It is interpolated straight into `log.error(...)` at main.py:1157-1163 on the Version-1 detection path, which runs before any signature is checked and before the `signedPayload is None` rejection — so an unauthenticated caller controls the content and the length of a production log record. `observability.RedactionFilter` applies `redact()` to the args (observability.py:237) but `redact` only masks secrets; it does not strip newlines or bound length. The non-JSON formatter is a single `%(message)s` line (observability.py:80), and `LOG_FORMAT=json` is an UNTICKED item on the launch checklist (docs/RUNBOOK.md:544), so plain text is the likely production state. The codebase already defends this exact class one file over: `RequestContextMiddleware` rejects an inbound `X-Request-ID` that is unprintable, newline-bearing or over 64 chars because "it lands in logs, so an unbounded or newline-bearing value would be a log-injection vector" (observability.py:100-102).

**What happens.** `POST /apple/notifications` with `{"notification_type": "V1\nSEP 12 CRITICAL snapworth.auth [x] ATTEST_SUCCEEDED subject=deadbeef tier=pro"}` emits the forged second line verbatim into the log stream — reproduced against a TestClient with the plain formatter. A second call with a 2,000,000-character `notification_type` writes all 2 MB into one log record (the run produced 1.9 MB of output). Both are 400 responses, so nothing stops a caller repeating either indefinitely — this endpoint has no rate limiter (see the separate finding).

**Fix.** Constrain the field at the schema — `notification_type: str | None = Field(default=None, max_length=64)` — and scrub before logging, e.g. log `body.notification_type[:64].replace("\n", " ").replace("\r", " ")` (or reuse the printable/length test `RequestContextMiddleware` already applies to `X-Request-ID`). The field exists only to name which App Store Connect knob is wrong, so 64 printable characters is more than the message needs.

### **[FIXED]** A refund that crosses UTC midnight decrements the new day's counter, silently releasing a live reservation and granting an extra free scan

`backend/quota.py:252`

`reserve` increments `_counter_key(subject)` (line 223-224), which embeds `_utc_day()` evaluated at reservation time. `_release` recomputes `_counter_key(subject)` at line 253, i.e. `_utc_day()` evaluated at refund time. Nothing ties the refund to the key the reservation actually claimed. A scan that starts before 00:00:00 UTC and refunds after it decrements tomorrow's counter instead of today's. Two consequences: yesterday's counter stays raised (the module's stated purpose at lines 21-22, "charging for a failed scan is both unfair and a support burden", is defeated, though the practical loss is nil because the day already rolled over), and the new day's counter is decremented below what is actually reserved. The `used < 0` clamp at lines 257-264 absorbs this only when no concurrent reservation exists on the new day; when one does, the clamp never fires and the decrement is a straight give-away. `/scan` has no per-subject serialisation — the rate limiter allows 20/h (main.py:1328) and both the Thrift Flip path and the widget can fire scans — so concurrent reservations for one subject are reachable. The refund is triggered on any `_analyse` failure (main.py:1347-1349) and on `request.is_disconnected()` (main.py:1354-1356); a Gemini call runs 5-25s, so the window is roughly the last 30 seconds of each UTC day per user.

**What happens.** Executed against the real modules with limit=1: reserve at 2026-09-12 23:59:5x -> day-N counter = 1. Midnight rolls to 2026-09-13. A second scan reserves on the new day -> day-N+1 counter = 1 (its one legitimate allowance, correctly claimed). The straddling day-N scan then fails and `refund` runs: day-N counter stays at 1 (charged for nothing) and day-N+1 counter goes 1 -> 0. A third `reserve` on 2026-09-13 is then GRANTED, so the subject gets two free scans that day instead of one. If the `set(..., "0")` at line 261 also fails (the `except CacheUnavailable: pass` at 263-264), the counter is left at -1 and `status.remaining` reports limit+1, so `reserve` grants two extra scans with no concurrency required at all.

**Fix.** Make the reservation carry its key. Have `reserve` return (or stash on the returned `QuotaStatus`) the exact counter key it incremented, and have `refund`/`_release` take that key instead of recomputing `_utc_day()`. Minimal version: add a `day: str | None = None` parameter to `refund`/`_release` and pass `QuotaStatus`'s reservation day from `auth.refund_quota`. Separately, drop the `used < 0` clamp's unconditional `set` to "0" — once the refund targets the right key it can only mask a genuine reset, and today it is what erases another request's live claim.

### DeviceCheck bit0 is never cleared, so "until the next reset" never happens: any user who has ever seen the paywall gets zero free scans on the day of any legitimate reinstall

`backend/quota.py:289`

`note_exhausted` sets `bit0=True` (line 289) and nothing anywhere sets it back to False — `update_bits` is called from exactly one place (grep: quota.py:289 is the only writer) and there is no periodic reset. But `starting_balance`'s own docstring at line 298 says a recognised reinstall "gets nothing back until the next reset", and `note_exhausted`'s docstring (line 279) frames the bit as "has spent its allowance", which is a daily fact. A single bit cannot carry a day, so the daily signal the code is written against is actually permanent. With `FREE_SCANS_PER_DAY = 1` (line 39), `reserve_quota` raises `QuotaExceeded` and calls `note_exhausted` (auth.py:494-496) on the second scan attempt of any day — i.e. for essentially every engaged free user, within their first day. bit0 therefore does not distinguish abusers from users; it flags nearly the whole free base, permanently. Then at lines 321-325 any future install on that hardware has its day-one counter overwritten with `str(self._limit)`, and line 334-335 writes `_DENIED` to the welcome key, permanently disqualifying that subject from the first-day allowance. Note that `query_bits` returns Apple's raw JSON (devicecheck.py:111), which includes `last_update_time` ("YYYY-MM") — the coarse period Apple provides for exactly this purpose — and line 321 reads only `bits.get("bit0")`, discarding it.

**What happens.** A free user hits the 402 once (their second scan attempt on any day) in September; bit0 is set forever. In January they set up a new phone from an encrypted backup, or simply re-download the app they deleted: a fresh App Attest key gives a new subject, `/auth/attest` passes the device token to `starting_balance`, `query_bits` returns bit0=True, and quota.py:324-325 presets that day's counter to 1 = the limit. `starting_balance` returns 0, so the mint reports `free_scans_remaining: 0` and a legitimately new install lands on the paywall before it has ever produced a valuation. The `_DENIED` write at 334-335 also means that install can never receive the first-day welcome the lever exists to create. The user recovers a normal 1/day from the following UTC day, but never the welcome.

**Fix.** Use the period Apple already returns. At line 321, treat bit0 as authoritative only when `bits.get("last_update_time")` equals the current "YYYY-MM"; when it is older, call `update_bits(device_token, bit0=False, bit1=False)` and fall through to `_welcome`. Alternatively store the exhaustion day in the durable cache keyed on the device token and gate the refusal on that, keeping bit0 purely as a cross-reinstall hint. Either way, also correct line 298's "until the next reset" or make a reset exist.

### **[FIXED]** snapSage's light value is 3.61:1 on a card and 3.38:1 on the background — the AA-compliant green is defined but only reachable via Increased Contrast

`ios/SnapWorth/DesignSystem/DesignSystem.swift:52`

`snapSage` light = #6F8F6B (line 52) measures 3.61:1 on snapCard (white) and 3.38:1 on snapBackground (#FBF7F2) — under the 4.5:1 AA minimum. The comment on line 51 labels it "money / positive values", and it is the colour of every price. The failure is real inside this file at line 761, where `ScanHistoryCard` draws `result.formattedRange` in `.fraunces(16, weight: .bold)` — 16 iOS pt ≈ 12 WCAG pt bold, below the 14pt-bold large-text threshold, so 4.5:1 applies and 3.61:1 fails. `ValueRangeView` (line 414) is fine because 44pt is genuinely large text (3:1 floor). The correct value already exists three lines down: `lightHighContrast: Color(hex: "4F6E4B")` on line 53 measures 5.73:1 — but `snapAdaptive` only serves it when `traits.accessibilityContrast == .high` (line 26), so a default-settings user never sees it. Same shape as the `--terra-text` finding in AUDIT-2026-09-07.md:184 ("defined at index.html:58 … used by nothing"), which was fixed on the website and not here.

**What happens.** Open History in light mode with default accessibility settings (HistoryView.swift:131 → ScanHistoryCard). Every scan's price range renders at 3.61:1 on the white card. The dark half of the pair is fine (#8FB08A on #221B17 = 7.07:1), so this is light-mode-only — i.e. the default.

**Fix.** Promote the compliant green to the default: `light: Color(hex: "4F6E4B")` on line 52, and pick a darker still value (or drop the override) for `lightHighContrast`. If the lighter green must stay for large hero numerals, split the token as the widget did with terracotta — `snapSage` for ≥18pt display use, `snapSageText` at #4F6E4B for body-sized money.

### GhostButton's label is snapTerracotta on the page background — 3.18:1 light mode at 17pt semibold

`ios/SnapWorth/DesignSystem/DesignSystem.swift:360`

Line 360 draws the label in `Color.snapTerracotta` with no background of its own, so it sits on `snapBackground`. Light terracotta #D96C47 on #FBF7F2 measures 3.18:1 (3.39:1 if the button happens to sit on a white card) at `.snapButton` = dmSans 17pt semibold, against a 4.5:1 requirement. The `lightHighContrast` value #BE5433 reaches 4.66:1 on white / 4.37:1 on the cream background, so even Increased Contrast is borderline-to-failing. Dark mode passes (#E8845F on #17120F = 7.4:1). The `ProgressView().tint(Color.snapTerracotta)` on line 356 has the same problem as a non-text indicator, and the 1.5pt capsule stroke on line 367 sits at 3.18:1 — right at the 3:1 non-text floor.

**What happens.** Open the paywall in light mode: "Restore purchase" (PaywallView.swift:167) and the "Try again" retry that appears when StoreKit pricing fails to load (PaywallView.swift:130) both render at 3.18:1. The restore path is App Review-relevant and is the one a user reaches when they have already paid.

**Fix.** Use the darker foreground for terracotta text rather than the brand accent: either promote #BE5433 to `light:` on line 48 (it is 4.66:1 on white, 4.37:1 on cream — pair it with a darker still HC value), or add a `snapTerracottaText` token at ≈#A94E29 (6.1:1, the value the website already settled on per AUDIT-2026-09-07.md:184) and use it at lines 356/360.

### snapBorder is 1.21-1.23:1 against the card in both themes, so an unselected plan radio and card outline have no visible boundary

`ios/SnapWorth/DesignSystem/DesignSystem.swift:654`

Line 654 draws the radio ring and line 695 the card outline in `Color.snapBorder` when `isSelected == false`. Measured against the surface they sit on: light #EFE6DC on snapCard white = 1.23:1; dark #342A24 on #221B17 = 1.21:1. WCAG 1.4.11 requires 3:1 for the visual boundary of a UI component, and the ring is the *only* affordance identifying an unselected radio — the inner dot (line 657-660) is drawn only when selected, and the card's own edge is the same failing token. The high-contrast pairs on line 73 are defined and still fail: #D3C4B4 on white = 1.70:1, #4C3E35 on #221B17 = 1.66:1. So a user who turns on Increased Contrast specifically to see control boundaries gets a 0.45 ratio-point improvement and still cannot see the ring. The selected state is fine (terracotta at 3.39:1 light / 6.38:1 dark), which means selection is legible but the set of available choices is not.

**What happens.** Open the paywall in either theme (PaywallView.swift:55-81). The Monthly card, being unselected, shows no radio ring and no card edge — its price and title appear to float on the background with no indication they are a tappable alternative. Turning on Settings → Accessibility → Display & Text Size → Increase Contrast does not fix it (1.70:1).

**Fix.** The border token is correct for dividers and skeletons but too weak for control affordances. Add a second token for interactive boundaries at ≥3:1 against snapCard — e.g. #8A7A6C light (≈3.2:1 on white) / #6B5A4E dark — and use it at lines 654 and 695 for the unselected case. Alternatively raise the HC pair on line 73 to a value that actually clears 3:1, since that is the setting's whole purpose.

### snapCardShadow is a fixed warm brown that is lighter than the dark-mode background, so cards lose all separation in dark mode

`ios/SnapWorth/DesignSystem/DesignSystem.swift:90`

`snapCardShadow` (line 90) is a hardcoded `Color(red: 120/255, green: 80/255, blue: 50/255)` = #785032 — the only colour in the palette that is not routed through `snapAdaptive`. Its relative luminance is 0.0996, which is *higher* than snapBackground's dark value #17120F (0.0065). Applied at 8% (line 269), it composites over the dark ground to #1F1712 — so in dark mode the "shadow" is a faint lightening halo whose contrast against the card #221B17 is 1.04:1, i.e. it is less distinguishable from the card than the plain background is (1.09:1). `SnapCardModifier` (lines 263-273) applies background + clipShape + shadow and no stroke, so in dark mode a card has no boundary at all: 1.09:1 to the background, an invisible halo, no border. Light mode works — the same shadow composites to #F1EAE3, 1.19:1 against the white card, which is the intended soft lift.

**What happens.** Put the device in dark mode and open History (HistoryView.swift:131) or any Result card (ResultView.swift:275, 355, 384, 697, 736, 816, 882, 939, 977, 1016 all repeat the same shadow call). Adjacent cards in the 2-column grid merge into one continuous surface — there is no edge between a card and the gap beside it.

**Fix.** Make the shadow adaptive like every other token — pure black at a higher opacity in dark mode (`snapAdaptive(light: Color(red: 120/255, green: 80/255, blue: 50/255), dark: .black)`) — and add a 1pt hairline stroke to `SnapCardModifier` in dark mode so separation does not depend on a shadow that cannot work on a near-black ground.

### Common URLError codes fall through to "Something went wrong" instead of the network message

`ios/SnapWorth/Models/AppError.swift:150`

The `switch url?.code` at lines 149-156 maps only `.notConnectedToInternet`, `.networkConnectionLost`, `.cannotConnectToHost` and `.timedOut`. Every other URLError drops through `default: break` (line 154) to the `localizedDescription` substring test at lines 158-162, which looks for "429"/"rate limit"/"network"/"offline"/"timeout"/"timed out"/"502"/"503". None of those substrings appear in the system strings for `.cancelled` ("cancelled"), `.cannotFindHost` ("A server with the specified hostname could not be found."), `.dnsLookupFailed`, `.secureConnectionFailed` ("An SSL error has occurred and a secure connection to the server cannot be made.") or `.dataNotAllowed`. All of them therefore reach `.unknown` and render "Something went wrong. Please try again." (line 93) — the least informative string in the app, with no hint that the network is at fault and no suggested remedy.

**What happens.** Two triggers are reachable today. (a) TLS interception: `CertificatePinningDelegate` calls `completionHandler(.cancelAuthenticationChallenge, nil)` when `SecTrustEvaluateWithError` fails (`CertificatePinning.swift:75-79`), which is what a corporate/school MITM proxy or a captive portal produces. The task then fails with `NSURLErrorCancelled`, so on that Wi-Fi every scan, listing and /trends call reports "Something went wrong. Please try again." indefinitely, and the user has no reason to suspect the network. (b) DNS failure on flaky cellular or hotel Wi-Fi yields `.cannotFindHost` or `.dnsLookupFailed` → the same message, when `.network`'s "No internet connection. Check your network and try again." (line 47) is the correct advice and is already written.

**Fix.** Extend the `.network` arm at line 150 to `.notConnectedToInternet, .networkConnectionLost, .cannotConnectToHost, .cannotFindHost, .dnsLookupFailed, .dataNotAllowed, .internationalRoamingOff, .resourceUnavailable`, and handle the two remaining cases explicitly rather than letting them fall through: add a `case .secureConnectionFailed, .serverCertificateUntrusted, .cancelled:` arm returning a distinct error whose copy names the likely cause ("This network is blocking a secure connection to SnapWorth — try cellular or another Wi-Fi network"), since for a user-initiated cancellation the caller should be suppressing the alert anyway.

### MoneyInput reads a dot-grouped amount as a decimal — "1.234.567" parses as 1234.567, a 1000x money error

`ios/SnapWorth/Models/MarketplaceFees.swift:45`

In `normalized`, the comma-only branch (lines 47-49) applies a three-digit grouping heuristic, but the dot-only branch (lines 45-46) unconditionally treats the last dot as the decimal separator. Two consequences: (a) with more than one dot and no comma, the string cannot be a decimal at all, yet the last dot is still taken as the decimal point; (b) a single dot followed by exactly three digits — the case the comma branch explicitly classifies as grouping, with the reasoning spelled out at lines 26-30 — is read as a decimal. The type's own header comment (lines 8-10) names German, French, Romanian and Brazilian users as the reason it exists, and in all four locales the dot is the grouping separator. The comma path handles the mirror input correctly: `normalized("1,234,567")` → "1234567".

**What happens.** Paste a price copied from a de-DE/pt-BR/ro-RO listing into the Paid, Sold or Fees field (paste is an explicitly supported path — see the comment at lines 32-33 about pasted currency symbols). `normalized("1.234.567")` returns "1234.567" and `normalized("1.250")` returns "1.250", so `paidPrice` becomes 1234.567 or 1.25 instead of 1234567 or 1250. Realized profit, ROI, the Invested stat and every CSV column derived from them are then off by 1000x — a $1250 buy records as a $1.25 buy, and a heavy loss reports as a large win.

**Fix.** Make the separator rule symmetric: when only one kind of separator is present and it occurs more than once, every occurrence is grouping; when it occurs once, apply the existing exactly-three-digits-after test to the dot as well as the comma (requiring at least one digit before it, so ".5" stays 0.5 and "45.50" stays 45.50).

### A negator anywhere earlier in a clause suppresses a genuine defect term, so "No stains and heavy wear at the cuffs" grades `.good`

`ios/SnapWorth/Models/ScanResult.swift:391`

`asserts` treats a clause as negated if any negator appears anywhere in the text before the needle (line 391), with no proximity or scope limit, and `clauseBreaks` (lines 370-372) contains the contrastive conjunctions but not the additive "and"/"with"/"plus". Running the algorithm: "No stains and heavy wear at the cuffs" → `.good`, because the single clause's "no " prefix suppresses "heavy". The existing test only pins the contrastive form ("No stains **but** heavy wear", ProductionHardeningTests.swift:2306), which passes because " but " is a clause break. Compounding it, line 389 examines only the *first* occurrence of the needle in a clause and `continue`s to the next clause, so "free of holes or stains and a stain on the cuff" never tests the second, asserted "stain".

**What happens.** Notes "No stains and heavy wear at the cuffs" with no usable `condition_grade` yield `baselineCondition == .good` (multiplier 1.0) for an item the model priced as worn. The chip preselects "Good" on a heavily worn item, and the user correcting it to "Used" scales the estimate by 0.78 — a 22% drop on numbers that were already the worn-item numbers. This is the same mispricing the doc comment at lines 396-409 describes, in the opposite direction.

**Fix.** Scope the negator instead of scanning the whole prefix: accept it only when it is the nearest preceding token (or within a small window, e.g. no more than two words) and no additive conjunction intervenes between it and the needle. Also loop over every occurrence of the needle in a clause rather than returning after the first, and add "No stains and heavy wear at the cuffs" → `.used` to the negation tests.

### submitEntitlement is the only authenticated call that bypasses sendRetryingAuth, so a server-rejected token silently keeps a paying user on the free tier

`ios/SnapWorth/Services/AttestationService.swift:75`

`submitEntitlement` builds the request by hand, sets `Authorization: Bearer` from the cached token (line 66), and sends it with a bare `session.data(for: request)` on line 75. Every other bearer-carrying request goes through `URLRequest.sendRetryingAuth` — ScanAPIClient.swift:408, ListingService.swift:193 and :363 — which on a 401 calls `AttestationService.invalidateCachedToken()`, re-mints and retries once. This one does not, so a 401 falls straight into the `guard http.statusCode == 200` on line 76 and throws `serverRejected`.

Nothing clears the dead token on that path. `accessToken()` (line 45-48) hands back the cached token for as long as it is more than a minute from its client-computed expiry, so every subsequent attempt re-attaches the same rejected credential. This is the failure the retry wrapper was written for, described in TokenStore.swift:198-207 in almost these words: "Left alone, `accessToken()` keeps returning that same token from cache until it expires — up to an hour — so every retry the user makes fails identically." The named trigger from AttestationService.swift:104-106 — "the backend's signing key rotates — a deploy with an ephemeral `TOKEN_KEYS` does exactly this" — produces a 401 here from backend/auth.py:386 (`TokenError` → 401 in `require_auth`, which `/auth/entitlement` depends on at auth.py:423).

The caller cannot compensate: `StoreKitPurchaseService.syncEntitlementToServer` (line 304-312) swallows the error into an `os_log` line, and the comment at :267-272 claims the retry on every status refresh makes it "self-healing if an earlier attempt failed offline" — true for offline, false for 401, because the retry re-sends the same dead token.

**What happens.** A user with the app already installed holds a token minted before a backend deploy that rotated `TOKEN_KEYS`. They buy Pro (or tap Restore Purchases). `refreshSubscriptionStatus` sets `isSubscribed = true` locally so the UI shows Pro, then fires `syncEntitlementToServer`, which 401s and is logged and dropped. The server never runs `entitlements.record`, so `require_auth` keeps resolving them as `tier="free"` and `/scan` enforces `FREE_SCANS_PER_DAY = 1`: the user has paid, the app says Pro, and their second scan comes back 402 "out of scans". Every foreground refresh repeats the same 401. It only clears when an unrelated scan happens to route a 401 through `sendRetryingAuth` and re-mint, or the token expires — after which the next status refresh finally records the purchase.

**Fix.** Route it through the same wrapper as every other authenticated call. Replace lines 66 and 75-78 with `attachBearerToken()` + `sendRetryingAuth`:

```swift
request.httpBody = try JSONEncoder().encode(EntitlementBody(...))
await request.attachBearerToken()
let (data, http) = try await request.sendRetryingAuth(on: session)
guard http.statusCode == 200 else {
    throw AttestationError.serverRejected(Self.detail(from: data))
}
```

(`attachBearerToken` calls `accessToken()` itself, so the explicit `let token = try await accessToken()` on line 62 and the manual header on line 66 both go away.) Also add a test that a 401 on `/auth/entitlement` results in exactly two requests with different Authorization headers — the `submitEntitlement` path has no test coverage at all today (docs/AUDIT-2026-09-07.md:328).

### subjectPublicKeyInfo has no P-384 branch, so the ISRG Root X2 backup pin can never match and silently degrades to a wrong hash

`ios/SnapWorth/Services/CertificatePinning.swift:134`

`subjectPublicKeyInfo` reconstructs an ASN.1 SPKI header for exactly three key shapes: RSA-2048 (line 126), RSA-4096 (line 130) and EC P-256 (line 134, `size == 256`). Anything else hits `else { return keyData }` on line 138-140 and the raw key bytes are hashed instead of the SPKI — for an EC key that is the bare uncompressed point (`04||X||Y`), whose SHA-256 bears no relation to the `openssl pkey -pubin -outform der | dgst -sha256` value the pins in Config.swift were generated from.

One of the four configured pins is such a key. `Config.swift:52` pins `ISRG Root X2` (the comment's "exp 2040-09-17" identifies it exactly), and ISRG Root X2 is ECDSA **P-384** — `kSecAttrKeySizeInBits == 384`, so line 134 is false and line 139 returns the bare 97-byte point. That pin is inert: `matchesPin` (line 103-115) can never return true for it. Let's Encrypt's ECDSA intermediates (the E-series) are P-384 too, so the same hole covers the whole ECDSA chain.

This is invisible today and stays invisible right up to the moment it matters. Config.swift:30 records the served chain as `leaf → YR2 → Root YR → ISRG Root X1`, and ISRG Root X1 is RSA-4096 — handled by line 130. So the report-only telemetry added for this purpose (line 89, `certificate_pin_mismatch`) reports zero mismatches, and the promotion gate in the doc comment at lines 22-27 ("Zero occurrences across a full release cycle means the pin set is correct") passes on a pin set that is one cert short of correct. The fallback on line 139 also makes a mis-headered key indistinguishable from a real MITM: both surface as a plain pin mismatch with no signal that the client simply could not hash the key.

Note this is not the already-fixed finding: docs/AUDIT-v2.md:373 reviewed this function when `pinnedSPKIHashes` was empty and called it correct for "RSA-2048/4096 and P-256". The defect was introduced by populating the set with a P-384 root.

**What happens.** When Let's Encrypt serves the ECDSA chain for api.snapworth.eu (leaf → P-384 E-series intermediate → ISRG Root X2 — the ACME default whenever the leaf key is ECDSA, and the direction LE's hierarchy is moving), every certificate in the evaluated chain is P-384, every one falls through line 139, and `matchesPin` returns false on a chain that is completely legitimate. With `Config.pinningEnforced` still false that is a `certificate_pin_mismatch` event on every single API request. Follow the documented step 3 and set `pinningEnforced = true` first, and it is `completionHandler(.cancelAuthenticationChallenge, nil)` (line 93) on every request: no scans, no listings, no token mint, for every user, unfixable without an App Store review cycle. That is the exact outage the two-stage rollout and the "backup" pin exist to prevent, and the backup pin is the one that cannot fire.

**Fix.** Add the P-384 branch before the fallback (SPKI header for id-ecPublicKey + secp384r1 over a 97-byte point):

```swift
} else if type == (kSecAttrKeyTypeECSECPrimeRandom as String), size == 384 {
    header = [0x30, 0x76, 0x30, 0x10, 0x06, 0x07, 0x2a, 0x86, 0x48, 0xce, 0x3d,
              0x02, 0x01, 0x06, 0x05, 0x2b, 0x81, 0x04, 0x00, 0x22, 0x03, 0x62,
              0x00]
}
```

And stop the silent degradation on line 138-140: log `.fault` ("unsupported pinned key shape: \(type)/\(size)") before returning, so an unhashable key is distinguishable from a mismatch in the telemetry that gates the enforcement flip. Then re-verify all four Config.swift:48-53 hashes against the live chain with the documented `openssl` command before flipping `pinningEnforced`, and add a unit test that feeds a real ISRG Root X2 DER through `matchesPin` and asserts it hits the pin.

### **[FIXED]** The checked continuation can be resumed twice on the Vision failure path

`ios/SnapWorth/Services/PriceTagOCR.swift:61`

Two independent paths resume the same continuation with no guard: the request completion handler at line 47 (`continuation.resume(throwing: error)`) and the `catch` around `handler.perform` at line 61. They are not mutually exclusive — Vision reports a failed request through both channels: the per-request completionHandler is invoked with the request's error *and* `VNImageRequestHandler.perform(_:)` throws that same error. The completion handler runs synchronously inside `perform`, so the ordering is handler-first, then the throw, then a second resume of an already-resumed `CheckedContinuation` — which traps with `SWIFT TASK CONTINUATION MISUSE: tried to resume its continuation more than once` rather than returning an error.

**What happens.** Any Vision request failure on a tag photo — an unsupported/odd colour space in the CGImage, a memory-pressure abort on a 12MP capture, or a revision/option error — crashes the app at line 61 instead of surfacing `OCRError` and letting `readPriceTag` (ThriftFlipViewModel.swift:119-120) show "Couldn't read the tag — enter the price manually." The manual-entry fallback the whole design rests on is unreachable on the one path it exists for.

**Fix.** Make resumption single-shot: capture a `let box = ResumeOnce(continuation)` (a small final class holding an `isResumed` flag, or an `os_unfair_lock`/`NSLock`-protected Bool) and route both line 47/53 and line 61 through it, ignoring the second call. The simplest correct shape is to only resume from the completion handler and, in the `catch`, resume only if the handler never ran.

### **[FIXED]** `strong` is derived from the parsed value instead of the token, so whole-cent prices like "19.00" are demoted to bare integers

`ios/SnapWorth/Services/PriceTagOCR.swift:102`

Line 102 computes `strong: hasSymbol || !isInteger(value)`, and `isInteger` (line 106) inspects the *numeric value*. The doc at lines 67-69 defines strong as "it had a currency symbol or a decimal fraction" — a property of the token text. The two disagree whenever the fraction is zero: the token "19.00" carries an explicit two-digit fraction (an unambiguous price signal) but parses to the integer 19, so it is classified weak and loses `best(of:)` (line 85-89) to any larger bare integer on the same line.

**What happens.** A jeans tag line that Vision returns as one observation, "W32 L34 19.00": the regex yields tokens "32", "34", "19.00" (verified), all classified weak, so `best` falls to `candidates.map(\.value).max()` == 34 and `firstPrice` returns 34 instead of 19. Waist/length numbers (30-44) routinely exceed thrift prices (5-20), so the size wins whenever the price is printed without a currency symbol and with .00 cents.

**Fix.** Compute strength from the matched text, not the value: add a capture group for the optional `[.,]\d{1,2}` tail in the regex at line 94 (or test the token with `#"[.,]\d{1,2}$"#`) and set `strong = hasSymbol || tokenHasFractionTail`. Test the `[.,]\d{1,2}$` form rather than "contains a separator" so that a grouped token like "1.299"/"1,299" is still treated as an integer price after the line-126 fix.

### **[FIXED]** Clearing history leaves the Live Activity showing the total of deleted scans

`ios/SnapWorth/Services/ScanRepository.swift:48`

`deleteAll` writes the widget blob directly with `WidgetDataStore.writeHaul(results: [])` and does not call `scheduleWidgetSync()`, which is the only path to `ThriftRunController.update` (line 127). `save` (line 26) and `delete` (line 37) both call `scheduleWidgetSync()`; `deleteAll` is the one mutation that bypasses it. The controller also has no invalidation entry point other than `update(results:)`, so a caller that does not route through the repository's sync has no way to tell a live run its data is gone.

**What happens.** User starts a run and scans three items totalling $120–$260, then goes to Settings and confirms "Clear history?" → "Delete all" (ios/SnapWorth/Views/SettingsView.swift:194). The SwiftData rows are deleted and the Home Screen widgets drop to zero, but the Lock Screen Live Activity keeps reading "$120–$260 / 3 finds this trip" for records that no longer exist — indefinitely, until the user happens to scan one more item or manually ends the run.

**Fix.** Call `scheduleWidgetSync()` from `deleteAll` instead of writing the blob inline, so the single hook that keeps widgets and the run in step (per the comment on lines 124-127) covers this path too. If the intent is that clearing history also ends the trip, `await ThriftRunController.end()` is the stronger choice.

### On an in-memory fallback launch, save() reports success for writes that are discarded at quit

`ios/SnapWorth/Services/ScanRepository.swift:14`

When the on-disk store fails to open, SnapWorthApp.swift:53-68 substitutes an `isStoredInMemoryOnly: true` container and records the reason in `AppLaunchState`. `ScanRepository.save` (line 14) then runs against that container, `context.save()` succeeds, and nothing on the write path consults `AppLaunchState.isRunningOnFallbackStore`. Grepping the whole iOS target, that flag is read only by SnapWorthApp.swift:29 — to fire one analytics event — and by ProductionHardeningTests.swift:1032/1038. No shipping surface reads it, so the session behaves exactly like a healthy one while every write is volatile.

**What happens.** The store is unreadable (corrupt store, a migration SwiftData refuses, or `NSFileWriteOutOfSpaceError` at open). The user scans an item: the server charges a quota unit, ScanViewModel.swift:91-95 increments `FreeScanCounter`, `try repository.save(result)` succeeds against the in-memory store so `saveFailed` stays false, and ResultView's footer (ResultView.swift:1200-1204, `didSave` defaults to true) tells the user the find was added to My Finds. It shows in History for the rest of the session, then is gone on the next launch — a scan the user paid for, positively claimed as saved. They also see an apparently empty library with no explanation of why.

**Fix.** Make durability visible at the boundary. In `ScanRepository.save`, treat a fallback store as a non-durable write — e.g. `if AppLaunchState.isRunningOnFallbackStore { scheduleWidgetSync(); throw AppError.persistence }`, or add a distinct case so the sheet can say "couldn't be saved to this device" — and render a persistent banner in RootView/HistoryView while `AppLaunchState.isRunningOnFallbackStore` is true. The flag already exists and is already documented as being for exactly this (AppLaunchState.swift:26-28); it is simply unread.

### The per-device identifier sent with every event is an unsalted SHA-256 of the IDFV, while the code comment and the shipped in-app privacy policy both say "salted"

`ios/SnapWorth/Services/TelemetryDeckAnalytics.swift:17`

`TelemetryDeck.Config(appID: appID)` at TelemetryDeckAnalytics.swift:17 omits the `salt:` argument. The SDK's initializer is `public init(appID: String, salt: String? = nil, ...)` and defaults it to the empty string (TelemetryClient.swift:194, 205-209). Every signal's user identifier is then computed as `CryptoHashing.sha256(string: customUserID ?? defaultUserIdentifier, salt: configuration.salt)` (SignalManager.swift:178) where `defaultUserIdentifier` is `UIDevice.current.identifierForVendor?.uuidString` (SignalManager.swift:398-402) — i.e. a plain, unsalted `sha256(IDFV)`. The SDK's own documentation for the property is explicit about why it exists: "Set the salt to a random string of 64 letters, integers and special characters to prevent the unlikely possibility of uncovering the original user identifiers through calculation" (TelemetryClient.swift:33-36). Two shipped statements assert the opposite of what the code does: TelemetryDeckAnalytics.swift:8-9 ("a one-way **salted** hash as the anonymous user identifier") and — the one that matters — the in-app privacy policy at LegalView.swift:45, "A one-way salted hash is used as an anonymous identifier." `Config.swift:100` has `telemetryDeckAppID` and no salt constant, so this was never configured, not mis-configured.

**What happens.** Any event at all, starting with `app_opened` at TelemetryDeckAnalytics.swift:46, ships `clientUser = sha256(IDFV)` with no salt. Because the transform is a globally fixed function with no app-specific secret, anyone who obtains a device's IDFV can compute that device's exact `clientUser` string and single out its entire analytics history on the TelemetryDeck side. IDFV is shared by every app published under the same team identifier, and it is trivially printable from any debug or support build — the audited support flow already declined to include it (AUDIT-2026-09-11 E-4 asserts the diagnostics block never contains `identifierForVendor`), which is exactly the linkage this salt is supposed to make impossible even if it leaked. With a 64-char salt the hash is not reproducible off-device at all. Separately, the assertion in LegalView.swift:45 is factually false in the shipped binary — the same Art. 13 accuracy class as I-18.

**Fix.** Add a fixed random 64-character constant (e.g. `Config.telemetryDeckSalt`) and pass it: `TelemetryDeck.Config(appID: appID, salt: Config.telemetryDeckSalt)`. It must never change afterwards — TelemetryClient.swift:38-39 warns that rotating it makes every existing user look new. Note the salt ships inside the binary, so it defeats an outside party holding an IDFV, not someone who reverse-engineers the app; if that trade is not wanted, the alternative fix is to correct both claims instead — TelemetryDeckAnalytics.swift:8-9 and LegalView.swift:45 — to say "an unsalted one-way hash of the device's vendor identifier". What is not acceptable is shipping the current pairing of empty salt and "salted hash" policy text.

### Every event also carries six accessibility settings and per-device retention counters — not in the comment's inventory, not in the in-app privacy policy, not covered by any declared data type in PrivacyInfo.xcprivacy

`ios/SnapWorth/Services/TelemetryDeckAnalytics.swift:8`

TelemetryDeckAnalytics.swift:7-9 states the SDK adds "its own default context (app/OS version, device model, locale)" and nothing else. In the pinned 2.14.1 (Package.resolved, revision ad4a03e), `DefaultSignalPayload.parameters` attaches considerably more to *every* signal. Signal.swift:104 merges `accessibilityParameters` (Signal+Helpers.swift:55-72): `TelemetryDeck.Accessibility.isReduceMotionEnabled`, `isBoldTextEnabled`, `isInvertColorsEnabled`, `isDarkerSystemColorsEnabled`, `isReduceTransparencyEnabled`, `shouldDifferentiateWithoutColor`, and `preferredContentSizeCategory`. Signal.swift:116-130 attaches `TelemetryDeck.Acquisition.firstSessionDate`, `Retention.averageSessionSeconds`, `Retention.distinctDaysUsed`, `Retention.distinctDaysUsedLastMonth`, `Retention.totalSessionsCount` and `Retention.previousSessionSeconds`. Signal.swift:73-83 adds `Device.timeZone`, `Device.screenResolutionWidth`/`Height`, `Device.screenScaleFactor`, `Device.architecture` and `Device.orientation`. None of this is gated by the app: `config.sessionStatsEnabled` (TelemetryClient.swift:169) gates only `SessionManager.startNewSession()` at TelemetryClient.swift:76, not the payload block at Signal.swift:116, and there is no flag at all for the accessibility block. LegalView.swift:45 — the policy the paywall links to — enumerates collection as "in-app events … along with your device model, operating system version, app version, and locale" and asserts the data "is not linked to your identity"; `ios/SnapWorth/PrivacyInfo.xcprivacy` declares PhotosOrVideos, DeviceID, ProductInteraction, CrashData, PerformanceData and OtherDiagnosticData, none of which covers assistive-technology settings. (For the record, the `AnalyticsEvent` taxonomy itself is clean under this lens: I traced all 30 cases and all 48 `track` call sites and no parameter carries a price, an item name, a raw error string or a user identifier — `ItemCategory(normalizing:)` closes the backend's free-form category, `AppLaunchState.classify` and `DiagnosticSummary.terminationBucket`/`signalName`/`durationBucket` are whitelist buckets with fixed fallbacks, and `ScanStreak.bucket` never emits the exact count. The exposure is entirely in the SDK's default payload.)

**What happens.** A user who has never touched Settings opens the app: `app_opened` (TelemetryDeckAnalytics.swift:46) transmits, alongside the unsalted `sha256(IDFV)`, that this device has VoiceOver-adjacent settings on — Reduce Motion, Invert Colors, Darker System Colors, Differentiate Without Color, and an accessibility text size such as `AccessibilityXXXL` — together with the date of their first session, total session count, and distinct days used. The accessibility flags are disability-adjacent data the policy never mentions and the privacy manifest never declares; the tuple (hashed IDFV + firstSessionDate + timeZone + screenResolution + six a11y flags + distinctDaysUsed) is a high-entropy device fingerprint, which is what makes "is not linked to your identity" at LegalView.swift:45 hard to defend. There is also an existing test asserting the policy discloses collection (`ProductionHardeningTests.swift:267 test_analyticsAndDeviceCheckCollectionAreDisclosed`) that passes while the enumeration is incomplete.

**Fix.** Two workable options. (a) Strip them at the boundary: in `AnalyticsEvent.parameters` (Analytics.swift:127) inject `"TelemetryDeck.Accessibility.*"` and `"TelemetryDeck.Retention.*"` keys with empty values on every event — event parameters are applied last, `defaultParameters.applying(enrichedMetadata).applying(parameters)` at SignalManager.swift:167-171, and TelemetryDeck.swift:47-51 preserves keys already prefixed `TelemetryDeck.`, so they do override the defaults (the reserved-prefix check at TelemetryDeck.swift:55-67 only logs, it does not drop). (b) Disclose them: extend the LegalView.swift:45 enumeration to name the accessibility settings, the retention counters, time zone and screen resolution, and add the missing `NSPrivacyCollectedDataType` entry to PrivacyInfo.xcprivacy. Whichever is chosen, correct the inventory in the TelemetryDeckAnalytics.swift:7-9 comment so the next reader is not told the payload is smaller than it is, and pin the assertion with a test over `DefaultSignalPayload.parameters`' key set so an SDK bump that adds another field fails CI instead of shipping silently.

### The eight-hour cap is unreachable: it is only evaluated when a scan is written, and equals the system's own limit

`ios/SnapWorth/Services/ThriftRunController.swift:74`

The cap guard lives inside `update()`, and `update()` has exactly one caller in the codebase — `ScanRepository.scheduleWidgetSync()` at ios/SnapWorth/Services/ScanRepository.swift:127, reached only from `save`, `delete` and `refreshWidget`. Nothing checks the cap on launch, on `scenePhase == .active` (SnapWorthApp.swift:79 drains widget actions only), or in `ScanView.onAppear`. So the cap requires a scan mutation to fire. Separately, `maximumRunDuration` is 8 hours (line 18) — the same as iOS's own maximum Live Activity lifetime — so even on the mutation path the guard can only evaluate true after the system has already ended the Activity, at which point (see the `activityState` finding) `end()` is called on an ended Activity and does nothing.

**What happens.** The motivating case in the comment on lines 16-18 is a user who "put the phone in a pocket and went home" — i.e. no further scan. Start a run at 4:00pm with three finds, leave the shop at 4:40pm, never scan or reopen the app. `update()` is never called again, so `maximumRunDuration` is never compared to anything. The Activity is then governed solely by the system: ended at midnight, removed from the Lock Screen up to four hours after that. It is still there at 3:00am claiming to be live, which is what these lines exist to prevent.

**Fix.** Add a `static func endIfExpired(now: Date = Date()) async` that ends any run older than `maximumRunDuration`, and call it from `ScanView.onAppear` and from an `onChange(of: scenePhase)` where `phase == .active`. Set `maximumRunDuration` strictly below the system lifetime (e.g. 6 hours) so the app's own cap can actually fire first.

### Widget claims "No flips sold yet this month" for a month whose sales have no cost basis

`ios/SnapWorth/Services/WidgetDataStore.swift:440`

`monthProfits` only collects items whose `realizedProfit` is non-nil (lines 415-421), and `realizedProfit` is nil whenever `paidPrice` was never entered (ScanResult.swift:296-299). `monthProfit` is then written as nil when that array is empty (line 440), which makes "sold nothing this month" and "sold things but never recorded what you paid" indistinguishable in the blob. `MonthProfitView` resolves that single nil to a definite claim: "No flips sold yet this month" (ios/SnapWorthWidgets/MoreWidgets.swift:251 and 259). The `paidPrice` field is optional and its only advertised benefit in the UI is the share-card multiple (ResultView.swift:345), so skipping it is the common path, not an edge case.

**What happens.** A Pro user marks 2 items sold in the current month and never entered a paid price for either. `monthProfits` is empty, so the blob gets `monthProfit: nil` and `monthFlips: 0`, and the widget shows "—" captioned "No flips sold yet this month" while the Flips ledger on the same data shows "2 items sold" (FlipsView.swift:96). The widget states as fact something the app contradicts.

**Fix.** Write the month's sold count independently of cost basis — e.g. add `monthSold: Int` counting `status == .sold && monthInterval.contains(soldDate)` — and have `MonthProfitView` use it to pick between three captions: a genuinely empty month, "N sold · add what you paid" when `monthSold > 0` but the profit is unknown, and "from N flips" when profit is known. Keep `monthFlips` as the count that actually contributed to the sum, per the existing comment at lines 411-413.

### Press is consumed and destroyed when onboarding is incomplete — no observer exists yet

`ios/SnapWorth/SnapWorthApp.swift:114`

`takePendingAction()` at line 114 clears the key as it reads and then line 116 posts `.snapWidgetOpenScan` synchronously. The `.task` at line 78 is attached to `RootView`, which is present in *both* branches of its own `Group` (line 164-176), so the drain runs even while `hasCompletedOnboarding == false` and `OnboardingView` is on screen. The only subscriber to `.snapWidgetOpenScan` is on MainTabView (MainTabView.swift:69), which is not in the hierarchy in that branch, so `NotificationCenter.post` has zero observers. The request is gone and nothing replays it when MainTabView is later mounted at line 173.

**What happens.** Fresh install (or reinstall — `hasCompletedOnboarding` is plain `@AppStorage`, wiped with the app). The user adds the Scan control from the Control Center gallery or assigns it to the Action Button without having finished onboarding, then presses it. The app opens on OnboardingView, line 114 consumes the pending `.scan`, line 116 posts into the void, and after they tap through onboarding they land on the Scan tab only because `selectedTab`'s default is 0 — the actual request was destroyed. Identical loss for a press taken while the user is mid-onboarding.

**Fix.** Do not consume a request there is no one to serve: `guard hasCompletedOnboarding else { return }` before the drain (the App-level `@AppStorage` at line 10 is already declared and currently unused), and call `drainPendingWidgetAction()` again from `onChange(of: hasCompletedOnboarding)` when it flips true. Alternatively hold the drained action in an `@Observable` router that MainTabView consumes on its first appearance, so the value waits for a consumer instead of being broadcast to nobody.

### seedWidgetData overwrites the widget's last good haul with zeros on a fallback launch

`ios/SnapWorth/SnapWorthApp.swift:145`

`seedWidgetData` guards only against a *throwing* fetch (`guard let results = try? ctx.fetch(...)`). On an in-memory fallback container the fetch does not throw — it succeeds and returns `[]` — so line 146 calls `WidgetDataStore.writeHaul(results: [])`, and `writeHaul` (Services/WidgetDataStore.swift:606-683) rewrites the entire `WidgetHaulData` blob in the app group: `totalLow/totalHigh: 0`, `itemCount: 0`, `lastItemName: ""`, `recentFinds: []`, `monthProfit: nil`, `monthFlips: 0`, then `WidgetCenter.shared.reloadAllTimelines()`. That blob is the only copy of the haul summary outside the SwiftData store, i.e. the only representation still standing while the store is unreadable, and it is destroyed on launch — before the app has any idea whether the store will ever open again.

**What happens.** Store fails to open on launch → fallback container → `.task { seedWidgetData() }` (line 77) runs → the Home Screen haul widget, the recent-finds widget and the month-profit widget all flip to an empty library, and the last-known totals are unrecoverable if the corruption is permanent. Ordering makes it worse on a transient failure (e.g. a full disk at open time): the widget was showing correct data a moment earlier and is wiped for a condition that clears itself on the next launch.

**Fix.** Skip the seed when the store is not the real one: `guard !AppLaunchState.isRunningOnFallbackStore else { return }` as the first line of `seedWidgetData` (SnapWorthApp.swift:144). Leaving the previous blob in place shows stale-but-true data instead of false zeros. The same guard belongs in `ScanRepository.scheduleWidgetSync` (ScanRepository.swift:122) so a fallback session's single in-memory scan does not replace the blob either.

### portfolioTotal bypasses the denormalised portfolioValue column, running two JSONDecoder decodes per item per render

`ios/SnapWorth/ViewModels/HistoryViewModel.swift:51`

`portfolioTotal` maps `$0.priceRange(for: $0.condition).likely`. That is the one money accessor in the app that does *not* read the denormalised column, and it is the expensive path twice over:

1. `$0.condition` (ScanResult.swift:187) is `conditionRaw.flatMap(...) ?? baselineCondition`. For any row where the user never picked a condition — the default for every scan — the `??` autoclosure fires `baselineCondition` (ScanResult.swift:175-181), which reads `valuationDetail`, which is `ValuationDetail.decode(valuationDetailData)` (ScanAPIClient.swift:251-255): a **freshly allocated `JSONDecoder` plus a full decode, uncached, on every access**.
2. `priceRange(for:)` (ScanResult.swift:197) then unconditionally evaluates `baselineCondition` again — a second allocation and decode — before two `Decimal` multiplies and a divide.

For rows written before the valuation-detail feature (`valuationDetailData == nil`, i.e. the entire legacy library) each of those two calls instead runs `Condition.inferred(from: conditionNotes)` (ScanResult.swift:410-425): `clauses()` does 11 sequential `components(separatedBy:)` passes over the notes, then up to 15 negation-scoped substring searches.
