Every other consumer already avoids all of this by reading `\.portfolioValue` (ScanResult.swift:233-238), which returns the stored `Decimal` when present: `insights` (lines 100, 102), `trendPoints` (line 175), `NotificationManager.digest` (NotificationManager.swift:395), and `MigrationTests.swift:673`. `ScanRepository.swift:69-71` states this optimisation as already in force — "`portfolioValue` reads the denormalised `portfolioValueRaw` column when present, so a touched library sums plain Decimals instead of running a condition-adjusted division per item per render" — and `portfolioTotal` is the one call site that doesn't. (Distinct from the previously-noted comparator cost in `sorted()`/`filtered()` at HistoryView.swift:27; this is the banner total, a different code path and a different fix.)

**What happens.** With a 1,000-item library, one body evaluation of HistoryView does 1,000-2,000 `JSONDecoder` allocations + decodes on the main actor just to render line 59's headline. Body re-evaluates on **every keystroke** in the search TextField (line 97 binds `vm.searchText`, an `@Observable` property read inside `body`), so typing a five-letter brand name does that work five times over, on top of the two `filtered()` passes, the `trendPoints` full sort, and `insights`' per-item `Calendar.current.dateComponents` (line 106). Visible as dropped frames and laggy text entry that scales with library size — the exact users this feature exists to celebrate.

**Fix.** Make line 51 read the column every other consumer reads: `Self.total(of: results.map(\.portfolioValue))`. Numerically equivalent (`refreshPortfolioValue`, ScanResult.swift:247-262, stores exactly `priceRange(for: condition).likely`, and rows with a nil column fall back to the same computation), it removes the per-render decode entirely for touched rows, and it makes the banner share one source of truth with the digest, the widget haul and the sparkline. Combine with the `.sold` filter from the first finding.

### An in-flight /listing response is applied to whichever marketplace is selected when it lands

`ios/SnapWorth/ViewModels/ResultViewModel.swift:89`

`generateListing` (line 79) reads `selectedMarketplace` at call time, passes it to `ListingAPIClient.generate`, and writes the result back unconditionally at line 89 (`generatedListing = listing`). `selectMarketplace` (lines 68-74) changes `selectedMarketplace` and clears `generatedListing`, but never cancels the in-flight request — there is no stored task — and the chips are not disabled while generating (`ResultView.swift:1036-1053`: the Button whose action is `vm.selectMarketplace(marketplace)` at line 1039 carries no `.disabled`). `GeneratedListing` already carries the marketplace it was built for (`ListingService.swift:92`, set at `:205`) precisely so this can be checked, and nothing checks it. `ListingAPIClient` keeps no generation token either, so the staleness cannot be caught service-side.

**What happens.** A Pro user taps "Generate eBay listing", then taps the Vinted chip during the 2-5 s round trip (the guard at line 80 blocks a second generate, so the UI just keeps showing "Writing your listing…"). The eBay response lands and is rendered under the highlighted Vinted chip: eBay-tailored title and description — the backend writes per-marketplace copy from `MARKETPLACE_GUIDANCE` at `backend/main.py:710-735`, eBay being keyword-front-loaded and formal against Vinted's casual style — plus the eBay ask and floor in `ResultView.swift:1107-1108`, an "Open eBay" button at `ResultView.swift:1123`, and `Analytics.track(.listingGenerated(marketplace: "ebay"))`. The user taps Copy and pastes eBay copy and eBay pricing into Vinted, whose fee structure differs (`MarketplaceFees.swift`). This is exactly the state the comment at ResultViewModel.swift:66-70 says must never occur ("so the user never sees eBay copy under a Vinted tab").

**Fix.** Hold the request in a cancellable task (`private var listingTask: Task<Void, Never>?`), cancel it in `selectMarketplace` before changing `selectedMarketplace`, and add a staleness guard before the assignment at line 89: `guard listing.marketplace == selectedMarketplace else { return }` — likewise before the `listingError` assignment at line 97, so a failed eBay request does not surface an error under the Vinted tab.

### restorePurchases has no re-entrancy guard and its isRestoring flag is never read, so Restore is a silent multi-second no-feedback tap

`ios/SnapWorth/ViewModels/SettingsViewModel.swift:10`

`isRestoring` is set at line 11 and cleared at line 12 but is read nowhere in the app (grep: only PaywallViewModel's separate copy is consumed, at PaywallView.swift:165-170). The Settings row that triggers it (SettingsView.swift:42-44) is a plain `SettingsRow` with no loading state and no `.disabled`, and `restorePurchases` itself has no `guard !isRestoring else { return }` — contrast PaywallViewModel.swift:55, which guards its purchase path. The underlying work is `AppStore.sync()` (StoreKitPurchaseService.swift:98), which can take seconds and can present a system App Store sign-in sheet.

**What happens.** User taps 'Restore purchases' on a slow network: no spinner, no disabled row, no alert until `AppStore.sync()` returns, so the tap looks ignored. Tapping again (the natural response) starts a second concurrent `AppStore.sync()` — two overlapping restores and potentially two sign-in prompts — and whichever finishes last overwrites `restoreMessage`, so the alert can report the outcome of the first attempt after the second has already changed the entitlement.

**Fix.** Add `guard !isRestoring else { return }` as the first line of `restorePurchases` (line 11), and consume the flag in the view: give the Restore row an `isLoading: vm.isRestoring` treatment and `.disabled(vm.isRestoring)`, the pattern already used at PaywallView.swift:167-170.

### OCR confirmation note shows a rounded price that contradicts the value it just wrote into the field

`ios/SnapWorth/ViewModels/ThriftFlipViewModel.swift:117`

`readPriceTag` writes the exact price into the editable field with `moneyField(price)` (line 116, which keeps cents: "8.99") and then, on line 117, asks the user to verify it using `Self.money(price)` — the whole-dollar formatter. The note exists for exactly one purpose ("tap to correct if it's off"), and it is the only sentence the user compares against the physical tag, so it is the one string that must not be rounded.

**What happens.** Scan a tag reading $8.99. The Shop price field correctly becomes 8.99, but the note underneath reads "Read $9 — tap to correct if it's off." The user compares "$9" with the $8.99 tag, concludes the OCR misread it, and retypes — or accepts $9 believing that is what was stored, and gets a verdict computed from 8.99 that matches neither.

**Fix.** Echo the value actually in the field instead of re-formatting the Decimal: `ocrNote = "Read $\(shelfPriceText) — tap to correct if it's off."`, or use the cents-capable formatter from the previous finding.

### An in-flight price-tag OCR writes the previous item's shelf price onto the next item after "New item"

`ios/SnapWorth/ViewModels/ThriftFlipViewModel.swift:116`

`readPriceTag` is launched as an unstructured `Task` from the view (ThriftFlipView.swift:458) and is never cancelled or generation-checked; on completion it unconditionally assigns `shelfPriceText` (line 116) and `ocrNote` (line 117). `reset()` (lines 165-175) clears `shelfPriceText`, `ocrNote` and `scanResult` but neither cancels that task nor invalidates its write, and the "New item" toolbar button (ThriftFlipView.swift:49) is enabled the whole time the OCR spinner is up. So a late OCR completion repopulates the purchase price after the form was cleared, and `calculation` (line 127-137) then becomes non-nil the instant the next scan seeds `resalePriceText` (line 74).

**What happens.** Tap "Scan tag", shoot the tag, then tap "New item" while the spinner is still showing (accurate-level Vision OCR on a full-resolution photo takes a few hundred ms to over a second). Item A's tag price lands in `shelfPriceText`. Scan item B: a full green/red verdict appears immediately, computed from item A's shop price, with "Read $X — tap to correct if it's off" under a field the user never filled in. If it reads profitable and they tap "Save to My Flips", item B's ledger row is persisted with item A's `paidPrice`.

**Fix.** Guard the write with a generation token: keep a `private var ocrGeneration = 0`, increment it in `reset()` (and at the start of `readPriceTag`), capture it locally in `readPriceTag`, and apply `shelfPriceText`/`ocrNote` only if the captured value still matches. Alternatively hold the `Task` and `cancel()` it in `reset()`, then check `Task.isCancelled` before assigning.

### saveToLedger carries only the paid price forward, so the ledger's realized profit silently ignores the fees and shipping the verdict was based on

`ios/SnapWorth/ViewModels/ThriftFlipViewModel.swift:152`

`saveToLedger` (lines 150-163) writes `result.paidPrice` and `result.status = .owned` and nothing else, even though the calculation in hand has `platformFees` and `shippingCost` as exact Decimals. `ScanResult.feesEstimate` — documented as "Selling/shipping fees the user expects" — stays nil, and `ScanResult.realizedProfit` is `sold - paid - (feesEstimate ?? 0)` (ScanResult.swift:296-298), which is what My Flips (FlipsView.swift:90, FlipsViewModel.swift:92) and the CSV export's Fees/Profit columns (FlipsViewModel.swift:180-181) read. The screen that just told the user "after fees" hands the ledger a fee-blind row.

**What happens.** eBay, shop price $10, expected resale $50, shipping $5. The verdict says net profit $28.38 after $6.63 of fees and $5 shipping. Tap "Save to My Flips", later mark it sold at $50 in ResultView: My Flips reports a realized profit of $40.00 and the CSV Fees column is empty — $11.63 higher than the number the app used to justify the purchase, and the monthly recap/share card totals inherit the same overstatement.

**Fix.** In `saveToLedger`, also persist the fee estimate the verdict used, e.g. `if let calc = calculation { result.feesEstimate = NSDecimalNumber(decimal: calc.platformFees + calc.shippingCost).doubleValue }` before `repository.save(result)` (ResultView's fees field then lets the user correct it at sale time).

### Free-tier row cap is applied to all tracked items, not sold flips, so a free user's realized-profit rows can be hidden entirely

`ios/SnapWorth/Views/FlipsView.swift:57`

Line 57 caps `items` — which is `vm.visibleItems(allResults)`, i.e. owned + listed + sold (FlipsViewModel.swift:34-44) — with `Array(items.prefix(Config.ledgerFreeSoldCap))`. The constant is named `ledgerFreeSoldCap` (Config.swift:95), this file's own doc comment says the free tier sees "the current month + last N sold" (lines 5-6), and the prior audit records the gate as "the only cap is ledgerFreeSoldCap=10 on sold flips" (docs/AUDIT-2026-09-07.md:136). The default sort is `.date`, ordered by `soldDate ?? timestamp` descending (FlipsViewModel.swift:49, 71), so freshly scanned owned items outrank older sales.

**What happens.** A free user who scans and marks 10 items Owned today, with two items sold last week, opens My Flips on the default All/Date view: the ten owned rows fill the cap, both sold rows fall past index 10, and the list shows "Unlock 2 more + all-time totals" instead of the user's own profit rows — even though they have only 2 sold flips against a documented 10-sold allowance. The rows they are being asked to pay for are the ones the free tier is supposed to include.

**Fix.** Cap only sold rows: keep every `status != .sold` row and take `prefix(Config.ledgerFreeSoldCap)` of the sold ones (then re-apply the sort), and compute `hidden` from the sold count alone.

### The monthly profit bars pin their label and money columns to 40pt and 74pt while the text inside scales with Dynamic Type

`ios/SnapWorth/Views/FlipsView.swift:186`

`monthRow` sets `.frame(width: 40, alignment: .leading)` on the month label (line 186, `dmSans(13)`) and `.frame(width: 74, alignment: .trailing)` on the money figure (line 199, `dmSans(12, weight: .semibold)`). Both fonts are anchored to `.body`, so at AX5 they render near 3x — about 40pt and 37pt respectively — inside frames that never move. Neither has `lineLimit` or `minimumScaleFactor`, so the text wraps into the fixed box and clips. `statCard` in the same file (lines 138-139) already applies `lineLimit(1)` + `minimumScaleFactor(0.55)` for this exact reason, and `summaryHeader` does the same at lines 93-94.

**What happens.** Set Larger Text to an accessibility size and open My Flips with a month whose profit is four digits: "+$1,250" is laid into a 74pt column at ~37pt type and breaks into unreadable fragments, and "Jul" truncates inside 40pt. The six-month profit history becomes unreadable at the setting that exists to make it readable.

**Fix.** Drop both fixed widths and let the row lay out with `Spacer()` plus `layoutPriority`, or keep them only below accessibility sizes — `@Environment(\.dynamicTypeSize)` and, when `isAccessibilitySize`, stack label/bar/value vertically. At minimum add `.lineLimit(1).minimumScaleFactor(0.55)` to match `statCard`.

### My Flips' overflow menu has no accessible name — VoiceOver reads the raw symbol, and it is the only route to Export CSV

`ios/SnapWorth/Views/FlipsView.swift:415`

The toolbar `Menu`'s label is a bare `Image(systemName: "ellipsis.circle")` (line 415) with no `.accessibilityLabel`, `.accessibilityValue` or `.accessibilityHint`. VoiceOver falls back to the symbol name, announcing something like "ellipsis circle, button" — not a purpose a user can act on. Behind it are the sort picker (line 403), "Share this month" (line 407) and "Export CSV" (line 411), with no other entry point anywhere in the app. HistoryView.swift:226-228 labels the identical control properly ("Sort", plus value and hint) and also adds `.snapHitTarget()`, so the correct pattern is one file away.

**What happens.** With VoiceOver on, open My Flips and swipe through the navigation bar: the control that holds sorting, the month share card and CSV export announces itself as a symbol name. A VoiceOver user has no way to know CSV export exists.

**Fix.** Add `.accessibilityLabel("More")` (or "Flip options"), `.accessibilityValue(vm.sort.rawValue)` and `.accessibilityHint("Sort, share the month, or export a CSV")` to the `Menu` at line 416, matching HistoryView.swift:226-228.

### Sage as money text on a white card is 3.61:1 — the profit figures, and the control that sells the trend

`ios/SnapWorth/Views/FlipsView.swift:321`

`Color.snapSage` (light 6F8F6B) on `Color.snapCard` (white) measures 3.61:1, below AA's 4.5:1 for every size it is used at: FlipsView.swift:321 (each row's realized profit, `dmSans(15, weight: .bold)` — 15pt bold is under the 18.66pt-bold large-text threshold), HistoryView.swift:586 (the notable-find price range, `dmSans(14, weight: .semibold)`), HistoryView.swift:474 ("Unlock value history", `snapCaption` semibold), and DesignSystem.swift:759-761 (every My Finds card's estimate at `fraunces(16, bold)`). Dark mode is 7.07:1 and fine. AUDIT-2026-09-11's verified-clean note records that the website carries "separate AA-compliant variants (`--terra-text`, `--sage-text`) for text on brand fills"; the app has no `sage-text` equivalent, so the money — the product — is the lowest-contrast text on these cards.

**What happens.** Open My Flips in light mode with sold items: every profit figure in the ledger renders at 3.61:1 on white. Same for the estimate on every card in My Finds and the "Unlock value history" button that is the trend feature's only call to action.

**Fix.** Promote the already-defined `lightHighContrast` sage (4F6E4B, DesignSystem.swift:53) into a `snapSageText` token — it measures 5.73:1 on white — and use it wherever sage is a foreground on card or background. Keep `snapSage` for fills, strokes and the 36pt+ headline figures, where it clears the 3:1 bars that apply.

### searchText survives the library emptying, so the first find after a wipe renders as "No results for …"

`ios/SnapWorth/Views/HistoryView.swift:233`

The search field is hidden when the library empties (`if !results.isEmpty`, line 91), but `vm.searchText` keeps its value — `vm` is `@State` held for the tab's whole lifetime (line 10). The `onChange(of: results.count)` handler at lines 232-234 exists for exactly this cleanup and resets `isEditing`, but not `searchText`. When the library becomes non-empty again, `filteredResults` (line 27 → HistoryViewModel.swift:28-31) filters the new row against the stale query and the grid branch falls to `NoSearchResultsView` at line 124-127 instead of showing the find.

**What happens.** User types "nike" in search, then empties the library (Settings → Delete all, or Edit → delete each remaining find). EmptyFindsView appears and the search field is hidden with "nike" still in `vm.searchText`. They tap "Start scanning" (line 426), scan a Levi's jacket, and return to My Finds: `results.count == 1`, so the banner says "Your finds are worth $…" and "1 item scanned", but the grid below reads **"No results for \"nike\"" / "Try a different item name or brand."** — the find they just made is not on screen. Recoverable only because the reappearing search field shows the stale text.

**Fix.** Clear the query in the handler that already runs for this transition — HistoryView.swift:232-234: `if results.isEmpty { isEditing = false; vm.searchText = "" }`.

### My Finds is a hard two-column grid at every text size, so the estimate truncates at accessibility sizes

`ios/SnapWorth/Views/HistoryView.swift:38`

`fixedColumns` (lines 38-41) uses `GridItem(.fixed(cardWidth))` with `cardWidth = (geo.size.width - hPad * 2 - gridSpacing) / 2` (line 37) — about 173pt on a 390pt iPhone, giving each card a 149pt content box (DesignSystem.swift:739/757 subtract 24). The grid never reflows for Dynamic Type. Inside, `ScanHistoryCard` draws the item name at `lineLimit(2)` and the price range at `.font(.fraunces(16, weight: .bold))` with no `lineLimit` and no `minimumScaleFactor` (DesignSystem.swift:759-761). `fraunces(16)` is anchored to `.body`, so at AX5 it renders near 50pt — "$1,250–$1,800" cannot fit 149pt at any line count and truncates. `ValueRangeView` (DesignSystem.swift:415-416) protects the same data with `minimumScaleFactor(0.6)` + `lineLimit(1)`; this card does not.

**What happens.** Set Larger Text to an accessibility size and open My Finds: each card's estimate — the one number the grid exists to show — is clipped or ellipsised, and two-word item names lose their second line.

**Fix.** Switch to `GridItem(.adaptive(minimum: 160))` so the grid falls to one column when cards cannot fit, or read `@Environment(\.dynamicTypeSize)` and use a single column when `isAccessibilitySize`. Independently, add `.lineLimit(1).minimumScaleFactor(0.6)` to the range at DesignSystem.swift:759 so a clipped price can never ship.

### Changing the condition does not clear the generated listing, leaving an Ask/Floor price computed for the old condition on screen and on the clipboard

`ios/SnapWorth/Views/ResultView.swift:293`

`ListingAPIClient.generate` prices the listing from `result.priceRange(for: condition)` (ios/SnapWorth/Services/ListingService.swift:160) and returns `listingPrice`/`negotiationFloor` derived from it. `conditionChip`'s action (lines 281-300) re-prices the item and refreshes the portfolio value, the widget and the share card, but never touches `vm.generatedListing`, so the generated card keeps rendering the old Ask/Floor (lines 1107-1108) and the old `condition.listingPhrase` wording next to a headline range that has moved. `ResultViewModel.selectMarketplace` (ResultViewModel.swift:71-76) already performs exactly this clear for the analogous case, with the comment "so the user never sees eBay copy under a Vinted tab"; the condition axis was missed. `copyGeneratedListing`/`shareGeneratedListing` put `listing.shareText` — which embeds `Price: $…` — on the clipboard, so the stale figure leaves the app.

**What happens.** Baseline `good`, range $40–$90. The user generates an eBay listing: Ask $65, Floor $40. They then notice the cuffs and tap "Used": the headline drops to $31–$70, but the card still reads Ask $65 / Floor $40 and "good used condition", and tapping Copy pastes "Price: $65" — 33% above the app's own current likely value — straight into the marketplace compose form. Nothing on screen indicates the listing is stale.

**Fix.** In `conditionChip`'s action, clear the generated listing the same way the marketplace switch does: `vm.generatedListing = nil; vm.listingError = nil` (best done as a `vm.conditionChanged()` that both call sites share), so the user is offered a regenerate instead of a price from the previous grade.

### **[FIXED]** The tag re-read moves the estimate without refreshing the widget or the thrift-run Live Activity

`ios/SnapWorth/Views/ResultView.swift:838`

`rescan(withTag:)` calls `result.applySharpened(response)` (line 838), which overwrites `valueLow`/`valueHigh` and calls `refreshPortfolioValue()` (ScanResult.swift:106) — then stops. Nothing calls `ScanRepository.refreshWidget()`, which is what writes `WidgetDataStore.writeHaul` and `ThriftRunController.update` (ScanRepository.swift:118-134). The comment on the condition chip at lines 288-291 asserts that "a condition change is the only way that moves without a row being inserted or deleted" — the tag re-read is a second such path, and ScanResult.swift:95-99 documents it as exactly that ("the tag re-read (#88) was the only path that moved a value and did not record it") while fixing only the portfolio history. `seedWidgetData()` is a root-view `.task` (SnapWorthApp.swift:77), so it re-seeds on cold launch only.

**What happens.** A Pro user mid-thrift-run scans a jacket, gets $40–$60, taps "Add the tag"; the re-read identifies it and returns $180–$240. The result sheet shows the new range, but the home-screen haul widget and the live thrift-run Activity on the Lock Screen keep totalling the item at its pre-re-read value until the user taps a condition chip, scans or deletes another item, or relaunches the app.

**Fix.** Add `ScanRepository(context: modelContext).refreshWidget()` after `result.applySharpened(response)` in the success branch, mirroring line 292. (Better: move the call inside `applySharpened`'s callers' shared path so the next value-mover cannot forget it.)

### **[FIXED]** The tag re-read moves an item's value without calling refreshWidget(), so the widget and Live Activity keep the old total

`ios/SnapWorth/Views/ResultView.swift:838`

`ScanRepository.refreshWidget()` exists precisely for "something other than an insert or a delete changed a value" (ScanRepository.swift:112-120), and the thrift-run commit wires the Live Activity to that same hook for "a scan added, removed, or re-graded" (line 128-132). Two paths change a saved item's value: the condition chip, which does call it (ResultView.swift:292), and the tag re-read at ResultView.swift:838, which does not. `result.applySharpened(response)` replaces `valueLow`/`valueHigh` (ScanResult.swift:87-88) and updates the denormalised portfolio value (ScanResult.swift:106), but nothing tells `WidgetDataStore` or `ThriftRunController`. This is the same omission the comment at ScanResult.swift:95-99 describes for the portfolio — fixed there, still open for the widgets.

**What happens.** A find is estimated at $45–$90. The user taps "Add the tag", photographs the label, and the re-read returns $120–$180. The app shows $120–$180 and the portfolio banner updates; the Home Screen haul widget and the in-progress thrift-run Live Activity keep summing $45–$90 until the next insert or delete. During a thrift run that is the number the user is deciding against, and it is wrong by the entire delta of the re-read — the feature's whole purpose.

**Fix.** Add `ScanRepository(context: modelContext).refreshWidget()` immediately after `result.applySharpened(response)` at ResultView.swift:838, matching the condition-chip path at ResultView.swift:292.

### `isRunOn` is never re-synced on foreground or on activity-state change, so the control lies about a run that has ended

`ios/SnapWorth/Views/ScanView.swift:478`

`isRunOn` (line 24) mirrors the Activity state and is written in exactly two places: `ThriftRunControl.onAppear` (line 478) and after a button tap (line 460). Nothing subscribes to `Activity.activityStateUpdates` or `activityStateUpdates` on the current activity, and `ScanView` does not observe `scenePhase` at all (only `MainTabView.swift:87` and `SnapWorthApp.swift:79` do, for unrelated work). The comment on lines 20-23 asserts the value "is re-read on appear because a run can end while the app is backgrounded (the eight-hour cap)" — but SwiftUI's `onAppear` fires when the view is inserted into the hierarchy, not when the app returns from the background, so the background case it names is precisely the one it does not cover.

**What happens.** User is sitting on the Scan tab and scans an item eight hours into a run. `ThriftRunController.update` hits the cap and calls `end()` (ThriftRunController.swift:75). The tab never disappeared, so line 478 does not re-run and `isRunOn` stays true: the control still reads "End run" for an Activity that no longer exists. The next tap takes the `isRunning` branch (line 451), calls `end()` on nothing, and only flips the label — so the user has to tap the control twice to start their next run, with the first tap appearing to do nothing. Same sequence when the user swipes the Activity off the Lock Screen, or when iOS ends it at its own limit, while the Scan tab stays on screen.

**Fix.** Give `ThriftRunControl` a `.task { for await _ in Activity<ThriftRunAttributes>.activityUpdates { isRunning = ThriftRunController.isRunning } }` (plus the per-activity `activityStateUpdates`), and re-read on `onChange(of: scenePhase)` where `phase == .active`, rather than relying on `onAppear` alone.

### `start()`'s false return is discarded, giving the silently-dead button the surrounding comments claim to have avoided

`ios/SnapWorth/Views/ScanView.swift:453`

`ThriftRunController.start()` is `@discardableResult` and its result is dropped here; line 460 then re-reads `ThriftRunController.isRunning`, which is false, so the label stays "Start a run" and no error is surfaced. The comment on lines 114-116 claims hiding the control behind `isAvailable` handles "exactly that case", but `isAvailable` only wraps `ActivityAuthorizationInfo().areActivitiesEnabled` (ThriftRunController.swift:34). `Activity.request` still throws for the other cases the controller's own comment on lines 51-53 lists — notably `ActivityAuthorizationError.globalMaximumExceeded` (the device is at its system-wide Live Activity limit across all apps) and `.denied` when the user revoked permission in the window between the line 41 check and the line 44 request — and `start()` swallows all of them into `return false`.

**What happens.** A user whose device already holds the system maximum number of Live Activities from other apps taps "Start a run". `Haptics.selection()` fires, `Activity.request` throws, `start()` returns false, line 460 sets `isRunning = false`, the label stays "Start a run", no Activity appears on the Lock Screen, and no message is shown. Tapping again does the same thing forever, with no indication of why.

**Fix.** Have `start()` return the thrown `ActivityAuthorizationError` (or a small enum) instead of `Bool`, and in the button's `Task` surface the failure — reuse `vm.errorMessage` / the existing alert at lines 319-326, and for `.denied` offer the Settings deep link that `permissionPlaceholder` already uses.

### The camera's only instruction is drawn straight onto the live preview with no scrim, unlike every other label in the same chrome

`ios/SnapWorth/Views/ScanView.swift:135`

`Text("Center the item — tags & logos help")` uses `Color.snapOnCharcoal.opacity(0.7)` (line 137) with no `.background` — the composite is 70% cream over whatever the sensor is showing. Every other chrome label in this file sits on a `Color.snapCharcoal.opacity(0.5)` capsule: the free-scan counter (lines 82/86), the streak badge (lines 103/106) and the thrift-run control (lines 466/469). Against a bright frame even those scrimmed badges only reach about 2.9:1; with no scrim at all the instruction has no floor. The viewfinder stroke above it (line 128) is `accessibilityHidden(true)` on the stated grounds that "the instruction below carries the same information" — so this line is load-bearing for both sighted and VoiceOver users.

**What happens.** Point the camera at a bright subject — a white garment on a white rail, a lit shelf, a pale table — and the only framing guidance on the screen vanishes into the preview. That is the normal case for a thrift store under fluorescent light, and it is the first screen every user meets.

**Fix.** Give the line the same treatment as its neighbours: `.padding(.horizontal, 12).padding(.vertical, 6).background(Color.snapCharcoal.opacity(0.5)).clipShape(Capsule())`, or add one bottom-anchored gradient scrim behind the whole control area so the counter, streak and instruction all sit on a known ground.

### 'Rate SnapWorth' calls requestReview(), which iOS silently drops — and ReviewPrompt has usually already spent the quota

`ios/SnapWorth/Views/SettingsView.swift:83`

`requestReview()` (the `\.requestReview` environment action, line 8) is the *ask the system to maybe show a prompt* API: iOS rate-limits it to roughly three prompts per 365 days per app and ignores the call otherwise, with no error and no callback. Apple's own guidance is not to call it in response to a user action for precisely this reason. The app already burns a request automatically: ReviewPrompt.recordSuccessfulScan() calls `AppStore.requestReview(in:)` on the 3rd successful scan of each app version (ReviewPrompt.swift:18, :37), fired from ScanViewModel.swift:122. Config.appStoreURL (Config.swift:76) already holds the product URL needed for the correct behaviour, and `vm.openURL` already exists for exactly this kind of row (used at line 39).

**What happens.** Any user who has completed 3+ successful scans on the installed version — i.e. every engaged user, the only ones who go looking for a Rate row — taps 'Rate SnapWorth' in Settings and absolutely nothing happens: no sheet, no App Store, no message. Tapping again does nothing. The row is dead for the users it is aimed at, and the growth lever ReviewPrompt.swift:4-6 describes is silently lost.

**Fix.** Make the explicit row a deep link instead of a system request: replace `requestReview()` with `vm.openURL("\(Config.appStoreURL)?action=write-review")`, which always opens the App Store review composer. Leave `requestReview`/ReviewPrompt for the automatic, non-user-initiated moment only.

### Settings never observes the purchase service, so the subscription card can keep showing the wrong plan

`ios/SnapWorth/Views/SettingsView.swift:28`

`purchaseService` is held as a plain `let purchaseService: any PurchaseService` (line 6) and its published state is read directly in the body at lines 28 (`SubscriptionCard(isSubscribed:)`), 37 (the `Manage subscription` row) and 50 (`NotificationSettingsView(isPro:)`). StoreKitPurchaseService is an `ObservableObject` with `@Published private(set) var isSubscribed` (StoreKitPurchaseService.swift:9), not `@Observable`, and no view in the app declares `@ObservedObject`/`@EnvironmentObject` (grep over ios/SnapWorth/Views: zero hits). The only subscriber is `@StateObject` in SnapWorthApp.swift:7, which re-evaluates the App body but hands SettingsView an identical view value, so SwiftUI has no registered dependency that would force this body to re-run. In practice the body only re-runs when SettingsView's own state changes — `showPaywall` flipping back on sheet dismissal, `vm.showRestoreAlert`, or the `@Query` at line 9 changing — which is why the buy-from-Settings and Restore paths appear to work and mask this.

**What happens.** Open the Settings tab while on the free tier (card renders 'Free Plan · 1 free scan a day · Upgrade for unlimited'). Switch to the Scan tab, hit the daily free-scan limit, and buy Pro from the paywall there. Switch back to Settings: nothing in SettingsView's own state changed and no new scan was saved, so the card still says 'Free Plan' with an Upgrade button and the 'Manage subscription' row is still absent — a paying subscriber is told they are on Free and re-offered the upgrade. The mirror case is worse: when `refreshEntitlements()` on foreground (MainTabView.swift:94) sees a lapsed subscription, an already-rendered Settings keeps painting 'SnapWorth Pro · Unlimited scans · Active' with no Upgrade button — the stale-Pro-chrome class that refreshEntitlements was added to eliminate.

**Fix.** Give the view a real dependency on the service. Cleanest with the existing existential: convert StoreKitPurchaseService/MockPurchaseService to `@Observable` (drop ObservableObject/@Published and the App's `@StateObject` for `@State`), which makes reads like `purchaseService.isSubscribed` inside `body` register with SwiftUI's observation tracking even through `any PurchaseService`. Otherwise inject the concrete service as an `@EnvironmentObject`/`@ObservedObject` in SettingsView rather than passing it as a plain `let`.

### The badge divides the raw `paidPrice` while the card prints it rounded to whole dollars; a sub-$0.50 paid price prints "Paid $0" beside a two-digit multiple

`ios/SnapWorth/Views/ShareCardView.swift:146`

`fmtCurrency` (ShareCardView.swift:193-195) uses `NumberFormatter.snapCurrency`, which has `maximumFractionDigits = 0` (DesignSystem.swift:249-256), so line 146 prints `paid` rounded to the dollar. `findBadge` (line 185-191) divides the unrounded `paid`. `paidPrice` is genuinely fractional in production: `MoneyInput.parse` (MarketplaceFees.swift:71-73) returns a `Double` from a decimal-pad string and `ResultView.swift:217` stores it verbatim, so "0.50", "1.50", "12.99" all persist as-is. The `paid == 0` guards on lines 146 and 186 test the exact stored value, not the printed one, so any paid price under $0.50 escapes the "Free" branch and is then printed as "$0" (half-even rounding: 0.5 → 0).

**What happens.** Enter $0.50 for a book the model prices at $45–$90. Line 146 renders "Paid $0 →", line 186's `paid == 0` is false so no "Free find", and line 189 computes `round(45/0.5) = 90` → the shared card reads "Paid $0 → / $45–$90 / 90x find": a multiple of a printed zero. Milder but more common: paid $1.50 prints "Paid $2" (half-even) with `round(45/1.5) = 30` → "30x find" next to numbers that read 22x.

**Fix.** Compute the badge from the same values the card prints. In `findBadge`, round first and bail below a dollar: `let paidShown = paid.rounded(); let lowShown = result.displayValueLow.rounded(); if paidShown == 0 { return "Free find" }; guard paidShown < lowShown else { return nil }; let multiple = Int(lowShown / paidShown)`. That also fixes the "Free →" vs "Paid $0 →" split on line 146 if the same rounded value drives the ternary.

### `GuessScoring.verdict` can report "$0 under the low end" — a non-zero miss rounded to zero dollars

`ios/SnapWorth/Views/ShareCardView.swift:371`

`verdict` (lines 366-375) decides "inside" against the unrounded `displayValueLow`/`displayValueHigh`, then formats the distance through `money` (line 391-393), which does `value.rounded()` and then `snapCurrency` with `maximumFractionDigits = 0`. Any miss in (0, 0.5) formats as "$0". The bounds are fractional as a matter of course: `priceRange` multiplies by `condition.priceMultiplier / baselineCondition.priceMultiplier` with factors 1.35 / 1.15 / 1.0 / 0.78 (ScanResult.swift:333-340), so a $45 low re-graded `.used` is 35.1 and re-graded `.likeNew` is 51.75. The verdict is rendered directly beneath `ValueRangeView(low: result.displayValueLow, high: result.displayValueHigh)` (ResultView.swift:663, 708), which prints those same bounds rounded — so the user reads "$35–$70" and is told their $35 guess is "$0 under the low end" of it.

**What happens.** Item priced 45–90 by the model, user re-grades to `.used`: `displayValueLow` = 35.1, `displayValueHigh` = 70.2, and the range renders "$35–$70". Turn on Guess First, type 35, tap Reveal. `35 >= 35.1` is false, so line 371 runs: `money(35.1 - 35)` = `money(0.1.rounded())` = `money(0)` = "$0" → "$0 under the low end." Any guess in (34.6, 35.1) produces it. The symmetric case exists on line 374 for guesses just over a fractional high end.

**Fix.** Score against the bounds as displayed, or treat a sub-dollar miss as a hit. Simplest: in `verdict`, round the bounds before comparing — `let lo = min(low, high).rounded(); let hi = max(low, high).rounded()` — so "inside" matches the printed range exactly and no distance can round to zero. Alternatively keep exact bounds and guard the copy: `let diff = (lo - guess).rounded(); if diff < 1 { return "Spot on — your guess is inside the estimate." }`. `GuessScoring` is documented as pure and testable, so add cases for low = 35.1 / guess = 35 and high = 70.2 / guess = 70.5.

### "Scan tag" is a ~16pt-tall tap target and "Choose from library" ~17pt — no padding, no `snapHitTarget()`

`ios/SnapWorth/Views/ThriftFlipView.swift:205`

The "Scan tag" button's label (lines 207-218) is a bare `HStack` at `dmSans(13, weight: .semibold)` with no padding, no frame and no `.snapHitTarget()`, so its tappable height is the 13pt line box — roughly 16pt against Apple's 44pt minimum. `Button("Choose from library")` at lines 98-100 is the same shape at 14pt, roughly 17pt tall, and it is the only route into Thrift Flip for anyone whose camera is unavailable. Both are in a file whose marketplace chips 20 lines below (line 183) carry `.snapHitTarget()` precisely because "the 9pt vertical padding left a ~31pt target" (AUDIT-2026-09-07 I-25) — 9pt of padding was judged too small, and these two have none.

**What happens.** Load an item in Thrift Flip and try to tap "Scan tag" — the OCR shortcut that is the feature's headline — with a thumb: the strike zone is a 16pt strip, so misses land on the inert "Shop price" header next to it. Same on the empty state's "Choose from library".

**Fix.** Add `.snapHitTarget()` to both buttons (line 218 and line 100). The modifier expands the tappable frame without changing the visual size, which is why it is applied to the chips in the same view.

### The resale and shipping money fields are capped at `maxWidth: 90` where ResultView's identical row uses `minWidth: 90`

`ios/SnapWorth/Views/ThriftFlipView.swift:436`

`labeledMoneyRow` sets `.frame(maxWidth: 90)` on its `TextField` (line 436) at `dmSans(15, weight: .semibold)`. `ResultView.moneyRow`, which this row is explicitly documented as mirroring (comment at lines 399-402), uses `.frame(minWidth: 90)` at line 437 — a floor, so it grows. A `maxWidth` is a ceiling: the font scales with Dynamic Type and the box does not, so a `TextField` scrolls its content horizontally and shows only part of the number.

**What happens.** Set Larger Text to an accessibility size, open Thrift Flip and type 1250 into "Expected resale": at ~46pt type in a 90pt box only the last digit or two are visible, and the user cannot see the figure the profit verdict is computed from. The Shop price field (line 410, unconstrained) behaves correctly, which makes the inconsistency visible on the same card.

**Fix.** Change line 436 to `.frame(minWidth: 90)` to match ResultView.swift:437.

### **[FIXED]** The Thrift Flip verdict — "Worth flipping" / "Skip it" — is 3.12:1 and 2.93:1 against its own tinted card

`ios/SnapWorth/Views/ThriftFlipView.swift:267`

`verdictCard` paints the headline `.foregroundStyle(accent)` (line 267) on `.background(accent.opacity(0.08))` (line 315), where `accent` is `snapSage` or `snapTerracotta` (line 258). Measured on the composite over `snapBackground`: sage 3.12:1, terracotta 2.93:1. The text is `dmSans(17, weight: .bold)` — 17 iOS pt is under the 18.66pt-bold large-text threshold, so 4.5:1 applies; the terracotta "Skip it" case fails even the 3:1 large-text allowance. The `metric` values at line 332 (`dmSans(18, weight: .bold)`, `accent` for net profit) sit on the same ground with the same problem. Dark mode is fine (6.28-6.89:1) — this is a light-mode failure only.

**What happens.** Run any Thrift Flip calculation in light mode: the one-line verdict the feature exists to deliver, and the net-profit figure under it, are the lowest-contrast text on the card they headline. The unprofitable case ("Skip it", terracotta at 2.93:1) is the worse of the two — the verdict that stops someone spending money.

**Fix.** Keep the tinted card but take the foreground from a text-weight variant rather than the accent itself: `lightHighContrast` sage (4F6E4B, DesignSystem.swift:53) measures 5.73:1 on white and the terracotta text value (#A94E29) 6.1:1. The glyph at line 263 already carries the verdict shape-wise, so only the colour needs to move.

### Pending action is written with no timestamp and no expiry, so an undelivered press replays days later

`ios/SnapWorthWidgets/AppIntent.swift:17`

`WidgetBridge.request(.scan)` stores only the raw string (`WidgetDataStore.swift:501-505` / the duplicated copy at `SnapWorthWidgets/SnapWorthWidgets.swift:501-505`): no timestamp, no sequence number. `takePendingAction()` returns whatever is in the App Group regardless of age, and nothing clears the key on `.background` or at launch. Any press whose app-open does not complete leaves a permanent request that the next `.active` transition (SnapWorthApp.swift:83) executes as if it had just happened. The cold-launch ordering makes this worse: with `openAppWhenRun = true` and `OpenScanIntent` compiled only into the widget-extension target (the whole `ios/SnapWorthWidgets` folder is a `PBXFileSystemSynchronizedRootGroup` owned solely by `SnapWorthWidgetsExtension`, project.pbxproj:181, 373-395 — the app binary does not contain the intent, so `perform()` cannot run in the app's process), the write and the app launch happen in two processes with no ordering guarantee; a write that lands after the line-78 drain is left behind rather than acted on.

**What happens.** User presses the Lock Screen Scan control on a locked phone and then walks away without authenticating (or the launch is interrupted). `perform()` has already written `pendingAction = "scan"`; the app never becomes active, so nothing drains it. The next day they open SnapWorth from the Home Screen to check My Flips, tab over to another app and come back — line 83 drains the day-old request and throws them to the Scan tab with the camera starting. The user never asked for a scan in that session.

**Fix.** Make the request self-expiring: write `["action": rawValue, "at": Date.now.timeIntervalSince1970]` and have `takePendingAction()` remove and return nil for anything older than ~10 seconds. Also clear the key on `scenePhase == .background` so a request can never outlive the session that received it. (Switching the control to `OpenURLIntent`, as in the already-active finding, removes the stored-state class of bug entirely.)

### **[FIXED]** Both Haul widget families have no accessibility grouping or label at all, so VoiceOver reads unlabeled money fragments and symbol names

`ios/SnapWorthWidgets/HaulWidget.swift:54`

`HaulWidgetSmallView` (lines 54-90) and `HaulWidgetMediumView` (lines 98-180) contain no `.accessibilityElement`, no `.accessibilityLabel`, and no `.accessibilityHidden` anywhere. Every other widget in the bundle authors one: `LockScreenCircularView` uses `.ignore` + a written label (LockScreenWidgets.swift:83-86) with a comment stating why fragments are unacceptable, `ScansLeftView` does it in both families (MoreWidgets.swift:149-150, 172-173), `MonthProfitView` does it (MoreWidgets.swift:249-250). The consequence: the decorative `camera.viewfinder` at lines 61 and 106 is announced by its symbol name, and the medium widget exposes eight separate elements in visual order — 'camera viewfinder', 'SnapWorth', 'Your haul', '$348–$620', '8 items', 'Last scanned', 'Patagonia Fleece', '$60–$95', 'Scan'. The two money amounts are announced as bare numbers separated from the words that identify them by intervening elements, and 'Last scanned' precedes rather than joins its own value.

**What happens.** VoiceOver user with 8 scans swipes onto the medium Haul Value widget. They must swipe through nine elements, and the two amounts arrive detached from their captions: '$348–$620' with 'Your haul' two swipes earlier, and '$60–$95' with no adjacent statement that it belongs to the Patagonia Fleece. On the small widget, '$348–$620' arrives between 'SnapWorth' and '8 items scanned' with nothing saying it is the haul total. Money correctness is the product, and the numbers are the part that loses its meaning.

**Fix.** Add `.accessibilityElement(children: .ignore)` plus an authored label to each view's root, mirroring MoreWidgets.swift:172-173. Small: `"Your haul is worth \(spokenRange), from \(WidgetHaulData.itemsLabel(haul.itemCount))"`. Medium: same, then `". Last scanned: \(haul.lastItemName), \(spokenLastRange)"`. Mark the header symbols at lines 61 and 106 `.accessibilityHidden(true)`.

### **[FIXED]** wWarmGray on wCharcoal is 3.82:1 — every caption in the widget bundle fails AA at 10-13pt

`ios/SnapWorthWidgets/HaulWidget.swift:85`

`Color.wWarmGray` (`#8A857E`, SnapWorthWidgets.swift:396) on `Color.wCharcoal` (`#2C2C2C`, :391) measures 3.82:1 by direct WCAG calculation. It is the colour of every caption and subtitle in the extension, all at 10-13pt where AA requires 4.5:1: HaulWidget.swift:85 ('8 items scanned'), :118 ('Your haul'), :128 (item count), :142 ('Last scanned'), :159 ('Tap to scan your first find'); MoreWidgets.swift:82 ('Nothing scanned yet'), :168 ('free scans left today' / 'Back tomorrow, or go Pro'), :245 ('from 3 flips' / 'Track profit with Pro'); SnapWorthWidgetsLiveActivity.swift:48 ('Last: …' in the Dynamic Island), :97 ('8 finds this trip'), :114 ('elapsed'). This is specifically the warm grey, not a blanket palette problem — `Color.wSage` (`#7D9E7E`) on the same ground is 4.70:1 and passes, and the header wordmark at `wBackground.opacity(0.7)` is 7.32:1. The widget palette is also a set of fixed hex literals with no `colorSchemeContrast` handling, so Increase Contrast does not improve any of it, unlike the app's `snapAdaptive` tokens (docs/AUDIT-v2.md:14).

**What happens.** Any user with any of these widgets installed. The caption naming what the big number means — 'Your haul', 'free scans left today', 'from 3 flips' — is the sub-4.5:1 element, at 10-12pt, and it does not improve when the user turns on Settings > Accessibility > Display & Text Size > Increase Contrast. A low-vision user reads the amount and cannot read what it counts.

**Fix.** Lighten the token. `#9B968F` keeps the warm hue and measures 4.76:1 on `#2C2C2C`; set `static let wWarmGray = Color(hex: "9B968F")` at SnapWorthWidgets.swift:396 — one edit covers all eleven call sites. If the current value must be kept for the large `MonthProfitView` figure (24pt bold, where 3:1 suffices), add a second `wWarmGrayText` token and use it for the captions only.

### The Lock Screen keeps showing a scanning streak that has already been broken

`ios/SnapWorthWidgets/LockScreenWidgets.swift:102`

`streak` is a snapshot written by the app as `ScanStreak.current()` (WidgetDataStore.swift:328). `ScanStreak.current()` returns 0 once the last scan is older than yesterday (ScanViewModel.swift:214-221), but the widget renders whatever the blob last stored — LockScreenWidgets.swift:102-104 ("N finds · N-day streak"), and the Pro branches at MoreWidgets.swift:163, 177 and 187. `ScanStreak` also lives in `UserDefaults.standard`, so the extension cannot recompute it, and the hourly `.after` policy at LockScreenWidgets.swift:33 schedules nothing at the local midnight where the streak actually lapses.

**What happens.** A user scans on Monday through Friday, reaching a 5-day streak; the Friday write stores `streak: 5`. They neither scan nor open the app over the weekend. Saturday and Sunday the Lock Screen rectangular complication still reads "12 finds · 5-day streak", a streak the app itself would compute as 0 on Sunday. It silently corrects to 0 or 1 whenever the app is next launched.

**Fix.** Store the day the streak was last extended (`ScanStreak`'s `lastKey` date) alongside the count, and treat the streak as 0 in the widget when `now` is more than one calendar day past it. Emit a timeline entry at the next local midnight from `LockScreenProvider`/`HaulOnlyProvider` so the lapse shows up without the app running, instead of relying on the blind hourly tick.

### **[FIXED]** accessoryRectangular combines children with no label, pulling in the symbol name and a meaningless middle dot; accessoryInline has no label at all

`ios/SnapWorthWidgets/LockScreenWidgets.swift:130`

`LockScreenRectangularView` ends with `.accessibilityElement(children: .combine)` at line 130 and no `.accessibilityLabel`, so VoiceOver synthesises the announcement from the children: the `Label("SnapWorth", systemImage: "camera.viewfinder")` at line 106 (symbol name included — it is not hidden), the `formattedRange` at line 113, and the composed string at lines 118-120 whose separator is a bare U+00B7 middle dot, `"\(haul.findsLabel) · \(streak)-day streak"`. Nothing in the result identifies the amount as a haul total. `LockScreenInlineView` has no accessibility treatment at all (lines 50-59): its entire content is `haul.compactRange`, so the element beside the Lock Screen clock announces just '$348–$620'. The sibling `LockScreenCircularView` gets this right at lines 83-86 — `.ignore` plus 'Haul value up to …' — with a comment explaining the reasoning, so two of the three accessory families in the same file were left behind.

**What happens.** User has the rectangular Lock Screen widget, 12 finds, a 5-day live streak, VoiceOver on. Focusing it announces the camera symbol's name, then '$348–$620', then '12 finds' run straight into '5 day streak' with the middle dot contributing nothing at default punctuation verbosity. On the inline family the announcement is two amounts and nothing else — no app, no meaning. Both are the surface whose whole purpose is reading your haul without unlocking.

**Fix.** Replace line 130 with `.accessibilityElement(children: .ignore)` plus an authored label built the way the circular view does it: `"SnapWorth. Haul worth \(spokenRange), \(haul.findsLabel)" + (streak > 1 ? ", \(streak) day streak" : "")`. Keep the '·' for display only. For the inline view add `.accessibilityLabel("SnapWorth haul, \(spokenCompactRange)")` at lines 55 and 57.

### "This month" profit survives the month boundary — September's figure captioned as this month's

`ios/SnapWorthWidgets/MoreWidgets.swift:271`

`monthProfit`/`monthFlips` are computed in the app against `Calendar.current.dateInterval(of: .month, for: Date())` at write time (WidgetDataStore.swift:304-313). The widget then renders them under a header hardcoded to "This month" (MoreWidgets.swift:239) with the caption "from N flips" (line 276) and the spoken "profit this month" (line 284). `HaulOnlyProvider` (line 27) schedules nothing at the month boundary and the extension has no access to the ledger, so the numbers cannot be recomputed or invalidated — they simply persist under a now-false label. Note this is latent behind the `isPro` defect above (today `monthProfit` is always nil); it becomes live the moment entitlement is wired up.

**What happens.** A Pro user sells four flips in September for $214 profit. They don't open the app on 1 October. The "Profit this month" widget continues to show "$214" / "from 4 flips" under the "This month" header for as long as the app stays closed — September's ledger presented as October's.

**Fix.** Write the month the figure belongs to into the blob (a `profitMonthStart: Date`, or just reuse `updatedAt`), and in `HaulOnlyProvider.getTimeline` emit a second entry dated at the start of the next month whose profit is cleared, with `policy: .after(nextMonthStart)` when that is sooner than the hourly tick. In the view, when `updatedAt` is outside the current month, show "—" with "Open SnapWorth to refresh" rather than a figure from a closed month.

### "Recent finds" says "Nothing scanned yet" next to a non-zero haul total after an app update

`ios/SnapWorthWidgets/MoreWidgets.swift:64`

The header branches on `haul.hasScans` and prints `haul.formattedRange` (lines 56-60) while the body branches on `haul.recentFinds.isEmpty` (line 64). Those two can disagree, because `recentFinds` is a v2 key that the hand-written decoder defaults to `[]` for an older blob (SnapWorthWidgets.swift:134) while `itemCount`/`totalLow`/`totalHigh` decode from v1 just fine. That is precisely the situation the model's own comment at SnapWorthWidgets.swift:33-39 says happens routinely: "an update installs the new extension before the user next opens the app". The views never got the corresponding guard.

**What happens.** A user on 1.3.x with 12 scans and a $348–$620 haul updates to 1.4.0 and adds the new "Recent finds" widget without relaunching the app. The blob is still v1, so `recentFinds` decodes to `[]` while `itemCount` is 12. The widget renders "$348 – $620" in its header and "Nothing scanned yet" in its body at the same time. It stays that way until the app is next opened.

**Fix.** Branch on `haul.hasScans` first. When `hasScans && recentFinds.isEmpty`, fall back to the one row a v1 blob does carry — a single row built from `lastItemName`/`lastItemRange` — or show "Open SnapWorth to refresh"; reserve "Nothing scanned yet" for `!hasScans`. Add a decode test that feeds the view model a v1 JSON blob and asserts the two halves of the widget agree.

### A nil freeScansRemaining ("unknown") is rendered as a hard zero, so the widget claims no scans are left

`ios/SnapWorthWidgets/MoreWidgets.swift:164`

`freeScansRemaining` is `Int?` and its documented meaning is three-valued: "`nil` means Pro, or never established" (SnapWorthWidgets.swift:49-50). Every consumer collapses nil to 0 with `?? 0`:
- `headline` line 164: `"\(haul.freeScansRemaining ?? 0)"`
- `subtitle` line 171-173: `left == 0 ? "Back tomorrow, or go Pro" : ...`
- `circularValue` line 178
- `accentForRemaining` line 182 → terracotta, the alarm colour
- `spokenLabel` line 189-191 → "No free scans left today"

nil is reachable. The hand-written decoder deliberately produces it for a blob written by an older build: `freeScansRemaining = try c.decodeIfPresent(Int.self, forKey: .freeScansRemaining)` (SnapWorthWidgets.swift:131) with no default, and the header comment at lines 31-37 says the widget process "routinely reads a blob written by an older build of the app (an update installs the new extension before the user next opens the app)". `isPro` in that same v1 blob decodes to `false` (line 132), so the Pro branch does not shield it — the code falls straight through to the free-tier branch and reports zero.

**What happens.** A free user on 1.3.x updates to 1.4.0. The new extension is installed with the update; the v1 blob in the app group has no `freeScansRemaining` key. They add the "Scans left" widget from the Home Screen gallery — which needs no app launch, so `seedWidgetData()` has not run. The widget renders "0" in terracotta with "Back tomorrow, or go Pro", and VoiceOver says "No free scans left today", while the server would happily grant them today's scan.

**Fix.** Branch on the optional instead of defaulting it. When `haul.freeScansRemaining == nil` and `!haul.isPro`, render the unknown state — "–" for the headline/circular value, "Tap to scan" for the subtitle, `Color.wWarmGray` for the accent, and "Free scans remaining unknown, tap to open SnapWorth" for the spoken label — rather than asserting zero. Zero should only be shown when the writer actually stored 0.

### No text anywhere in the widget extension scales with Dynamic Type — every size is a fixed point value

`ios/SnapWorthWidgets/QuickScanWidget.swift:76`

Every `Text` and `Image` in all eight widget views uses `.font(.system(size:))`, which is a fixed point size and does not respond to the user's text-size setting; scaling requires `.system(size:, relativeTo:)`, a semantic `TextStyle`, or `@ScaledMetric`. The smallest and most affected: QuickScanWidget.swift:76 and :80 (10pt captions), SnapWorthWidgetsLiveActivity.swift:113 ('elapsed', 10pt), and the 11pt tier at HaulWidget.swift:62, :65, :84, :107, :110, :141, :167, MoreWidgets.swift:67, :72, :142, :157, :167, :234, :244, :312, and QuickScanWidget.swift:48. The accessory families are the clearest regression, since the system sizes those for the user and this code overrides it: LockScreenWidgets.swift:73, :75, :107, :114, :121, :124, :126 and MoreWidgets.swift:142, :144. The app itself does the opposite deliberately — its type ramp anchors each alias to a `TextStyle` and `snapSymbol` uses `@ScaledMetric` so icons track growing labels (docs/AUDIT-v2.md:14) — so the widget extension is the one surface that opted out, and no prior audit read it.

**What happens.** User sets Settings > Accessibility > Display & Text Size > Larger Text to any accessibility size (AX1-AX5). Every other element on their Lock Screen and Home Screen grows; all eight SnapWorth widgets render byte-identically to the default setting, leaving 10pt and 11pt captions on the rectangular Lock Screen widget and the Quick Scan tile. The Lock Screen rectangular widget in particular sits next to system widgets that did scale, so the size mismatch is visible side by side.

**Fix.** Change every `.font(.system(size: N, weight: w, design: d))` to `.font(.system(size: N, weight: w, design: d).leading(.tight))` paired with `relativeTo:` — i.e. `.system(size: 11, weight: .medium, design: .default)` becomes `.system(size: 11, weight: .medium, relativeTo: .caption2)` — or use the semantic styles directly (`.caption2`, `.caption`, `.headline`, `.title2`) in the accessory families as Apple's accessory-widget guidance requires. Where growth would clip, keep the existing `.lineLimit` and add `.minimumScaleFactor(0.7)` rather than freezing the size; the captions at HaulWidget.swift:84, :117, :127, :141, MoreWidgets.swift:81, :167, :244 currently have neither.

### **[FIXED]** compactMoney emits the minus sign after the dollar sign — "$-500" for a loss

`ios/SnapWorthWidgets/SnapWorthWidgets.swift:163`

`compactMoney` builds its output by string interpolation with a hardcoded leading `"$"`:
- line 163: `return "$\(Int(dollars))"` → for `dollars == -500`, `"$-500"`
- line 169: `return "$\(String(format: "%.1f", thousands))K"` → for `thousands == -1.5`, `"$-1.5K"`

Negative input is not hypothetical here, it is designed for: `MonthProfitView` feeds `haul.monthProfit` straight into `WidgetHaulData.compactMoney(profit)` (MoreWidgets.swift:263 and again at 284 for the VoiceOver string) and explicitly colours the result terracotta when `profit < 0` (MoreWidgets.swift:268). The source value can be negative — `ScanResult.realizedProfit` is `soldPrice - paidPrice - fees` (ScanResult.swift:298-299) with no floor, and `WidgetDataStore.swift:305-313` sums those without clamping.

The sibling formatter in the same type gets this right: `money()` (line 82-85) goes through `NumberFormatter` with `numberStyle = .currency`, which produces "-$500". So the bundle renders the same loss two different ways depending on which widget you look at. Note the block is byte-checked against `ios/SnapWorth/Services/WidgetDataStore.swift` (lines 163-172 there) by the "Widget model is in sync" CI step, so the fix must land in both copies identically.

**What happens.** A Pro user buys a jacket for $80, sells it for $45 with $12 of fees, and has no other sales this month. `monthProfit` is -47, and the Profit-this-month widget renders "$-47" in terracotta with "from 1 flip". At a larger loss — say -1500 — it reads "$-1.5K". VoiceOver announces "dollar minus forty-seven profit this month" (MoreWidgets.swift:284).

**Fix.** Take the sign out before prefixing the symbol, in both copies of the shared block:
```swift
static func compactMoney(_ value: Double) -> String {
    let dollars = value.rounded()
    let sign = dollars < 0 ? "-" : ""
    let magnitude = abs(dollars)
    guard magnitude >= 1_000 else { return "\(sign)$\(Int(magnitude))" }
    let thousands = (magnitude / 100).rounded() / 10
    guard thousands >= 10 else { return "\(sign)$\(String(format: "%.1f", thousands))K" }
    return "\(sign)$\(Int(thousands.rounded()))K"
}
```
This also lets the two `abs(...)` guards drop out, and keeps the post-rounding boundary behaviour the comment at lines 156-162 documents (999.6 → "$1.0K", 9,999 and 10,000 → "$10K").

### **[FIXED]** compactMoney rounds half-away-from-zero while money() rounds half-even, so the same total renders one dollar apart

`ios/SnapWorthWidgets/SnapWorthWidgets.swift:169`

`compactMoney` rounds with `Double.rounded()` (line 169), which is `.toNearestOrAwayFromZero`. Every other money path rounds through a `NumberFormatter` with `maximumFractionDigits = 0` — `WidgetHaulData.money` (lines 82-96) and the app's `NumberFormatter.snapCurrency` (ios/SnapWorth/DesignSystem/DesignSystem.swift:249-256) — whose `roundingMode` is left at its documented default of `.halfEven`. For any amount ending in exactly 50 cents the two disagree by a dollar: 126.50 formats as "$126" through the formatter and "$127" through `compactMoney`. This is the same "one number, two spellings, one dollar apart" failure the function's own doc comment (lines 156-160) says it was written to prevent; the existing tests only exercise integers and a 617-step stride (SnapWorthTests.swift:946-976), so no half-dollar value is covered.

**What happens.** Two items priced $55-$X that the user graded Like New off a Good baseline contribute 55 x 1.15 = $63.25 each, so `totalLow` = 126.50. An accessoryRectangular Lock Screen widget renders `formattedRange` and shows "$126 - ..." (LockScreenWidgets.swift:97) while an accessoryInline widget placed above the clock renders `compactRange` and shows "$127-..." (LockScreenWidgets.swift:48) — same blob, same instant, same widget kind. Same root cause on the ledger: a Pro user whose month profit is exactly $22.50 (paid $10.00, sold $32.50, no fees) sees "$23" in MonthProfitWidget (MoreWidgets.swift:241) while the month share card renders the identical Decimal through `snapCurrency` as "$22" (ShareCardView.swift:336).

**Fix.** Make the compact path round the way the formatter does: `let dollars = value.rounded(.toNearestOrEven)` at line 169, and likewise `(magnitude / 100).rounded(.toNearestOrEven)` at line 174 and `thousands.rounded(.toNearestOrEven)` at line 178. Apply the identical edit to the app copy at ios/SnapWorth/Services/WidgetDataStore.swift:171-181 — CI diffs the shared block byte-for-byte — and extend LockScreenMoneyTests with half-dollar cases asserting `compactMoney(126.5) == money(126.5)`.

### The only single-number haul figure in the widgets is the top of the range, not the midpoint the app shows

`ios/SnapWorthWidgets/SnapWorthWidgets.swift:181`

`compactTotal` is defined as `compactMoney(totalHigh)` (line 181) and is the figure the circular Lock Screen complication draws unqualified (ios/SnapWorthWidgets/LockScreenWidgets.swift:67). The app's only single-number haul figure is the sum of condition-adjusted midpoints: `HistoryViewModel.portfolioTotal` sums `priceRange(for:).likely` (HistoryViewModel.swift:50-52) and `PortfolioBanner` prints it under "Your finds are worth" (HistoryView.swift:59, 344). The blob carries no midpoint total at all — only `totalLow`/`totalHigh` (lines 41-42) — so the widget has no way to show the number the app shows. The accessibility label at LockScreenWidgets.swift:78 says "Haul value up to ...", which is the correct qualifier; the visible text has none.

**What happens.** A library of three items each estimated $100-$200 gives totalLow $300, totalHigh $600, midpoint total $450. The app's portfolio banner reads "Your finds are worth $450"; the circular Lock Screen complication for the same library reads "$600" — 33% higher — with nothing on screen saying it is the top of a range. Every re-graded item widens the gap, since the low/high spread is what the condition multiplier scales.

**Fix.** Add a `totalLikely: Double?` field to the shared model, summed in `writeHaul` exactly as `lo`/`hi` are (`results.reduce(Decimal.zero) { $0 + $1.priceRange(for: $1.condition).likely }`, alongside WidgetDataStore.swift:388-393), default it in `init(from:)` like every other v2 field, and make `compactTotal` prefer it (falling back to `totalHigh` for a blob written by an older build). Mirror the block into ios/SnapWorthWidgets/SnapWorthWidgets.swift byte-for-byte.

### **[FIXED]** Display money strings are reused verbatim as VoiceOver labels, so the en-dash range is never spoken as a range

`ios/SnapWorthWidgets/SnapWorthWidgets.swift:80`

`formattedRange` returns `"\(money(totalLow))–\(money(totalHigh))"` (line 80) — two currency amounts joined by a bare U+2013 en dash with no surrounding whitespace — and `compactRange` does the same (line 190). Those display strings are then interpolated straight into accessibility labels rather than into a separate spoken form: SnapWorthWidgetsLiveActivity.swift:121 announces `"Thrift run, 8 finds, worth $348–$620"`, and at LockScreenWidgets.swift:113 and :55 the same strings are the only content of a combined/unlabeled element. A typographic dash between two amounts is not a spoken relationship at default punctuation verbosity, so the two amounts arrive adjacent with nothing joining them — and the unspaced form removes even the pause that whitespace would have produced. The author already knew the fix: LockScreenWidgets.swift:85 spells the relationship in words, `"Haul value up to \(haul.compactTotal)"`, rather than relying on punctuation.

**What happens.** VoiceOver user with a thrift run in progress, total $348 to $620. The Live Activity announces 'Thrift run, 8 finds, worth 348 dollars 620 dollars' — two amounts with no 'to', which is indistinguishable from a single malformed figure and gives no sense of the spread. Same on the rectangular and inline Lock Screen widgets, and in the `RecentFindsView` header (MoreWidgets.swift:71) and each row (`find.range`, MoreWidgets.swift:94, combined at :100).

**Fix.** Add a spoken sibling next to `formattedRange` in the shared model — `var spokenRange: String { hasScans ? "\(Self.money(totalLow)) to \(Self.money(totalHigh))" : "nothing yet" }`, plus the same for `compactRange` and for `ThriftRunAttributes.ContentState` (SnapWorthWidgets.swift:353) — and use it in every `accessibilityLabel` while leaving the en-dash form for display. Since the model is byte-for-byte mirrored in ios/SnapWorth/Services/WidgetDataStore.swift (see the header comment at SnapWorthWidgets.swift:5-20), make the same edit in both copies or the 'Widget model is in sync' CI step will fail.

### **[FIXED]** The thrift-run Live Activity never reads `context.isStale`, so an abandoned run looks live for hours

`ios/SnapWorthWidgets/SnapWorthWidgetsLiveActivity.swift:15`

`ActivityConfiguration(for:)` binds `context` at line 15 and both builders use only `context.state` and `context.attributes` — `context.isStale` is read nowhere, and `ThriftRunLockScreenView` (lines 65-117) has no stale state at all. The app does set one: `ThriftRunController.swift:47` and `:95` pass `staleDate: now + staleAfter` with `staleAfter = 90 * 60` (line 22). The comment there — "the system dims the Activity, signalling 'this may be out of date'" — is incorrect: `staleDate` only flips `context.isStale`, and rendering the stale state is the widget's job. Compounding it, line 100 renders `Text(startedAt, style: .timer)`, which the system keeps ticking unbounded, and the only thing that ends an over-long run is `ThriftRunController.update`'s `maximumRunDuration` check (ThriftRunController.swift:74-77), which requires the app to be running to fire.

**What happens.** A user starts a run, scans three items, pockets the phone and drives home. The app is never woken again, so no `update()` lands. Ninety minutes later `context.isStale` is true, yet the Lock Screen renders exactly the same live-looking "$95 – $150" / "3 finds this trip" with the elapsed timer now reading 1:31:07 and climbing past the 8-hour `maximumRunDuration` that was supposed to end the run. Nothing on screen tells the user the figure is last-known rather than current.

**Fix.** Read `context.isStale` in both builders. In `ThriftRunLockScreenView`, take it as a parameter: when stale, replace the ticking `Text(startedAt, style: .timer)` with a static "started 14:05", drop the money figure to `wWarmGray`, change the subtitle to "Last known — open SnapWorth to refresh", and extend the accessibility label to say so. In the Dynamic Island, render the compact trailing count in the muted colour when stale. Consider also passing a `staleDate` no later than `startedAt + maximumRunDuration` so the run is visibly stale by the time the app intends to end it.

## Low

### `dayKeyFormatter` pins the time zone for the process lifetime while every other date helper re-reads `Calendar.current`, so ledger keys and fire dates can disagree by a day

`ios/SnapWorth/Services/NotificationManager.swift:586`

Line 586 assigns `f.calendar = Calendar.current` inside a `static let`. `Calendar.current` is a non-autoupdating snapshot carrying a concrete `TimeZone`, and the static is initialized once per process — so the formatter's zone is fixed at first use. Every other helper in the same section (headed "Date helpers (timezone/calendar correct)", line 572) reads `Calendar.current` freshly per call: `ledgerFireDate` at 576-580, and `date(fromDayKey:)` itself at 595-597, which parses with the pinned formatter and then re-reads the components with a fresh calendar. Once the device zone changes without a relaunch, the key written by `dayKey` and the date recovered by `date(fromDayKey:)` no longer agree with the fire date `ledgerFireDate` computed.

**What happens.** Process first touches `dayKeyFormatter` on America/Los_Angeles; the user then flies to Asia/Tokyo and the app is resumed, not relaunched. An item listed Sep 11 gives `ledgerFireDate` = Sep 25 10:00 JST. `dayKey` formats that instant in the pinned LA zone (Sep 24 18:00 PDT) → key "20260924". `rescheduleLedgerDay("20260924")` → `date(fromDayKey:)` parses LA midnight Sep 24 = Sep 24 16:00 JST, whose components in Tokyo are Sep 24, and schedules the reminder for Sep 24 10:00 JST — a day earlier than the 14-day follow-up it is supposed to be. Two items whose fire dates fall on adjacent days can also land in the wrong bucket, coalescing or splitting reminders incorrectly.

**Fix.** Drop the stored formatter for key generation and derive the key from the same calendar the fire date was computed with: `let c = Calendar.current.dateComponents([.year,.month,.day], from: date)` formatted as `String(format: "%04d%02d%02d", ...)`, and rebuild the date by feeding those integers back into `Calendar.current` with hour 10. If the formatter is kept, set `f.calendar = Calendar.autoupdatingCurrent` (and `f.timeZone = TimeZone.autoupdatingCurrent`) so it tracks the device.

### An unviewed recap is silently destroyed by the next month's third scan — the one case the fallback state exists for

`ios/SnapWorth/Services/NotificationManager.swift:167`

`storeRecapPending` overwrites `notif_recap_fire` (line 167) and `notif_recap_label` (line 168) unconditionally on every recap scheduling, and resets `notif_recap_viewed` whenever the label differs (lines 164-166). `readyRecapLabel()` (lines 172-178) can only ever surface the single current label, and only once `Date() >= fire`. So scheduling next month's recap erases a recap that is already due and not yet viewed, pushing `fire` a month into the future. The comment at line 154 says this state exists "so the in-app banner works even if denied" — i.e. for users who will never see the notification, which is exactly the population that loses the recap here.

**What happens.** Notifications denied (or the priming alert was declined). User scans 3+ times in August → label "August", fire Sep 1 10:00. On Sep 1 the History banner is due but they have not opened History yet. They scan (three times over Sep 1-3 on the free tier, or three times on Sep 1 as Pro) → `scheduleMonthlyRecap` calls `storeRecapPending(fireDate: Oct 1 10:00, label: "September")`, `notif_recap_viewed` is reset and `fire` becomes Oct 1, so `readyRecapLabel()` returns nil. The August recap banner is never shown to anyone and there is no other surface for it.

**Fix.** In `storeRecapPending`, do not overwrite while a recap is already due and unviewed — e.g. `guard readyRecapLabel() == nil || label == d.string(forKey: "notif_recap_label") else { return }` — or store the pending recaps as a month-keyed set so a due-but-unviewed month survives until `markRecapViewed()` clears it.

### scheduleWidgetSync's "debounce" never cancels the prior task, so N mutations run N full syncs

`ios/SnapWorth/Services/ScanRepository.swift:124`

`scheduleWidgetSync()` creates a fresh, unretained `Task { @MainActor in ... }` on every call and sleeps 600 ms inside it (lines 124-133). Nothing holds a reference and nothing cancels a previous one, so this is not a debounce — it is N independent tasks, each of which then performs a full `context.fetch(FetchDescriptor<ScanResult>())` of the entire library on the MainActor (:126), a full `WidgetDataStore.writeHaul` (which reduces `priceRange(for:)` over every row twice, plus the month-to-date ledger pass — WidgetDataStore.swift:616-651), a `WidgetCenter.shared.reloadAllTimelines()` (WidgetDataStore.swift:683), and a `ThriftRunController.update(results:)` (:132). The doc comment at :101-111 describes the intent as deferring one sync off the presentation path; the implementation multiplies it instead. WidgetKit meters timeline reloads per widget per day, so redundant `reloadAllTimelines()` calls spend a budget a later genuine post-scan refresh needs.

**What happens.** On the result sheet, tap through the condition chips — each tap's action calls `ScanRepository(context: modelContext).refreshWidget()` (ResultView.swift:292), which goes straight to `scheduleWidgetSync`. Tapping four of the five chips while deciding on a grade queues four tasks that all fire within ~600 ms of each other, producing four whole-library MainActor fetches, four `writeHaul` passes over every saved find, four `reloadAllTimelines()` calls and four Live Activity updates, where one was intended. On a large library that is four O(history) passes on the main thread during an interactive sheet.

**Fix.** Store the task and cancel the previous one: add `private var widgetSyncTask: Task<Void, Never>?` to `ScanRepository` and write `widgetSyncTask?.cancel(); widgetSyncTask = Task { @MainActor in try? await Task.sleep(for: .milliseconds(600)); guard !Task.isCancelled else { return }; ... }`. Note the repository is constructed per call site as a local (`ScanRepository(context: modelContext)` at ResultView.swift:292, ScanView.swift:385), so the debounce state has to live somewhere that outlives it — make the task a `static` on `ScanRepository` keyed by nothing (there is one store), or move the coalescing into `WidgetDataStore` itself.

### Concurrent product loads can leave `pricingFailed` stuck true, showing a "couldn't load plans" warning above fully loaded prices

`ios/SnapWorth/Services/StoreKitPurchaseService.swift:145`

`loadProducts()` has no in-flight deduplication and no generation stamp: each invocation suspends at `await Product.products(for:)` (line 132) and then unconditionally writes `pricingFailed` on resume — `true` on the failure path (line 134), `fetched.count < productIDs.count` on the success path (line 145). Two invocations overlap by construction: `init()` starts one at line 46, and PaywallView's `.task` starts another via `reloadProducts()` at PaywallView.swift:228-229 whenever `!isPricingLoaded || pricing.isEmpty` — which is exactly the window in which the init fetch has not finished. Both are on the main actor, so they interleave cleanly at the shared await, and last-writer-wins on the flag regardless of which result is better.

**What happens.** Cold launch on flaky cellular: the paywall is opened while `init()`'s `Product.products` call is still in flight, so a second fetch starts. The first resumes with both products (pricing populated, `pricingFailed = false`); the second then resumes having failed, sets `pricingFailed = true`, and returns early without touching `pricing`. The paywall now shows correct, purchasable $39.99 and $4.99 cards with an enabled CTA — and, directly above them, "Couldn't load every plan. Try again, or continue with the one shown." (PaywallView.swift:122-128) plus a retry button, for a screen where nothing failed. The mirror case is worse in principle: a late success clears a genuine failure flag while the cards still read "—".

**Fix.** Serialise the load: keep `private var loadTask: Task<Void, Never>?` and have `loadProducts()` return `await loadTask?.value` if one is in flight, otherwise create, store, await, and nil it out — so the paywall's retry joins the init fetch instead of racing it. A generation counter checked after the await before writing `pricingFailed` also works.

### A subscriber who records sales without a cost basis is told "No flips sold yet this month" by the widget while the app says N items sold

`ios/SnapWorth/Services/WidgetDataStore.swift:440`

Line 440 uses `!monthProfits.isEmpty` as the test for "anything sold this month", but `monthProfits` (lines 415-419) collects only `result.realizedProfit`, which is nil whenever `paidPrice` is missing (ScanResult.swift:296-299: "Nil unless the item is sold **and** a paid price is known"). So a month full of sales with no cost basis is indistinguishable from a month with no sales, and `MonthProfitView` renders the Pro nil-branch as the factual claim "No flips sold yet this month" (MoreWidgets.swift:251, :259) over a "—" (:240). The app counts the same month differently: `Summary.itemsSold = scopedSold.count` (FlipsViewModel.swift:91) counts every sold-in-month item regardless of cost basis, so the two surfaces disagree about whether the month had sales at all.

**What happens.** A Pro user marks 3 items sold in September, entering sale prices but not what they paid (a common flow — the sold sheet is the one that asks). The Flips tab shows "Profit this month $0 · 3 items sold"; the Home Screen widget shows "—" captioned "No flips sold yet this month", denying three sales the payer recorded. Same shape when only some items have a basis: the app says "5 items sold" while the widget says "from 2 flips".

**Fix.** Carry the sold-in-month count separately from the profit-contributing count — add a `monthSold` field written as the count of items with `status == .sold` and a `soldDate` in the month (independent of `realizedProfit`) — and choose the empty caption from it: "3 sold · add what you paid" when `monthSold > 0` and `monthProfit == nil`, reserving "No flips sold yet this month" for `monthSold == 0`. Keep `monthFlips` as the profit-contributing count so "$214 from 6 flips" stays true of the same six.
